## 📚 Paper List

- **[Beyond Success Rate: Interventional Evaluation of Memory in Vision-Language-Action Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23372)**  `arXiv:2610.23372`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Keyuan Luo, Yi Xie, Wanyun Ling, Yuanzhe Li, Ziyue Li, Zhuoqi Zeng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When we say that a robot uses memory, does that memory actually play a role? Success-rate ablations alone cannot establish the use of task-relevant facts: they change information and computation together, while aggregate rates can hide offsetting gains and losses. We propose an Interventional Memory Evaluation Protocol (IMEP) for vision-language-action (VLA) systems to address four questions: (1) Does memory provide a net benefit? (2) Under what conditions does memory help? (3) Do changes to memory alter task outcomes? (4) Are these effects specific to task-relevant facts? At the retrieval interface, we compare target-fact substitutions with fact-preserving replacements, minimal numerical perturbations, and exact replicas, while assessing decision correctness separately from motor execution success. We evaluate within-episode, cross-episode, and agentic memory. In cross-episode learned-bank experiments, a one-unit-in-the-last-place (ULP) intervention, including its installation procedure, changes 11.9% of seed-paired outcomes, while changing key facts produces no clear additional effect over fact-preserving replacements. To examine when memory helps, we introduce ChainBench, where correct decisions require historical information unavailable in current observations. The full memory configuration raises task success from 39.1% to 74.3%, matching the oracle-decision condition under shared recorded executions. These results show that correct history can support robot decisions, but establishing whether a memory system realizes this benefit requires fact-specific evidence beyond aggregate success rates.
  </details>

- **[NestedVLA: Learning to Consolidate and Generate Skills for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23271)**  `arXiv:2610.23271`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Guodong Du, Wanyu Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision--Language--Action (VLA) models promise generalist robotic agents, yet consolidating skills learned across heterogeneous tasks and environments remains difficult: training data is fragmented across platforms, joint multi-task training suffers from interference, and model merging tends to introduce parameter conflicts or require architectural changes. We instead advocate a paradigm in which skills are first learned independently and then consolidated post hoc. To this end, we propose NestedVLA, which replaces parameter aggregation with parameter generation: a nested hypernetwork, conditioned on the target task, synthesizes task-adaptive action-expert parameters that compose with a frozen base VLA. Task-specific compressed VLM residuals are retained separately. This formulation enables flexible task-conditioned skill reuse while mitigating interference. Across three RL benchmarks (MetaWorld, ManiSkill, CALVIN), NestedVLA outperforms the strongest model-merging baseline by 41 pp on average, matches per-task fine-tuning within 1 pp on ManiSkill, and substantially closes the gap on CALVIN, pointing toward a storage-efficient route to skill consolidation. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Embodiment-Classifier Guidance: Transferring Frozen VLAs Across Robot Dynamics without Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22576)**  `arXiv:2610.22576`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Devin Han, Hershraj Niranjani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow-based policies such as $\pi_0$ are trained on a fixed set of embodiments, and transferring them to new ones is an open problem. To adapt a frozen $\pi_0$ to a new embodiment without additional demonstrations or parameter updates, we train a lightweight embodiment classifier to distinguish successful action chunks on the source body from those on the target body, using a few calibration tasks. On transfer tasks that $\pi_0$ saw on its source body but the classifier was never trained on, we nudge each denoising step of $\pi_0$ with the classifier's gradient, which estimates the direction from the distribution of successful actions on the source body toward that on the target body. On the LIBERO simulation benchmark with four modified controllers, embodiment-classifier guidance raises $\pi_0$'s average success on the target bodies from 72% to 80% across 12 suite-controller pairs, with a 31% relative reduction in failures (11 of 12 gain; 5 significant after Holm correction). In simulation, guidance is data-efficient, needing few target-body rollouts per task, which makes it promising for sim-to-real transfer, where rollouts are costly. Guidance is mixed on weak wrists and harmful on a locked wrist, when the classifier lacks data or favors infeasible motions. Collecting more target-body data with guided $\pi_0$ can fill such gaps: on one weak-wrist arm, guided $\pi_0$ solved a task on which unguided $\pi_0$ had zero successes in the original data, and retraining the classifier on the guided successes raised success from 52.5% to 70.7%.
  </details>

- **[Expand-and-Prune: Test-Time Candidate-Space Adaptation for End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23262)**  `arXiv:2610.23262`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Fabian Schmidt, Karol Fedurko, Markus Enzweiler, Abhinav Valada_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Candidate-based end-to-end driving planners reason over a finite set of trajectory hypotheses. However, for sparse planners, a limited candidate set may provide insufficient support for scene-relevant maneuvers, while fixed downstream candidate budgets do not account for prediction uncertainty. To address these challenges, we introduce Expand-and-Prune, a test-time framework that jointly adapts candidate support and capacity without updating learned parameters. The framework expands the native candidate set using semantic driving intent and temporally propagated predictions and then uses uncertainty in intermediate trajectory scores to adaptively prune the resulting candidates. Across four heterogeneous candidate-based planners and four benchmarks, Expand-and-Prune consistently improves planning performance. It achieves state-of-the-art results on LangAuto, increasing the Driving Score from 68.2 to 75.9, while providing further improvement on Bench2Drive, Fail2Drive, and NAVSIMv2, with particularly strong improvements in out-of-distribution scenarios. Moreover, in its full instantiation, 72.5% of selected trajectories originate from the newly introduced intent-guided or temporal candidates, confirming that candidate-space expansion provides useful hypotheses beyond those generated natively by the planner. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MambaAct: Efficient Mamba Action Experts for Flow-Matching Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22951)**  `arXiv:2610.22951`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Khuyen Pham, Tran Nguyen Le, Edoardo Fazzari, Cesare Stefanini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models have shown strong promise for generalist robotic manipulation, but their inference cost remains a major bottleneck for real-time control. This limitation is especially pronounced in flow-matching VLAs such as $\pi_{0.5}$, where the action expert is evaluated repeatedly during iterative denoising. We introduce MambaAct, an efficient Mamba action expert for flow-matching VLA policies. MambaAct retains the pretrained visual-language prefix encoder of $\pi_{0.5}$, while replacing its transformer-based action expert with a compact Mamba-based action expert. Within this expert, Mamba serves as the action mixer that models dependencies across the noisy continuous-action sequence. To preserve visual-language conditioning without repeated token-level cross-attention, MambaAct uses a lightweight Context Compressor to map Vision-language model (VLM) prefix tokens into a compact conditioning signal that gates and modulates each Mamba action block. MambaAct uses a $10\times$ smaller action expert and reduces flow-matching denoising latency by $64.8\%$ on LIBERO and $58.8\%$ in real-world deployment, while improving mean LIBERO success by $1.08$ percentage points across three independent fine-tuning seeds and maintaining gains on LIBERO-Plus and real-world benchmarks. Our code and model weights are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[WorldOracle: Benchmarking Action-Conditioned World Models against Real-World Executions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18826)**  `arXiv:2610.18826`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Haoyi Duan, Liliang Chen, Nan Wang, Yang Zhang, Yuhang Lu, Xiaotong Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce WorldOracle, a benchmark specifically designed to evaluate action-conditioned world models (ACWMs) against paired real-world executions. Given the same initial observation and low-level action sequence, WorldOracle asks whether a predicted rollout reproduces what happens in the real world, including physical consequences and task-state transitions. We evaluate models along four complementary dimensions: physical state correctness, interaction spatial alignment, key interaction area fidelity, and multi-view action coherence. To support this evaluation, we curate real-world training and evaluation suites that cover different interaction dynamics and embodiments. The evaluation suite contains long, multi-segment episodes with both successful and failed executions, enabling evaluation across varied action outcomes and behavior distributions. Experiments on seven representative ACWMs reveal distinct model strengths and failure modes. WorldOracle provides a systematic foundation for measuring and advancing action-conditioned world modeling for manipulation. We release the datasets, evaluation code, and leaderboard:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[AWAM: Active Trajectory-Conditioned View Imagination for Robust Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23396)**  `arXiv:2610.23396`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiawei Geng, Zhenyang Liu, Yuzheng Wu, Yanwei Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rapid progress in embodied intelligence has enabled increasingly capable robots to follow language instructions and manipulate diverse objects. However, strong results in structured environments do not automatically transfer to open-world conditions, where clutter, occlusion, and changing viewpoints can obscure the spatial relationships required for action. To address this perceptual gap, a virtual active-perception paradigm is introduced. AWAM, an Active World-Action Model actively plans where to observe and imagines what will be seen along the planned trajectory before acting. Task-related virtual camera motion is predicted and used as a structured query into a geometry-conditioned world model, producing a reusable spatial prior for manipulation, which is later compressed into a fixed memory and fused with live observations through cross-attention for diffusion-based action prediction. AWAM achieves competitive success rates with 75.9% on LIBERO-Occ, 62.4% on LIBERO-Plus, 76.5% on RLBench-OG Occlusion1, and 94.4% in real-robot trials. These results support improved robustness to the evaluated occlusions and distribution shifts, addressing part of the gap between structured manipulation and less controlled environments.
  </details>

- **[MANGO-Grasp: Mahalanobis Fields over Geometry-Oriented 3D Gaussians for Cross-Embodiment Dexterous Grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02014)**  `arXiv:2608.02014`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Heng Zhang, Kevin Yuchen Ma, Mike Zheng Shou, Weisi Lin, Yan Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-embodiment dexterous grasping aims to synthesize stable grasps across heterogeneous multi-fingered hands with little or no embodiment-specific tuning. Existing interaction-centric methods achieve promising results, but their object representations often underrepresent local surface geometry, hand descriptors do not explicitly capture both morphology and kinematics, and hand-object interaction modeling is typically isotropic. We propose MANGO-Grasp, an anisotropic interaction framework that represents objects as geometry-oriented 3D Gaussian primitives to better preserve local surface geometry, and encodes hands into pretrained descriptors that capture both morphology and kinematics. Hand-object interaction is modeled using anisotropic Mahalanobis fields that reflect the directional nature of contact and guide a shared optimization for grasp realization across embodiments. On the CMAP and MultiGripperGrasp benchmarks, MANGO-Grasp outperforms the strongest seen-hand baseline by up to 8.24 percentage points in simulation. It also transfers zero-shot to the unseen SharpaWave hand, improving over the strongest zero-shot baseline by up to 16.57 percentage points, and achieves 86% success in real-world experiments. The code and additional materials will be made available upon publication atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[No Eyes, No Problem: What Does Touch Contribute to Blind Baoding Ball Manipulation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23438)**  `arXiv:2610.23438`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ayush Deshmukh, Nalin Agarwal, Elle Miller, Sethu Vijayakumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans manipulate objects within their hands through touch alone with remarkable ease, but for robots, achieving sustained real-world manipulation without vision remains an open challenge. Although reinforcement learning (RL) can generate sophisticated blind manipulation policies in simulation, transferring them to a real dexterous hand remains hindered by continuously changing contacts, motion-induced tactile information drift, and the gap between simulated and physical dynamics. We study this problem through Baoding ball rotation on the Shadow Dexterous Hand Lite, using only proprioception and binary tactile feedback as observations. We achieve successful zero-shot transfer through a combination of a sensor-aligned, coupling-aware embodiment in simulation, tactile calibration, and two novel episodic domain randomisations: slew-rate action limits and STuck-AT Taxels. On the physical hand, the policy achieves sustained blind Baoding rotation, completing 225 consecutive $\mathbf{180^\circ}$ rotations (112.5 full rotations) at $\sim0.36 \;\text{rot/s}$ in a single continuous trial without any vision or object-state information. Furthermore, we evaluate the contribution of tactile sensing relative to proprioception alone and identify the hand regions where tactile information is most important. This is the strongest reported hardware result for Baoding ball manipulation in both rotation count and speed, irrespective of the sensing modality used. By effectively bridging the sim-to-real gap, our framework sets a new benchmark for sustained tactile-driven dexterous manipulation on physical hardware.
  </details>

- **[SkyAgent: Revisable Target Memory for Continuous Aerial Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23293)**  `arXiv:2610.23293`  `cs.RO`  
  _Kaibo Yang, Xingyu Wu, Enze Zhu, Yajie Chai, Xuyang Li, Jiahui Cui, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous aerial search builds target hypotheses from sparse, viewpoint-dependent observations. When later evidence contradicts a stored hypothesis, however, the candidate can remain available to the planner and continue triggering approach or verification actions. We call this trajectory-level failure false candidate persistence (FCP). We equip SkyAgent with Revisable Target Memory (RTM), which associates evidence with persistent candidates, discounts correlated views, and revises each candidate through Tentative, Confirmed, and Rejected states. The key interface binds every generated waypoint to its source candidate: rejection therefore invalidates stale goals, while conservative reactivation protects temporarily occluded targets. Across four splits from the CityAVOS, UAV-ON, and OpenUAV benchmarks, our SkyAgent equipped with RTM outperforms all competitors, improving SR by 2.90--6.31 points while substantially reducing Behavior-FCP and post-contradiction travel with a low false-rejection rate. Across 35 matched real-world DJI scenarios per method, RTM consistently improves task success and behavioral reliability, further validating its state-to-action correction under physical deployment conditions.
  </details>

- **[SenseSwarm: Swarm Exploration via Neural Structure Estimation with Shared World-Anticipation for Rapid Mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22905)**  `arXiv:2610.22905`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haohua Que, Haojia Gao, Mingkai Liu, Hoiian Au, Qian Zhang, Jiayue Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A robot swarm covers a building faster with more robots, but the map is only as complete as the space the robots have physically visited. We target the amount of an unknown building a decentralized swarm knows at each instant, ahead of physical coverage. SenseSwarm serves a lightweight learned map predictor on the swarm's shared occupancy map and folds the predicted structure back into a stigmergic guidance field. Its 709K-parameter predictor is fine-tuned on the swarm's own multi-front observations, which we show is necessary: a single-robot predictor collapses on such views. We build a benchmark of twelve real building floors from HouseExpo, HKUST, and KTH, up to 24,000 square meters, and run 1,620 simulations across nine coordination methods, swarm sizes 32 to 512, and three seeds. On physical coverage the swarm is near parity with a greedy frontier planner, as expected. On map reconstruction, every non-predictive baseline needs 1.3 to 1.9 times longer to reach an 80 percent known map, and SenseSwarm holds an equal-time area lead of about 670 square meters, up to 1,359. Folding the prediction into the shared field matters: the same predictor applied per robot matches our speed but suffers a 6.9 times higher inter-robot contact rate at 512 robots. The prediction is computed once per shared map, so its cost stayed flat from 32 to 512 robots.
  </details>

- **[Structured Perception Representations for Off-road Dynamics Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22900)**  `arXiv:2610.22900`  `cs.RO`  
  _Micah Nye, Samuel Triest, Matthew Sivaprakasam, Isaiah Adu, Sebastian Scherer, Wenshan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomously navigating off-road terrain at high speed demands accurate control, which often relies on dynamics models that capture complex interactions between the robot and terrain. In off-road regimes, terrain interaction is a major component of the vehicle's dynamics. Prior work attempts to capture this effect by training learned models of the vehicle's dynamics, conditioned on its perception of the local environment. However, said conditioning is often overly simplistic, ignoring the spatial structure of the dynamics problem. Our approach, SpatialBEV, exploits this spatial structure. At each step, we query the bird's-eye-view (BEV) features around the predicted state, conditioning the next prediction on current geometric and semantic cues. We additionally present a benchmarking approach that mines a dataset's most difficult scenarios. On datasets collected across seasons, SpatialBEV reduces prediction error by 17.1%, compared to physics-based and learned baselines. We further evaluate on hardware on high-speed ($\geq$10 m/s) and geometrically challenging courses with a 25.3% reduction in maximum cross-track error. More details can be found atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PASS-VLN: Passability-Aware Local-Command Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22812)**  `arXiv:2610.22812`  `cs.RO`  
  _Cong Zou, Xiangsheng Shi, Xuanhui Wang, Fubin Wang, Guangjun Ge, Chao Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable vision-and-language navigation (VLN) requires not only understanding instructions but also determining whether and how a robot can physically execute them. However, existing VLN benchmarks typically assume executable instructions and focus on long-horizon tasks. This leaves body-dependent local execution, including passability and collision-free execution, largely unexamined. Evaluating these factors within long-horizon tasks would entangle local physical execution with task planning and long-term spatial memory, making navigation failures difficult to attribute. We therefore focus on grounding local navigation commands in executable motion under environmental and robot body constraints. We present PASS-VLN, a passability-aware local-command VLN benchmark and model. The benchmark holds the scene, instruction, start pose, and reference route fixed while varying robot body width, requiring target completion when the instructed passage is feasible and stopping before blockage otherwise. Our model encodes body width through obstacle inflation in a depth-derived local bird's-eye view, fuses geometry with visual and linguistic context, and predicts discrete latent action codes for continuous closed-loop execution. Experiments show that PASS-VLN improves collision-free success by approximately 12 percentage points over a strong VLN model fine-tuned on our benchmark, while adapting reach-versus-stop behavior to body constraints and generalizing to unseen body widths. When integrated with a high-level planner, PASS-VLN achieves higher success with fewer local commands, while real-world deployment further demonstrates its effectiveness as a low-level executor. The benchmark and model will be released.
  </details>

- **[Can a Frontier VLM Serve as a Robot Policy for Memory-Dependent Manipulation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17350)**  `arXiv:2610.17350`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jack B. Jedlicki, Amish Sethi, Heng Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We use a frozen frontier vision-language model, GPT-6-Astra, directly as the robot policy on RoboMME, a benchmark of 16 memory-dependent manipulation tasks spanning temporal, spatial, object, and procedural memory. The model keeps the whole episode in context and outputs every end-effector waypoint and gripper command, with no RoboMME-specific training. On the 800 test episodes it succeeds in 92.1%, above the previously reported human score of 90.5%, which was obtained with an oracle skill interface, and far ahead of the best leaderboard entry (60.8%) and of a hierarchical system that pairs the same model with a RoboMME-trained policy (79.1%). It matches or exceeds the human score on 13 of the 16 tasks. Its 63 failures concentrate in two tasks and are mostly premature completions and mistimed or unintended button presses. These results blur the usual division between long-horizon planning, memory and end-effector control, and raise the question of how much of this stack a single general-purpose model can take over in embodied systems. Project website:this https URL. Code, prompts and all episode logs:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[DexBioLab: Benchmarking Dexterous Manipulation with Dynamic Experimental Phenomena in Realistic Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13643)**  `arXiv:2610.13643`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xuanye Wu, Yue Shi, Yongcan Yang, Yiqun Wang, Zhaoqi Wan, Dayu Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automating biological laboratories requires robots to perform dexterous tool interactions under realistic visual conditions while handling dynamic experimental phenomena, presenting significant challenges for robotic manipulation. To enable systematic evaluation under these conditions, we introduce DexBioLab, a simulation benchmark and framework for dexterous manipulation in realistic biological laboratory environments. DexBioLab comprises 14 tasks across 4 robot embodiments, spanning functional tool use, precision manipulation, language-conditioned visual grounding, and instrument operation. We introduce controllable dynamic experimental phenomena, including flames, vapor, and bubbles, to capture the evolving visual conditions of laboratory procedures. To further enhance environmental realism, we develop a hybrid rendering pipeline that integrates interactive simulated foreground objects with Gaussian-splatting backgrounds reconstructed from 6 real laboratory scenes. For efficient large-scale data generation and evaluation, we further build a parallel simulation framework on top of mjlab, proposing Parallel Finite-State Machine (PFSM) and integrating batched rendering with decoupled trajectory replay for demonstration generation and model-independent closed-loop policy evaluation. Our evaluation reveals substantial variation in policy performance across tasks, exposing distinct strengths and weaknesses of current manipulation policies. Real-world experiments further demonstrate a reduced sim-to-real gap, supporting the validity of AutoBio+ for realistic manipulation evaluation. Supplementary materials are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Monkey See, Can Monkey Do? A Benchmark for Evaluating Robot Skill Learning by Observation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08209)**  `arXiv:2609.08209`  `cs.RO`  
  _Weiwei Gu, Anmol Gupta, Anant Sah, Ryan Varghese, Lalitha Shreya Vanam, Prabhath Adireddi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning from Observation (LfO) is a fundamental robotic capability that replicates how humans and animals socially learn from each other. Beyond its biological parallels, this modality provides a practical solution for data scaling in sample-inefficient and data-starved domains like robotics. Recent work has demonstrated promising results in learning manipulation skills from human videos, yet progress in this area remains difficult to assess. Existing methods vary widely in assumptions, hardware choices, and environment setups making it difficult to draw meaningful comparisons and identify advances in the field. To address these challenges, we introduce RoboReel: a unified benchmark for evaluating models that learn policies from human videos. RoboReel consists of bundled real-world human demonstration videos, simulated robot trajectories, and evaluation environments on ten manipulation tasks. We develop four test suites to evaluate the models' performance on multiple axes, including the robustness to visual distractors and the ability to complete long-horizon tasks. We further validate RoboReel by recreating a subset of its tasks in the real world, showing that relative performance trends observed in simulation are preserved in the real world. Our benchmark covers learning-from-observation models from different categories, and studies the effectiveness of multiple representation choices in our benchmark evaluation that covers over seven state-of-the-art algorithms (including our VLA based variants) in the field of LfO. Finally, we present an analysis of the different types of algorithms showing that long-horizon tasks and tasks with low tolerances are still challenging for current models. Webpage:this https URL
  </details>

- **[When Does Corruption Break the Plan? A Degradation-Tolerance Benchmark for Camera-Only End-to-End Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29005)**  `arXiv:2608.29005`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haohua Que, Handong Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Camera-only end-to-end (E2E) driving models are nearing deployment, where the camera stream is degraded by blur, noise, low light, weather, frame loss, and memory faults. How much a policy tolerates before its driving breaks is unclear. Corruption-robustness benchmarks target detection or bird's-eye-view perception, not the planning output that drives the car. We present DriveDegrade, a benchmark for image-degradation tolerance in camera-only E2E driving. Sixteen corruption families at five severities are injected on the fly inside the image loader, one operator reaching fifteen policies, and we evaluate open-loop planning on nuScenes and NAVSIM plus a CARLA closed-loop anchor. First, mild degradation barely affects planning, and the families that break it have a clear threshold at mid severity. Second, fragility is corruption-dependent: blur, JPEG, and raindrop damage planning most, while weather and bit error are tolerated far into the range. Third, a flat curve is ambiguous, so we separate corruptions that degrade the image from those that remove it. A planner that reads its camera must lose accuracy when information is deleted, whatever it does under quality loss. On these two axes the planners separate sharply, quantifying the ego-status shortcut without mistaking indifference for robustness. A released vision-language-action planner is flat on both axes, and blinding all six of its cameras costs it only 11.5 percent.
  </details>

- **[EDAR: Learning Environment-Dependent Action Representations for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11427)**  `arXiv:2607.11427`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yuecheng Xu, Tong Yang, Jingkai Jia, Chi Zhang, Xuelong Li, Wenqiang Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning effective action representations is critical for robotic manipulation, where raw control trajectories are often noisy, redundant, and difficult to model directly. Existing methods mainly encode the structure of the action stream itself, treating the role of actions in the environment as implicit. Yet manipulation is about changing the world: the same action segment can induce different outcomes under different scene contexts, making action semantics inherently environment-dependent. We propose EDAR, an Environment-Dependent Action Representation that grounds action tokens in both executable control structure and expected visual consequences. By coupling motor commands with their environment-conditioned effects, EDAR encourages the learned action space to capture interaction semantics rather than merely command-level patterns. Experiments on simulated and real-robot manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that EDAR improves downstream policy learning, especially in long-horizon manipulation. These results highlight the importance of grounding action representations in executable control structure and environment-conditioned visual change.
  </details>

- **[WireCraft: A Simulation Benchmark for Industrial DLO Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18097)**  `arXiv:2606.18097`  `cs.RO`  
  _Chongyu Zhu, Ramy ElMallah, Hyegang Kim, Zachary Tang, Jiachen Rao, Artem Arutyunov, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deformable Linear Objects (DLOs), such as wires and cables, are central to industrial assembly. Unlike rigid objects, whose state is captured by a 6-DoF pose, DLOs have an infinite-dimensional configuration space and deform continuously under contact with grippers, fixtures, and the workspace, making them a demanding benchmark for general dexterous manipulation. Despite their importance, policy development and comparison remain difficult: existing benchmarks are often tied to specific hardware setups, lack modular and customizable task assets, or study generic deformable-object tasks without the fixtures relevant to real-world industrial wire manipulation. Few benchmarks align simulation, real-world data, and shared evaluation protocols. To bridge this gap, we introduce WireCraft, a simulation benchmark for industrial DLO manipulation with configurable difficulty and assets, spanning three task families: connector insertion, clip routing, and channel seating. It supports two complementary DLO physics models, articulated and deformable, and the released trajectories come from both simulation and a physical UR5. We benchmark reinforcement learning (RL), imitation learning (IL), and vision-language-action (VLA) policies under shared metrics. Privileged state-based RL solves a representative setting in each task family with over 82% success, confirming the tasks are well-posed. Image-based policies remain less successful across the evaluated tasks, with ACT and DiT VLA falling below privileged State PPO. On a physical UR5, supplementing real demonstrations with simulated data improves success by 15-65 percentage points in the best-performing mixture for each task. These results indicate that industrial DLO manipulation, though tractable under privileged state, remains an open challenge for current vision-based learning.
  </details>

- **[SidewalkBench: Benchmarking Visual Navigation on Urban Sidewalks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16953)**  `arXiv:2606.16953`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhizheng Liu, Honglin He, Vivek Alumootil, Akshat Pandya, Brad Squicciarini, Wayne Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Urban sidewalk navigation presents significant challenges due to complex structural layouts, dynamic pedestrian behaviors, and long distances. While recent visual navigation models offer a promising solution, the lack of a unified benchmark hinders quantitative and reproducible evaluation. To bridge this gap, we propose SidewalkBench, a comprehensive benchmark designed for visual navigation on urban sidewalks. Built upon NVIDIA Isaac Sim, SidewalkBench brings GPU-accelerated simulation of diverse, high-fidelity sidewalk environments, including both procedurally generated and real-world scanned scenes. We further populate the scenes with rich, reactive event-based pedestrian behaviors and flexible, efficient animation, enabling standardized model evaluation under realistic real-world settings. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 9 visual navigation models on 330 unit-test scenarios, 800 pedestrian-reactive scenarios, and 105 long-horizon scenarios. Our findings highlight that pedestrian interaction and long-horizon robustness remain critical bottlenecks for existing models, and scaling up sidewalk training with synthetic data emerges as a promising solution.
  </details>

- **[PanoAir: A Panoramic Visual-Inertial SLAM with Cross-Time Real-World UAV Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.00852)**  `arXiv:2604.00852`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yiyang Wu, Xiaohu Zhang, Yanjin Du, Tongsu Zhang, Chujun Li, Siyang Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate pose estimation is fundamental for unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) applications, where Visual-Inertial SLAM (VI-SLAM) provides a cost-effective solution for localization and mapping. However, existing VI-SLAM methods mainly rely on sensors with limited fields of view (FoV), which can lead to drift and even failure in complex UAV scenarios. Although panoramic cameras provide omnidirectional perception to improve robustness, panoramic VI-SLAM and corresponding real-world datasets for UAVs remain underexplored. To address this limitation, we first construct a real-world panoramic visual-inertial dataset covering diverse flight conditions, including varying illumination, altitudes, trajectory lengths, and motion dynamics. To achieve accurate and robust pose estimation under such challenging UAV scenarios, we propose a panoramic VI-SLAM framework that exploits the omnidirectional FoV via the proposed panoramic feature extraction and panoramic loop closure, enhancing feature constraints and ensuring global consistency. Extensive experiments on both the proposed dataset and public benchmarks demonstrate that our method achieves superior accuracy, robustness, and consistency, completing all 17 sequences without tracking failure and reducing the ATE by up to 47.1% compared with the best-performing baseline. Moreover, deployment on an embedded platform validates its practical applicability, achieving comparable computational efficiency to PC implementations. The source code and dataset are publicly available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Towards Robot Skill Learning and Adaptation with Gaussian Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01480)**  `arXiv:2603.01480`  `cs.RO`  
  _A K M Nadimul Haque, Fouad Sukkar, Sheila Sutjipto, Cedric Le Gentil, Marc G. Carmichael, Teresa Vidal-Calleja_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General robot skill adaptation requires expressive representations robust to varying task configurations. While recent learning-based skill adaptation methods refined via Reinforcement Learning (RL), have shown success, existing skill models often lack sufficient representational capacity for anything beyond minor environmental changes. In contrast, Gaussian Process (GP)-based skill modelling provides an expressive representation with useful analytical properties; however, adaptation of GP-based skills remains underexplored. This paper proposes a novel, robust skill adaptation framework that utilises GPs with sparse via-points for compact and expressive modelling. The model considers the trajectory's poses and leverages its first and second analytical derivatives to preserve the skill's kinematic profile. We present three adaptation methods to cater for the variability between initial and observed configurations. Firstly, an optimisation agent that adjusts the path's via-points while preserving the demonstration velocity. Second, a behaviour cloning agent trained to replicate output trajectories from the optimisation agent. Lastly, an RL agent that has learnt to modify via-points whilst maintaining the kinematic profile and enabling online capabilities. Evaluated across three tasks (drawer opening, cube-pushing and bar manipulation) in both simulation and hardware, our proposed methods outperform every benchmark in success rates. Furthermore, the results demonstrate that the GP-based representation enables all three methods to attain high cosine similarity and low velocity magnitude errors, indicating strong preservation of the kinematic profile. Overall, our formulation provides a compact representation capable of adapting to large deviations from a single demonstrated skill.
  </details>

- **[TRAIN: Terrain-Aware Interactive Navigation for Quadruped Robots via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.12724)**  `arXiv:2602.12724`  `cs.RO`  
  _Wei Zhu, Irfan Tito Kurniawan, Ye Zhao, Mitsuhiro Hayashibe_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This study introduces TRAIN: TeRrain-Aware Interactive Navigation, a deep reinforcement learning (DRL)-based framework for quadrupedal navigation across unstructured terrain and human-populated environments. Conventional quadrupedal navigation typically separates motion planning from locomotion control, neglecting whole-body constraints and terrain awareness. On the other hand, end-to-end methods are more integrated but require high-frequency sensing, which is often noisy and computationally costly. In addition, most existing approaches assume static environments, limiting their use in human-populated settings. To address these limitations, we propose a two-stage training framework with three DRL pipelines. (1) TRAIN-Loco employs an asymmetric actor-critic (AC) model for quadrupedal locomotion, enabling agile traversal of uneven terrains without explicit terrain or contact observations. (2) TRAIN-Nav applies a symmetric AC framework for navigation with interactive pedestrians, directly mapping transformed LiDAR data to ego-agent actions under differential-drive kinematics. (3) A unified pipeline, TRAIN, integrates TRAIN-Loc and TRAIN-Nav, supporting terrain-aware quadrupedal navigation in uneven and interactive environments. Comprehensive benchmarks against quadrupedal locomotion and robot navigation baselines demonstrate the effectiveness of TRAIN. Hardware experiments further confirm its potential for sim-to-real transfer.
  </details>

- **[Coordination Matters: Process-Aware Evaluation of Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06557)**  `arXiv:2605.06557`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Maria Ana Cardei, Matthew Landers, Afsaneh Doryab_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) benchmarks commonly emphasize aggregate outcomes such as return, success rate, or completion time. While essential, these metrics can obscure how agents coordinate and fail to distinguish policies that achieve similar outcomes through different interaction processes. We propose a process-aware evaluation framework that complements aggregate performance with diagnostics of interference, coordinated task completion, participation, and coordination efficiency. We evaluate the framework in STAT, a controlled spatial task-allocation testbed, and examine its broader applicability in two established cooperative MARL benchmarks. Across diverse value-based and actor-critic methods, we find policies with nearly identical return can exhibit substantial differences in coordination behavior. Controlled scaling in STAT reveals distinct process signatures for spatial, task, and agent scaling, while a reward-shaping ablation shows that explicitly penalizing assignment conflicts can alter coordination behavior with little change in task return. Results in the established benchmarks further show that the framework remains informative under different coordination mechanisms. These findings support process-aware evaluation as a complement to outcome-based benchmarking. The framework provides a common structure for selecting and interpreting environment-specific diagnostics that characterize not only what cooperative policies achieve, but how they coordinate to achieve it.
  </details>

- **[Where Do Multi-Agent Systems Fail? Evidence-Grounded Diagnosis of Collective Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38761)**  `arXiv:2609.38761`  `cs.MA`  
  _Zhengye Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a multi-agent system answers correctly, it is tempting to conclude that its agents shared, checked, and used information as intended. Yet a system can break one of its collective mechanisms, the rules that govern how agents route, admit, store, and act on shared information, and still return the right answer, while a wrong answer rarely reveals which mechanism failed. We ask what evidence from an execution is sufficient to conclude that a particular mechanism was violated. Our answer is a diagnostic contract, which separates what counts as a violation from which execution records can establish one, and concludes that a violation is supported, ruled out, or unknown; removing records can make this conclusion unknown but never reverse it. We test contracts for four mechanisms by replaying executions from the step where a mechanism acts, once unchanged, once with the mechanism broken, and once with it restored. Broken mechanisms often left the answer correct. An LLM diagnoser detected many more violations from internal records than from public outputs, yet with identical records a generic prompt often claimed certainty the records did not support, which prompts stating the contracts largely avoided. The contracts also applied, in narrow form, to mechanisms in independently developed systems, but a diagnostic behavior that was nearly perfect on our benchmark degraded on an independently developed workflow. A correct outcome is therefore no substitute for records of how collective mechanisms operated, and agreement on one benchmark does not show that a diagnoser transfers to another system.
  </details>

- **[Learnable Hodge-Laplacian Driven Mixture of Experts for Steady-PDE Surrogates on Coarse Unstructured Meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20585)**  `arXiv:2610.20585`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Stavros Nousias, Akis Nousias, Konstantinos Gkrispanis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current approaches for steady partial differential equations surrogate models on unstructured meshes apply a single mixing mechanism to the whole solution. This paper proposes an architecture that partitions the solution among vanilla transformer experts, each focused on a different spectral scale. The partition is built from a discrete Hodge Laplacian $L_0=\star_0^{-1} d_0^{\top}\star_1 d_0$, that serves as a fitting layer measuring high-frequency content. A gating mechanism takes the mesh as input and produces the per-vertex weights that recombine the expert outputs. AirFRANS is a steady Reynolds-averaged Navier--Stokes (RANS) airfoil benchmark. We evaluate our approach in a reduced remeshed version of the benchmark with each unstructured mesh counting 9.6K faces and 4.9K vertices. We compare our approach with the Transolver on the same dataset and dataset slit. Our approach reduces relative $L_2$ error by 31% and interior mean squared error by 47%.
  </details>

- **[Sample, Mix, or Verify? Allocating Test-Time Compute under Imperfect Verifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23255)**  `arXiv:2610.23255`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Srinivasan Iyengar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A common way to make language models and agents more accurate is to spend more compute at inference time: generate many candidate solutions and submit the one a verifier prefers. Success then requires that some candidate is correct and that the verifier picks it. The first grows predictably with the number of samples; the second does not, because real verifiers (unit tests, majority votes, LLM judges, reward models) accept wrong answers, reject right ones, and make correlated mistakes. With a fixed budget, a practitioner must decide how many samples to draw, which models to draw them from, and how much to spend on verification, and today these choices are made by rules of thumb. We introduce SPLIT, which makes them from a small labelled pilot. It rests on an exact formula for the probability of submitting a correct answer under accept/reject and top-score selection that accounts for varying task difficulty, blind spots shared across models, and correlated repeated verifier calls. SPLIT fits the formula by empirical Bayes and uses it to plan budgets on unseen tasks. Across seven benchmarks spanning math, science QA, code and software-engineering agents, 26 models, 175 agent systems and six kinds of real verifiers, all from public releases, SPLIT lands within 0.02-0.8 percentage points of the best allocation in hindsight, while every fixed rule we test loses at least 2.5 points in some setting. Regret depends only on how well SPLIT ranks configurations, and it stays near-optimal under compute, price and latency costs and online. The formula also explains when asking more LLM judges lowers precision. As a forecaster far beyond the pilot, SPLIT beats power-law fitting when the verifier caps success, and we report where it loses.
  </details>

- **[LED: Improving Masked Diffusion Language Models by Lookahead Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23122)**  `arXiv:2610.23122`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Yuyan Zhou, Zeyuan Yang, Yuxuan Song, Zhicheng Cai, Hongli Yu, Weiying Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) have emerged as a promising alternative to autoregressive language models, enabling non-autoregressive text generation through parallel decoding. Existing dLLMs can be broadly categorized into two families: masked diffusion models (MDMs) and uniform diffusion models (UDMs) In this work, we theoretically show that MDMs are easier to optimize due to their mask-based transition schedule but have lower computational expressivity, whereas UDMs enable iterative state revision and greater expressivity but are substantially harder to train. This trade-off raises a natural question: can we obtain the expressivity of iterative revision without giving up the simple, easy-to-optimize transition schedule of MDMs? Motivated by this analysis, we propose Lookahead Editing (\ours{}), which augments masked diffusion decoding with a rewritable proposal state. At each decoding step, the model performs several lookahead updates to iteratively refine future predictions in this proposal state before committing tokens to the masked sequence. This design introduces a rewritable proposal that uses additional compute to improve expressivity while keeping the simple mask-based transition schedule of MDMs. We fine-tune Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen3-8B to obtain LED-\texttt{7B} and LED-\texttt{8B}, respectively. Across eight benchmarks, LED SFT yields average gains of $+11.4$ and $+7.7$ points over standard MDM SFT. Moreover, using SFT alone, LED-\texttt{8B} outperforms LLaDA2.1-16B Q-mode, which is trained with continued pre-training, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning.
  </details>

- **[Predicting Task Difficulty Without Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05797)**  `arXiv:2608.05797`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Stefan Krsteski, Charlotte Meyer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A fundamental challenge in evaluating and training autonomous agents is measuring the intrinsic difficulty of the tasks they attempt. Estimating this quantity can be useful for environment designers creating synthetic data or benchmarks, as well as for constructing training curricula, thereby offering a way to reduce compute costs. Such an estimation becomes increasingly important as agents (particularly LLM-based) move into longer-horizon domains, where empirical trial-and-error becomes a severe computational bottleneck. However existing work is largely confined to static tasks and relies on evaluation metrics that, as we show, can give an incomplete picture of predictive performance. In this paper we study ex ante difficulty prediction across 17 agentic benchmarks spanning coding, mathematics, machine learning, web navigation, function calling, and other domains. We find that AUC can mask poor difficulty estimates, identify token-level entropy as a useful predictive signal, and demonstrate how residuals between expected and observed difficulty can expose environment flaws such as contamination and infeasibility.
  </details>

- **[Tree-Aware Posterior Distillation for Long-Horizon Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23181)**  `arXiv:2610.23181`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Heming Zou, Qi Wang, Yixiu Mao, Yun Qu, Yuyi Guo, Zeyuan Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards has become a scalable post-training paradigm for large language models. However, training agents over long-horizon interactions presents two challenges: using only terminal rewards makes turn-level credit assignment sparse and high-variance, while sequential mini-batch updates on a fixed rollout batch introduce policy staleness as the current policy drifts from the behavior policy. Existing approaches obtain denser credit via learned critics, process reward models, or on-policy distillation, but they rely on a value network, step-level annotations, or a stronger external teacher that increase training complexity. Meanwhile, importance sampling and clipping mitigate policy drift, but they do not specify a finite target that stops sequential updates from overshooting. In this work, we introduce TreeOPSD, a critic-free, tree-aware posterior self-distillation framework for long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning. Treating each interaction turn as a tree node, TreeOPSD branches alternative continuations from shared prefixes and recursively backs up terminal rewards into implicit turn-level credit. It then maps each credit to a closed-form, bounded teacher distribution parameterized by the likelihood ratio relative to the frozen behavior policy. Minimizing the divergence between this teacher and the student policy recovers the policy-gradient direction as the tangent at the behavior policy while bounding updates toward a finite target across sequential mini-batches. Empirically, TreeOPSD improves agentic capabilities across several competitive benchmarks and model scales, especially for long-horizon tasks, e.g., raising the average accuracy of Qwen3-30B-A3B-Thinking-2507 by 4.97 points over GRPO on the Deep Research setting.
  </details>

- **[Can LLMs predict the outcomes of machine learning experiments?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22599)**  `arXiv:2610.22599`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ayush Bansal, Aldo Pareja, Sean Klein, Serge Didenko, Artem Lukoianov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in AI make Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) increasingly plausible. Large Language Models (LLMs) can already propose, select, and run Machine Learning (ML) experiments. However, the space of possible design choices in ML is vast. An LLM that proposes an experiment needs a reliable intuition of whether it is worth running given the timing constraints of long training runs. In this work, we measure such intuition in current frontier LLMs by evaluating how accurately they predict the impact of self-generated ML experiment ideas before these are put into training runs. To this end, we construct a dataset of 288 LLM-proposed ideas on eight MLE-bench tasks. We then prompt five frontier LLMs (GPT-6 Astra, Claude Opus 5.5, Claude Fable 5.1, Kimi K3, and GLM-5.3) to predict the impact of each idea and compare their predictions with the actual outcomes after training. We report four main findings. First, LLM predictions are substantially better than random chance but far from accurate: even the best model, Claude Opus 5.5, explains only half of the variance in outcomes (R^2 = 0.49). Second, we notice that all tested models systematically underestimate the magnitude of the changes. Third, prediction quality varies widely across tasks. Finally, the choice of the coding harness that actually implements the ideas has little effect on outcomes, and we find no evidence of self-preference. Higher reasoning settings and additional context lead to modest gains. Overall, our results show that current LLMs have useful but limited intuition about which ML ideas will work. We release our dataset to support further study.
  </details>

- **[Progressive Misalignment Can Emerge from Benign Recursive Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22467)**  `arXiv:2610.22467`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Karim Abdel Sadek, Federico Di Gennaro, Fanny Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training models on their own outputs has attracted widespread attention as a possible route to advance AI capabilities. One prominent approach is self-distillation, where a model is trained to match the token-level predictions it would have produced when given access to additional context, such as a cue, feedback, or instruction. However, the safety properties of this training procedure are poorly understood. In this paper, we present a surprising safety failure of recursive self-distillation, where each self-distilled model becomes the teacher for the next round of training. We observe that when a safe model is recursively self-distilled on its own (safe) generations while receiving an additional non-harmful cue, it becomes increasingly misaligned across a diverse set of harmful queries; we call this Progressive Misalignment. We show this failure mode in both the Qwen3 and Phi-4 model families on SORRY-Bench and StrongREJECT. Progressive misalignment also occurs in a more realistic setup, where the fixed cue is replaced by feedback from a simulated user with benign intent. Using a gradient-based method, we are also able to find benign prompts that lead to progressive misalignment when applying recursive self-distillation, showing that harmful training prompts are not necessary for this failure mode to emerge. We hope our work encourages future investigations of the safety properties of AI systems recursively trained on their own data.
  </details>

- **[Group-Graph Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22995)**  `arXiv:2606.22995`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yunan Wang, Minghui Song, Zihan Zhang, Shaohan Huang, Haizhen Huang, Furu Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Group-based Reinforcement Learning (RL) has significantly enhanced Large Language Models (LLMs) in agentic scenarios. To achieve finer-grained policy updates, recent agentic RL frameworks have shifted from trajectory-level to step-level training. However, long-horizon agentic RL suffers from severe reward sparsity and delay, as feedback is often deferred for dozens of interaction steps. While existing step-level frameworks refine training granularity, their credit assignment remains coarse-grained and still treats agent exploration as isolated, linear trajectories. This oversimplified perspective ignores the inherent graph structure of state transitions, leading to high-variance state-value estimation and myopic, localized credit assignment. To overcome these critical bottlenecks, we propose Group-Graph Policy Optimization (G2PO), a novel group-based RL algorithm tailored for multi-turn agentic tasks. G2PO explicitly transforms linear interaction trajectories into a global state-transition graph. By aggregating identical observations across different trajectories, we introduce group-aggregation state-value estimation that reduces sampling variance and trajectory-dependent bias. Furthermore, we redefine agent actions as transitions between state nodes and propose an edge-centric advantage estimation strategy. By globally standardizing Temporal Difference (TD) errors across the entire graph, G2PO explicitly identifies and prioritizes critical transitions that drive absolute task progress. Extensive experiments on representative long-horizon benchmarks-WebShop, ALFWorld, and AppWorld-demonstrate that G2PO substantially outperforms state-of-the-art prompt-based and RL baselines, achieving remarkable success rate improvements of up to 22.2% over GRPO.
  </details>

- **[Two Minds Better Than One: Collaborative Reward Modeling for LLM Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10597)**  `arXiv:2505.10597`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiazheng Zhang, Wenqing Jing, Zizhuo Zhang, Zhiheng Xi, Shihan Dou, Rongxiang Weng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward models (RMs) are central to aligning large language models (LLMs) with human values, yet noisy preference feedback can induce reward misgeneralization through spurious correlations and overfitting. We characterize the training dynamics of preference pairs and uncover a distinctive optimization signature of noise: persistently high losses, sharp temporal fluctuations, and irregular gradient updates. Our analyses further show that excluding such pairs improves downstream alignment. In practice, however, reliable preferences are not known a priori, and self-filtering based on a model's own training signals can amplify confirmation bias. To address this challenge, we introduce Collaborative Reward Modeling (CRM), an online framework for identifying reliable preferences. CRM couples cross-model peer review with curriculum learning: two RMs evaluate each other's sample selections, while curriculum keeps their learning progress synchronized. Across extensive experiments, CRM improves RM generalization by up to 9.94 points on RewardBench under 40% preference noise. CRM further generalizes to implicit-reward alignment, offering a robust and broadly applicable approach to learning from imperfect feedback.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Marginal Influence: Interaction-Aware Memory Selection for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23266)**  `arXiv:2610.23266`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _BHVSP Subrahmanyam, Neeraj Choudhary_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-agent memory selection often uses retrieval rankings based on record relevance or individual influence. Such rankings cannot identify sufficient subsets under redundant or jointly necessary evidence. We study per-decision memory sufficiency: selecting a small subset that preserves full-memory behavior. To separate search error from model error, we introduce JointCoreBench, a benchmark with nine interaction families, six workflow domains, natural-language decisions, and exact sufficient-set oracles, and JointCore, a relevance cost-ordered group-deletion baseline that tests blocks before individual records. On 6,912 exact conditions, cost-ordered JointCore recovers a minimum-cost sufficient set in 100.0% of cases, reduces subset evaluations from 30.00 to 11.13 (62.9%), and achieves 87.8% active-memory savings. Across four 12B-70B model families and 432 tasks per model, its unweighted mean is 10.28 versus 16.00 model queries and 50.6% versus 43.1% minimum-cost recovery relative to cost aware singleton deletion. Both methods preserve full-memory behavior under the deterministic primary interface. Tests with physical deletion, UNKNOWN masks, PENDING sentinels, counterfactual values, and alternative labels identify interface dependent behavior. JointCore is an efficient benchmark baseline; the study does not establish deployment-ready memory compression.
  </details>

- **[A Stronger Teacher Within: Strengthening MoE Models via On-Policy Bidirectional Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23185)**  `arXiv:2610.23185`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Haoyu Chen, Jun Bai, Yang Liu, Jianfei Zhang, Wenge Rong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has substantially improved Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs, but its outcome-level rewards provide only sparse supervision. On-Policy Distillation offers dense token-level feedback, yet requires a stronger compatible teacher, while on-policy self-distillation relies on hand-designed privileged information. We observe that the MoE architecture itself offers a way to resolve this dilemma, as increasing the activation budget per token exposes latent expert capacity and yields a potentially stronger teacher within the model. However, naive expansion degrades performance because newly co-activated experts are not optimized to work together. We therefore propose MoEvolve, a bidirectional distillation framework following a recovery-then-improvement strategy: we first restore the activation-expanded high-$k$ model and further strengthen it by exploiting its additional active capacity, then reverse the direction of distillation to transfer the enhanced capabilities back into the original low-$k$ configuration, retaining its inference-time activation budget. Across diverse MoE models and benchmarks, MoEvolve improves the initial models by up to $22.2$ domain-average points and outperforms same-budget RLVR baselines by up to $6.5$ points. Notably, the final low-$k$ models often surpass their activation-expanded teachers.
  </details>

- **[Sparse Autoencoders Can Induce Local Geometry for Mitigating Catastrophic Forgetting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22439)**  `arXiv:2610.22439`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Kamil Faber, Katarzyna Filus, Łukasz Pawela_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Catastrophic forgetting remains a challenge in supervised continual learning, as adapting to new tasks causes representation drift and loss of previously learned information. This raises the question of which representational changes should be constrained while preserving plasticity. We therefore introduce Sparse Autoencoder Geometric Anchoring (SAGA), which converts a Sparse Autoencoder (SAE) trained at each task boundary into local, low-rank constraints on representation drift. Replay examples specify where the constraints apply, while leading right singular directions of the encoder Jacobian specify which changes are penalized. The resulting geometry is input dependent and can be retained without keeping the SAE for subsequent tasks' training. We establish its local relation to latent code matching, define its spectral truncation error, and derive a conditional bound on frozen-readout drift. With replay and snapshot history, the method achieves the highest mean Class-IL accuracy and lowest mean forgetting among the evaluated ER, DER++, ER-ACE, and SGD baselines on three pretrained ViT-B/16 benchmarks, with accuracy gains of 12.15, 4.79, and 3.22 percentage points over the strongest conventional baseline on CIFAR-100, CUB-200, and ImageNet-R, respectively. From scratch results are more mixed, while rank ablations and geometry controls show that SAE-induced low-rank geometry offers useful stability-plasticity trade-offs.
  </details>

- **[SelectArena: A Modern Benchmark for Feature Selection on Real-world Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13644)**  `arXiv:2610.13644`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Bastian Schäfer, Dominika Matus, Lennart Purucker, Ricardo Knauer, Erik Rodner, Frank Hutter, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selecting a subset of features (columns) from a tabular dataset is a ubiquitous challenge in real-world applications. Industry experts seek to minimize model training costs, climate scientists require an interpretable subset of factors to understand extreme weather events, and clinicians need to choose among diagnostic tests due to financial and time constraints. When looking at the scientific literature to determine which feature selection method to use, practitioners across domains face an unrepresentative landscape of benchmarking. Despite extensive research, existing feature selection benchmarks provide limited guidance because they rely on outdated models, ad hoc evaluation protocols, or artificially generated data that do not reflect real-world problems. To close the gap between scientific benchmarking and real-world applications, we introduce SelectArena, a modern benchmark for feature selection. We compare 15 widely used feature selection methods on 71 manually curated real-world tabular datasets, covering classification and regression problems, using standardized evaluation protocols and state-of-the-art tabular (foundation) models. Our results reveal clear trade-offs across predictive performance, runtime, stability, and validity. Tree-based methods, CART and RFImportance, along with F-Test are Pareto-optimal when taking into account predictive performance and runtime. F-Test, information-theory-, and distance-based methods lead on stability and validity, with F-Test emerging as a balanced choice across our metrics. Search-based methods are slow, unstable and achieve low performance. SelectArena turns these findings into practical guidance and, as an extensible platform, supports future feature selection research.
  </details>

- **[FormInv: A Measurement Protocol for Semantic Invariance in Mathematical Reasoning Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29001)**  `arXiv:2605.29001`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Nishal Thomas, Noel Thomas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A paraphrase-quality audit of MathCheck (ICLR 2025) detected 4 semantically incorrect paraphrases in 129 groups (3.1%); removing them drops GPT-4o from rank 2 to rank 4 and elevates Claude Haiku and DeepSeek V3 above it. Cross-model unanimity found these errors automatically for under $10; in our own dataset the same protocol found that 47% of auto-generated connective-variation paraphrases were semantically incorrect. That flaw compounds a deeper measurement gap. On the theorems every model answers correctly in canonical form, a semantically equivalent rewording still flips the answer for 5-42% of them: Claude Haiku 4.5 flips a known-correct answer about 9x as often as DeepSeek V3 (41.9% vs. 4.7% on the 43 theorems all models share), a difficulty-controlled gap we call the Conditional Inconsistency Rate (CIR). The CIR ranking replicates on an independent 103-theorem set. Raw Semantic Consistency Rates (SCR) span 50-82% across 9 models, but this spread is mostly a mechanical consequence of compounding accuracy over k ~ 6 paraphrases; what CIR isolates is the within-theorem clustering of failures. Because no model dominates on all paraphrase families, the families a benchmark includes implicitly fix its model ranking (a No-Free-Benchmark corollary). FormInv supplies the audit protocol, CIR and SCR as invariance measures, evaluated on 9 models across 366-811 items whose canonical statements are Lean4-verified, and FormInvSelector for regime-aware model selection.
  </details>

- **[STRIDE: Reasoning-Aware Training for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08625)**  `arXiv:2605.08625`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Md Atik Ahamed, Mihir Parmar, Palash Goyal, Chun-Liang Li, Qiang Cheng, Tomas Pfister, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) excel at numerical forecasting but operate as black boxes lacking qualitative reasoning. Conversely, applying LLMs directly to temporal data introduces a modality gap: text tokenizers fragment continuous numerical values, degrading mathematical relationships and exploding sequence lengths, leading to computational overhead. To resolve this, we introduce STRIDE (Strategic Time-series Reasoning Injected via Distilled Embeddings), a novel framework natively integrating LLM reasoning into the continuous embedding space of TSFMs. Instead of discrete tokens, STRIDE distills reasoning traces into a lightweight LLM and dynamically projects its pooled hidden states as a continuous cross-modal prior into the numerical encoder. Jointly optimized via cross-entropy and quantile losses, STRIDE readily attaches to diverse architectures (e.g., Chronos-2, Timer-S1). STRIDE ranks #1 on the GIFT-Eval leaderboard (0.625 MASE, 0.423 CRPS) when ensembled with Synapse. On TFRBench, STRIDE consistently outperforms baselines in both forecasting accuracy and reasoning quality, with ablations confirming gains stem from strategic reasoning. Thus, injecting semantic reasoning as a continuous prior equips TSFMs with human-interpretable reasoning while improving predictive accuracy.
  </details>

- **[MOSAIC: Module Discovery via Sparse Additive Identifiable Causal Learning for Scientific Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05524)**  `arXiv:2605.05524`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Shicheng Fan, Nour Elhendawy, Jianle Sun, Ke Fang, Kun Zhang, Yihang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Causal representation learning (CRL) seeks to recover latent variables with identifiability guarantees, typically up to permutation and component-wise reparameterization under appropriate assumptions. However, identifiability does not imply interpretability: latent semantics are typically assigned post hoc by alignment with known ground-truth factors. This limitation is particularly acute in scientific time series, where underlying mechanisms are unknown and discovering interpretable structure is a primary goal. In contrast, scientific observations (such as residue-pair distances, climate indices, or process sensors) are inherently semantic, as they correspond to named physical quantities. This raises a key question: can the interpretability of observations be transferred to the identifiable latent space? We propose MOSAIC (Module discovery via Sparse Additive Identifiable Causal learning), a sparse temporal VAE that integrates temporal CRL identifiability with support recovery over observed variables. MOSAIC identifies latent variables via regime-conditioned temporal variation, and recovers for each latent a sparse set of associated observations through an additive decoder, yielding module-level interpretability. We show that ANOVA main-effect supports are identifiable under general smooth mixing functions, with finite-sample recovery guarantees for a group-lasso estimator and empirical evaluation of MOSAIC's entropy regularizer. Empirically, MOSAIC recovers domain-consistent variable groups across RNA molecular dynamics, solar wind, ENSO climate, the Tennessee Eastman process, and a synthetic tokamak benchmark, enabling interpretable discovery of latent mechanisms in scientific time series.
  </details>

- **[RamanBench: A Large-Scale Benchmark for Machine Learning on Raman Spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02003)**  `arXiv:2605.02003`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Mario Koddenbrock, Christoph Lange, Robin Legner, Martin Jäger, Martin Kögler, Mariano N. Cruz Bournazou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning (ML) has transformed many scientific fields, yet key applications still lack standardized benchmarks. Raman spectroscopy, a widely used technique for non-invasive molecular analysis, is one such field where progress is limited by fragmented datasets, inconsistent evaluation, and models that fail to capture the structure of spectral data. We introduce RamanBench, the first large-scale, fully reproducible benchmark for ML on Raman spectroscopy, consisting of streamlined data access (this https URL), evaluation protocols and code (this https URL), as well as a live leaderboard (this https URL). It unifies 77 datasets (including 17 first released with this benchmark) across four domains, comprising 325,862 spectra and spanning classification and regression tasks under diverse experimental conditions. We benchmark 28 models under a standardized protocol, including classical methods (e.g., PLS), Raman-specific (e.g., RamanNet), tabular foundation models (TFMs; e.g., TabPFN), and time-series approaches (e.g., ROCKET). TFMs consistently outperform domain-specific and gradient boosting baselines, while time-series models remain competitive. However, no single method dominates across these heterogeneous datasets, revealing that Raman spectroscopy remains an open challenge for machine learning. Therefore, we invite the community to contribute new approaches to our living benchmark, with the potential to accelerate advances in critical applications such as medical diagnostics, biological research, and materials science.
  </details>

- **[Interpretable experiential learning based on state history and global feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00940)**  `arXiv:2605.00940`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Anton Kolonin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A new interpretable experiential learning model based on state history and global feedback is presented. It is capable of learning a behavioral model represented by a transition graph between sets of states, with transitions attributed with utility and evidence count. This model is expected to be suitable for solving reinforcement learning problem in resource-constrained environments. The model was thoroughly evaluated on the OpenAI Gym Atari Breakout benchmark, demonstrating performance comparable to some known neural network-based solutions.
  </details>

- **[CastFlow: Bridging Evidence Acquisition and Accurate Prediction in Agentic Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27840)**  `arXiv:2604.27840`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Bokai Pan, Mingyue Cheng, Xiaoyu Tao, Zhiding Liu, Shuo Yu, Yuchong Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series forecasting is a fundamental task in temporal data analytics, and large language model (LLM)-based agents offer a promising paradigm through autonomous tool interaction and evidence-grounded reasoning. However, two fundamental challenges remain: how to acquire forecast-relevant evidence through adaptive tool interactions, and how to translate such evidence into accurate numerical predictions. To address these challenges, we propose CastFlow, a role-specialized agentic forecasting framework that bridges general-purpose tool reasoning and specialized temporal forecasting. Specifically, CastFlow introduces an Experience Memory that distills successful tool-use trajectories from training data, guiding a frozen LLM to orchestrate a multi-view toolkit for evidence acquisition. A lightweight specialized LLM then performs evidence-grounded temporal reasoning to refine an ensemble forecasting prior. To enhance this capability, we develop a two-stage training strategy combining supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, where final forecasting outcomes of the overall workflow guide the optimization of the specialized model. Extensive experiments on ten real-world benchmarks demonstrate that CastFlow outperforms competitive baselines on nine datasets. Our findings highlight the importance of bridging autonomous evidence acquisition and specialized temporal reasoning for accurate and adaptive agentic forecasting. The source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[M$^2$RNN: Non-Linear RNNs with Matrix-Valued States for Scalable Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14360)**  `arXiv:2603.14360`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Mayank Mishra, Shawn Tan, Ion Stoica, Joseph E. Gonzalez, Tri Dao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformers are highly parallel but are limited to computations in the TC$^0$ complexity class, excluding tasks such as entity tracking and code execution that provably require greater expressive power. Motivated by this limitation, we revisit non-linear Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) for language modeling and introduce Matrix-to-Matrix RNN (M$^2$RNN): an architecture with matrix-valued hidden states and expressive non-linear state transitions. We demonstrate that the language modeling performance of non-linear RNNs is limited by their state size, and show how the state size expansion mechanism enables efficient use of tensor cores. Empirically, M$^2$RNN achieves perfect state tracking generalization at sequence lengths not seen during training. These benefits also translate to large-scale language modeling. In hybrid settings that interleave recurrent layers with attention, Hybrid M$^2$RNN outperforms equivalent Gated DeltaNet hybrids by $0.4$-$0.5$ perplexity points on a 7B MoE model, while using $3\times$ smaller state sizes for the recurrent layers. Notably, replacing even a single recurrent layer with M$^2$RNN in an existing hybrid architecture yields accuracy gains comparable to Hybrid M$^2$RNN with minimal impact on training throughput. Further, the Hybrid Gated DeltaNet models with a single M$^2$RNN layer also achieve superior long-context generalization, outperforming state-of-the-art hybrid linear attention architectures by up to $8$ points on LongBench. Together, these results establish non-linear RNN layers as a compelling building block for efficient and scalable language models.
  </details>

- **[AOI: Turning Failed Trajectories into Training Signals for Autonomous Cloud Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03378)**  `arXiv:2603.03378`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Pei Yang, Wanyi Chen, Asuka Yuxi Zheng, Xueqian Li, Xiang Li, Haoqin Tu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents offer a promising data-driven approach to automating Site Reliability Engineering (SRE), yet their enterprise deployment is constrained by three challenges: restricted access to proprietary data, unsafe action execution under permission-governed environments, and the inability of closed systems to improve from failures. We present AOI (Autonomous Operations Intelligence), a trainable multi-agent framework formulating automated operations as a structured trajectory learning problem under security constraints. Our approach integrates three key components. First, a trainable diagnostic system applies Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to distill expert-level knowledge into locally deployed open-source models, enabling preference-based learning without exposing sensitive data. Second, a read-write separated execution architecture decomposes operational trajectories into observation, reasoning, and action phases, allowing safe learning while preventing unauthorized state mutation. Third, a Failure Trajectory Closed-Loop Evolver mines unsuccessful trajectories and converts them into corrective supervision signals, enabling continual data augmentation. Evaluated on the AIOpsLab benchmark, our contributions yield cumulative gains.
