(1) The AOI runtime alone achieves 66.3% best@5 success on all 86 tasks, outperforming the prior state-of-the-art (41.9%) by 24.4 points. (2) Adding Observer GRPO training, a locally deployed 14B model reaches 42.9% avg@1 on 63 held-out tasks with unseen fault types, surpassing Claude Sonnet 4.5. (3) The Evolver converts 37 failed trajectories into diagnostic guidance, improving end-to-end avg@5 by 4.8 points while reducing variance by 35%.
  </details>

- **[LagNet: Lag-Structured Operators for Long-Term Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23735)**  `arXiv:2610.23735`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhenan Yu, Guangxin Jiang, Jiuyun Jiang, Jin Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term time series forecasting maps a finite history window to an extended future horizon. Many existing models focus primarily on the design of input representations and model structures, while paying less attention to the structural form of the history-to-future forecasting operator itself. We examine this operator directly and find that, across model families, sensitivity matrices exhibit recurring diagonal patterns whose diagonals correspond to relative temporal lags. Motivated by this observation, we develop a framework for direct multi-step forecasting based on lag-structured temporal operators. Its basic building block, LagLayer, constructs a Toeplitz matrix by tying history-to-future connections with the same relative lag and incorporates horizon-wise and history-wise scaling coefficients to relax exact sharing. This design enables LagLayer to serve as an effective replacement for temporal linear layers in existing forecasting models. Stacking LagLayer modules with activations and multiple feature maps yields LagNet. Experiments on long-term time series forecasting benchmarks show that our method is competitive with strong baselines, while sensitivity matrix diagnostics and limited-data experiments demonstrate that LagLayer provides an effective and sample-efficient alternative to a linear layer.
  </details>

- **[Blitz: Accelerating the Diffusion Modules of AlphaFold3 and Boltz-2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23703)**  `arXiv:2610.23703`  `cs.LG`  
  _Vinay Saji Mathew, Soundar Kumara, Gretta Kellogg, William K. M. Lai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion-based protein structure prediction models produce all-atom structures in about 200 denoising steps, and that cost has driven a number of few-step samplers. We present Blitz, a few-step distillation framework that uses a geometric noise schedule to distribute steps more evenly across noise scales, then learns those updates through multistep consistency training. Physical steering and target-free candidate selection help reduce the remaining geometric defects at inference. We apply Blitz to AlphaFold 3 and Boltz-2, reducing denoising from 200 steps to eight. Because published few-step results use different benchmarks and scoring procedures, we compare both distilled models with their teachers and other few-step methods under a common evaluation protocol covering structural accuracy, interface quality, ligand placement, and stereochemical validity. Blitz retains teacher-level structural accuracy, with AF3-Blitz outperforming all evaluated few-step baselines and exceeding its teacher on several accuracy metrics.
  </details>

- **[JAXtari: High-Throughput and Easy-to-Modify Arcade Learning Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23650)**  `arXiv:2610.23650`  `cs.LG`  
  _Quentin Delfosse, Raban Emunds, Paul Seitz, Sebastian Wette, Jannis Blüml, Daniel Kirn, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Arcade Learning Environment (ALE) has been a cornerstone of Deep Reinforcement Learning research, providing a standardized benchmark for evaluating general agents. However, its lack of native support for object-extraction and environment modification restricts research into evaluating whether agents learn robust causal models or rely on brittle visual heuristics. Furthermore, the ALE's dependence on CPU-based emulation creates a significant bottleneck in modern GPU-accelerated training pipelines, limiting both experimental scale and iteration frequency. In this work, we introduce JAXtari, an open-source, high-performance reimplementation of Atari 2600 environments. By leveraging massive GPU parallelization, JAXtari allows PPO agents to finish training on 100M environment steps on a single A100-GPU in under an hour. Beyond speed, it provides object-centric observations and a flexible modification system, allowing researchers to, e.g. develop neuro-symbolic methods or discover novel shortcuts and subsequently train and adapt their agents rapidly, sometimes in only minutes.
  </details>

- **[Bridging Compressed Communication and Local Steps via Spectral Method in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23434)**  `arXiv:2610.23434`  `cs.LG`  
  _Dmitriy Bystrov, George Chirkov, Daniil Medyakov, Dmitry Bylinkin, Aleksandr Beznosikov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated Learning (FL) enables collaborative training of complex models across large-scale networks of edge devices while keeping data localized. However, data heterogeneity and high communication overhead remain fundamental bottlenecks. Recently, the spectral optimizer Muon has demonstrated remarkable advantages over Adam-like methods in centralized training. While recent efforts have attempted to transition Muon to FL, naive integrations into traditional architectures fail short. Crucially, existing approaches do not support the the communication compression and local steps --- the two foundational techniques of efficient FL. To address this limitation, we introduce a novel spectral FL method that utilizes both local updates and compression. We establish rigorous convergence guarantees under mild assumptions, notably eliminating the restrictive bounded heterogeneity condition. Empirical evaluation on standard image classification benchmarks validates the efficiency and robustness of our approach.
  </details>

- **[Residual Stochastic Concept Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23421)**  `arXiv:2610.23421`  `cs.LG`  
  _Stephen Marcou, Mateo Espinosa Zarlenga, Sonia Laguna, Moritz Vandenhirtz, Julia E. Vogt, Mateja Jamnik_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Concept-based models (CMs) learn human-interpretable intermediate representations that enable users to understand the model's reasoning and steer its predictions through test-time concept interventions. However, in realistic settings, their performance can be limited by concept incompleteness, where the training concepts fail to capture all task-relevant information. Adding an unconstrained residual pathway to the model's predictive process can overcome the consequences of incompleteness. However, this introduces an inscrutable, leak-prone prediction pathway that may weaken concept interventions, as these often leave residual variables unchanged. To address this limitation, we introduce Residual Stochastic Concept Bottleneck Models (Res-SCBMs), a family of concept-based models that jointly model concept and residual variables through a multivariate Gaussian distribution. Res-SCBMs learn a covariance structure that explicitly captures dependencies between the known concepts and learnt residual variables, allowing interventions to propagate to statistically dependent residual variables through Gaussian conditioning. This extends the intervention capabilities of previous CMs without requiring concept completeness during training. Our results show that, across multiple concept-incomplete benchmarks, Res-SCBMs improve predictive performance over competing baselines while retaining effective test-time concept interventions.
  </details>

- **[Forecasting Voltage Time Series under Anomalies Using a WGAN Model With Lipschitz Control and Progressive Kernel Critic Strategy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23394)**  `arXiv:2610.23394`  `cs.LG`  
  _Najmieh Sadat Safarabadi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In time-series forecasting tasks, the use of recursive forecasting on high-dimensional data substantially increases the complexity of the prediction process. The main challenge is that long-term dynamics becomes harder to model, since the sequences show greater temporal variability. To counteract this limitation and improve the modeling of long-term dynamics, and more importantly, address high-dimensional recursive forecasting, this paper uses a Wasserstein GAN equipped with a progressive Lipschitz penalty and an adaptive kernel. This model is capable of taking a window of the data stream and forecasting the next values, subsequently reconstructing the time-series stream and dynamically adapting its kernel to each new window. The proopsed model is a deep learning framework based on a Wasserstein GAN (WGAN-CLP) with an adaptive kernel to model alterations in streaming voltage time series. To establish the methodological foundation, we review the WGAN-LP formulation another variant model in this class and extend its framework through an adaptive kernel critic model. We then evaluate this extended model using power ratios, correlation coefficient (CC), and relative root mean squared error (RRMSE). The experimental results demonstrate that the proposed model outperforms benchmark methods in both accuracy and robustness. The evaluation shows promising performance of the WGAN-CLP with an adaptive kernel framework in forecasting sequence alterations across the voltage and energy time-series datasets.
  </details>

- **[Data-Efficient Classifier Pretraining Through Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23289)**  `arXiv:2610.23289`  `cs.LG`  
  _Qinyu Li, Yee Whye Teh, Chris Holmes, Joel Jaskari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning with limited data is susceptible to overfitting, especially with modern deep neural networks. Prior to the introduction of AlexNet, reconstruction-based pretraining with auto-encoder and denoising auto-encoder were found to be beneficial, partially due to the limited size of the training sets at the time. Motivated by these traditional approaches, we propose DiffPT, a simple diffusion modeling approach to pretrain a given classifier backbone for limited domain-specific training data. We experiment on time-series data, standard natural-image benchmarks, and medical imaging datasets, and contrast the diffusion approach to a multitude of modern and traditional un/self-supervised methods. While contrastive pretraining performs better on CIFAR-10 and Tiny ImageNet in low-data regimes, DiffPT is particularly effective on medical imaging and time-series tasks, where it outperforms self-supervised baselines across a range of low-data regimes. Our results suggest that DiffPT is a simple and effective approach for data-efficient classifier pretraining on different modalities.
  </details>

- **[FLARE: Physics-Grounded Evaluation of Language-Model Agents for Feeder Restoration with Degraded Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23251)**  `arXiv:2610.23251`  `cs.LG`  
  _BHVSP Subrahmanyam, Neeraj Choudhary_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Restoring service after distribution-grid faults requires decisions from incomplete and conflicting information while respecting electrical constraints. Addressing this need, we introduce FLARE (Feeder-Level Agent Restoration Evaluation), a physics-grounded benchmark for post-isolation feeder restoration. FLARE con- tains 800 AC-feasible cases across five observation settings and allows agents to gather evidence, evaluate switches, and perform restoration actions under fixed operational limits. We evaluate five instruction-tuned models over 78,750 episodes, including topology, distributed-generation, and loading shifts. Mean normalized regret ranges from 0.082 to 0.148. Although every model has zero median regret, P95 regret ranges from 0.814 to 1.000, revealing an important failure tail. Shadow replay shows that 96.4% of 15,706 guard-rejected actions would have caused a physical violation. FLARE enables reproducible evaluation of language-model based restoration support while emphasizing the need for external safety checks.
  </details>

- **[Response-Informed Value Estimation for Cost-Aware Multi-Turn LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23239)**  `arXiv:2610.23239`  `cs.LG`  
  _Haotian Yan, Jiaqi Liu, Bingyi Jing, Tianjun Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-turn LLM routing selects a model at every turn before its response is available, yet task success is revealed only when the interaction ends, leaving each intermediate choice without a label of its own. Logged trajectories, however, retain the response each call produced, which exposes what each turn required and how well the chosen model served it, evidence that outcomes alone hide. We introduce RIVET (Response-Informed Value Estimation for Turn-Level Routing), which exploits this post-call evidence during training while keeping routing strictly pre-call at deployment. RIVET jointly trains two value estimators over a shared state-model representation: a deployable Routing Value Estimator that scores candidate models from pre-call information, and a training-only Response-Informed Value Estimator that additionally observes the realized response. Conditioning on the response turns response-induced return variation from residual noise into an observed covariate, and we show that this reduces the leading asymptotic estimation error of the shared representation. On the MINT benchmark, RIVET solves more held-out tasks than the strongest multi-turn baseline while using 74.7% less total call cost.
  </details>

- **[Process Reward Modeling with Reliability- and Conservatism-Aware Bayesian Belief](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23223)**  `arXiv:2610.23223`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhongjian Qiao, Zhixiang Zhou, Jiafei Lyu, Peisong Wang, Qiaosheng Zhang, Siyang Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Process Reward Models (PRMs) play a central role in test-time scaling for large language model (LLM) reasoning. However, PRMs may provide inaccurate process rewards for test-time scaling, which we identify as arising from two sources of uncertainty: (1) \textit{PRM uncertainty} that arises from insufficient supervision data and imperfect PRM training; and (2) \textit{policy uncertainty} that results from the mismatch between the policy to collect supervision data and the policy for test-time scaling, leading to systematic reward overestimation. Previous studies fail to jointly consider both uncertainties. To bridge this gap, we propose BayesianPRM, which treats the process reward as a random variable within a reward hypothesis space and maintains a Bayesian belief over it. Since exact Bayesian inference over the continuous hypothesis space is generally intractable, we discretize the reward hypothesis space with a reward ensemble and introduce two complementary belief updates, each targeting one source of uncertainty: (1) to address PRM uncertainty, we introduce \textit{reliability-aware belief updating} that measures how well each ensemble member explains the supervision data and updates the belief with an amortized belief network optimized by the evidence lower bound (ELBO); and (2) to tackle policy uncertainty, we propose \textit{conservatism-aware belief calibration} which further reweights the belief toward lower rewards to suppress overestimation. These two updates yield a reliability- and conservatism-aware Bayesian belief, whose expectation determines the process reward. Experiments on multimodal reasoning benchmarks indicate that BayesianPRM consistently yields more accurate process reward estimates while enhancing test-time scaling performance.
  </details>

- **[Grounded Credit Assignment in Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Turn Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23148)**  `arXiv:2610.23148`  `cs.LG`  
  _Qirui Liu, Hao Chen, Yi Wen, Weijie Shi, Yao Zhao, Xianhe Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Outcome-reward reinforcement learning such as GRPO gives every token of a search-agent rollout the same advantage, although the rollout exercises two capabilities: finding evidence and using it. We show that accuracy improves without grounding: Search-R1 raises accuracy on all seven QA benchmarks, yet on Bamboogle the evidence-supported share of correct answers falls from 84.0% to 42.9%. We attribute this to GRPO's credit assignment: rollouts that retrieve answer-bearing evidence are favoured in aggregate only in proportion to their accuracy gain over the rest, a grounding gap that answering from memory or misusing evidence shrinks or reverses. To address this, we propose Grounded Credit Assignment (GCA), which uses verifiable sub-outcomes (evidence retrieval and answer correctness) to correct search and answer credit separately: a wrong answer is blamed more than the search that found the evidence, and a correct answer that bypasses retrievable evidence is credited less. Never negative on evidence-finding search, the correction does not shrink with the grounding gap; capped at 10% of each token's advantage, it never overrides the outcome signal. Without extra models, rollouts, or training stages, GCA improves average EM over Search-R1 by 5.7 (Qwen2.5-3B) and 6.3 points (Qwen3-30B-A3B) and raises this share from 76.9% to 93.4%.
  </details>

- **[GR-OPD: Group-Relative Sequence Credit for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23042)**  `arXiv:2610.23042`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yushu Wang, Renkai Yang, Jingcao Xu, Zili Geng, Haihong Tang, Yaohui Jin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) provides token-level teacher feedback, yet common sampled-token updates assign credit locally and omit the effects of earlier actions on subsequent feedback. We introduce \method{} (Group-Relative On-Policy Distillation), which decouples feedback computation from credit assignment by aggregating token feedback into calibrated sequence-level credit. We establish a theoretical basis for this approach by showing that, under ideal on-policy assumptions, broadcasting raw summed feedback to every response token yields an unbiased estimate of the negative gradient of the sequence-level reverse-KL objective. To support effective learning in practice, \method{} discounts later feedback that may become less reliable under distribution shift and uses same-prompt rollout groups to calibrate teacher guidance. It preserves relative response preferences while retaining a controlled fraction of the group mean, which we term collective teacher pressure. Across five Qwen3 teacher--student settings and six mathematical reasoning benchmarks, \method{} outperforms vanilla OPD in macro Avg@K in every setting, including gains of 10.9 and 11.5 percentage points in two settings with matched thinking patterns. It also maintains stable training under mismatched thinking patterns, where vanilla OPD collapses. Ablations show that retaining an appropriate amount of collective teacher pressure can improve learning, while excessive collective teacher pressure or an unrestricted feedback horizon can destabilize training.
  </details>

- **[Towards Looped Models Done Right, Part I: Topology, Input Injection, Recurrent-State Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23036)**  `arXiv:2610.23036`  `cs.LG`  
  _Benhao Huang, Chufan Shi, Junlin Chen, Shicheng Wen, Zhengzhong Liu, Eric Xing, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped language models reuse physical Transformer blocks across logical depth, but established architectures entangle recurrence placement, input injection, and recurrent state organization. We isolate these axes in models trained from scratch at matched parameter scale, logical depth, and token budgets, and then test whether the resulting design principles transfer to resident- and active-parameter-matched Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models. The prelude-recurrent block-coda sandwich architecture consistently improves performance on context extraction and reasoning-intensive tasks. Input injection primarily enhances knowledge retrieval and context utilization, whereas random initialization and separate H/L recurrent states provide mixed or negative returns. Taken together, these results favor the Huginn-style design: at the 730M-parameter and 336B-token scale, the complete Huginn architecture outperforms Ouro across all ten benchmarks. The advantages of Huginn over Ouro transfer to MoE models. At the 8BA0.8B (32BA3B when unrolled) scale trained on 500B tokens, Huginn outperforms Ouro on nearly all benchmarks spanning knowledge, reasoning, and coding, while also exhibiting more balanced expert routing. Under matched training and inference FLOPs, the Huginn-style model uses 75% fewer resident parameters than a 112-layer feedforward MoE baseline. Despite its smaller physical parameters, it outperforms the feedforward baseline on DROP and GSM8K, matches it on MATH500, and remains competitive on the other benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[A Feature Selection Layer Approach for Multi-Class Classification on Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22898)**  `arXiv:2610.22898`  `cs.LG`  
  _João Pedro Silveira e Silva, Márcio Dorn, Bruno Iochins Grisci_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In the age of data-driven decision-making, deep learning models have become powerful tools for uncovering patterns and making predictions that influence many aspects of society. However, their broader adoption in scientific and business applications is often limited by a lack of interpretability. Feature weighting methods, which assess the relevance of individual features, are among the most widely studied approaches for improving interpretability. While post hoc techniques have advanced significantly, especially in modeling nuanced relationships between features and labels in multiclass problems, embedded methods typically fall short in this regard. Embedded techniques integrate feature relevance learning directly into the model training process, but they are often unable to capture label-specific relationships. To address the limitation, we propose multiple extensions to the Feature Selection Layer, an embedded feature weighting mechanism for neural networks. Through extensive experimentation, we identify the most effective extension and benchmark it against established feature weighting methods. Our results show that the proposed method matches existing techniques in identifying globally relevant features and outperforms them in capturing label-specific relevance. By leveraging the internal dynamics of the trained neural network, it reveals how individual features contribute to the prediction of specific labels, requiring no post-processing beyond the training process.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking and Enhancing Agentic Causal Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22846)**  `arXiv:2610.22846`  `cs.LG`  
  _Pingchuan Ma, Rui Ding, Shuai Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents can now run causal discovery algorithms on a dataset and report what comes out. Whether they do more than that, such as noticing where an algorithm went wrong and fixing it with further tests, is hard to tell from current benchmarks. Most of them give the variables meaningful names, and an LLM shown only the names of the Tübingen cause-effect pairs already gets 99% of the directions right. With the names removed, the data are usually clean enough that picking a better algorithm than PC closes most of the gap. We introduce Heuresis, a benchmark of anonymous simulated datasets whose mechanisms range from linear Gaussian to heavy-tailed. For each family of datasets it separates what a better existing algorithm can gain from what only a closer look at the data can gain, and it traces every edge an agent changes. Every family leaves a gap that none of the algorithms, the router and the two causal-discovery foundation models we ran can close, so a score above the best chosen algorithm has to come from using the data. We instantiate the benchmark with an agent harness and three LLMs, GPT-6.1 Sol, Claude Opus 5.5 and Qwen 3.8 Flash. GPT-6.1 Sol surpasses the best chosen algorithm on all six families, by 14% adjacency F1 on the most difficult one, and Claude Opus 5.5 does so by 7.4% there. A third of the true edges the two agents restore on the two hardest families appear in no algorithm's output. The code and data will be publicly released to facilitate future work.
  </details>

- **[Acquisition Function-Guided Generation for Bayesian Optimization over Discrete Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22763)**  `arXiv:2610.22763`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tommaso Ancilli, Thomas Wedenig, Henry Moss, Robert Peharz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bayesian optimization (BO) over discrete spaces is challenging due to missing gradients and the sparsity of valid solutions. Latent-space BO sidesteps both issues by fitting a surrogate in the latent space of a generative model, which however substantially complicates the direct incorporation of domain knowledge that is naturally expressed in the original discrete space. In this work, we instead combine a pretrained generative model with a surrogate defined directly in data space, where inductive biases such as domain-specific kernels can be readily incorporated. Rather than maximizing the acquisition function over the discrete design space, our method samples candidates from the generative model under a distribution tilted by the acquisition function. To recover useful gradients for sampling, we embed this tilted distribution into a diffusion bridge that yields a smooth, posterior-averaged acquisition function. This leads to \emph{acquisition function guided generation} (AFGG), which we instantiate with a categorical diffusion model and an autoregressive normalizing flow. Across a broad suite of molecular benchmarks, the diffusion model variant performs on par with leading BO methods, while the flow model variant outperforms state-of-the-art methods in most cases and remains highly competitive otherwise. Our implementation is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Spectral Limits of Polynomial Graph Filters on the Long-Range Ising Model Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22713)**  `arXiv:2610.22713`  `cs.LG`  
  _Amin Najafgholizadeh, Mohsen Afsharchi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Long-Range Ising Model benchmark (LRIM) guarantees long-range dependence by construction: each label is the node's spin times the spin field convolved with a power-law coupling, so any model's distance from the target is measurable. We prove that a readout whose filter is a polynomial in the adjacency, the linear core of isotropic message passing and spectral graph networks, leaves an error floor that no degree lowers, because the target varies along a level set of the spectrum on which every such filter is constant. Computed exactly, the floor and the best filter of each degree predict two spectral networks adapted from published architectures to within a tenth of a decade. A filter escapes the floor only if it weighs separately the offsets the adjacency lumps together, and must still resolve the coupling across the whole lattice. A strided filter of about fifty coefficients does both: fitted to the training labels, its test error is 52 times below the best published network's. A dilated convolutional network beats the best published result at 256 nodes, matches it at 1,024, and gives the first in-domain results at the three larger sizes, while a Fourier neural operator with its standard modes falls further behind as the lattice grows. At its standard budget the dilated network stays two to four orders of magnitude short of a larger strided filter fitted to the labels. The analysis carries over to the benchmark's second coupling.
  </details>

- **[Normalizing Flow Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22711)**  `arXiv:2610.22711`  `cs.LG`  
  _Francesco M. Ruscio, Timothy James Hitge, Fred Zhangzhi Peng, Danyal Rehman, Zander W. Blasingame, Nicholas M. Boffi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scalable approaches to learning neural dynamical systems have placed diffusion, flow matching, and few-step generators in flow maps at the forefront of generative modeling. Such state-of-the-art models have limited access to explicit model densities despite evolving from classical generative modeling approaches that leveraged tractable likelihoods, e.g., Normalizing Flows and Autoregressive models. In this paper, we combine these threads of few-step generation and tractable sample likelihoods and introduce Normalizing Flow Maps (NFM), a class of exactly invertible pushforward maps that generalizes normalizing flows and flow maps. More precisely, NFMs learn the integrator of an underlying dynamical system, but crucially, they provide efficient exact likelihood evaluation by construction. To enable scalable training of NFMs, we derive two complementary axes of consistency inherent to NFMs that yield (self) distillation objectives: (1) pointwise matching, which unlocks $\ell_2$-based flow-map style regression losses, and (2) distributional matching, which matches pushforward distributions to the marginals of a prescribed forward process. On conditional image generation on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-64, NFMs achieve state-of-the-art sample quality among parameter-matched normalizing flows, while supporting likelihood-based RLHF. For equilibrium sampling in Boltzmann Generators, NFMs outperform prior state-of-the-art normalizing flows on both single-system and transferable peptide benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Edit Discovery in Human-AI Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22661)**  `arXiv:2610.22661`  `cs.LG`  
  _Alireza Mirrokni, Shayan Kiyani, George Pappas, Hamed Hassani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human-AI interactions are becoming a rich source of data for improving AI systems: while many model responses are already satisfactory, others expose failures that could become valuable supervision if identified and corrected. We study this setting through the notion of an editor: a more capable evaluation process that can inspect a response and either confirm it or modify it, but whose invocation is costly. The editor may be a human expert, a stronger AI system, or the same model equipped with additional computation, context, tools, or information. The key challenge is to decide which interactions to send to the editor before knowing whether an edit will actually be made, since invoking the editor itself consumes limited resources such as expert time, inference cost, latency, or tool-use overhead. We formulate this problem as Edit Discovery Optimization (EDO), which seeks to minimize edit opportunities missed among skipped interactions while controlling unnecessary confirmations and respecting an editor-cost budget. We characterize the population-optimal policy and show that it takes the form of a cost-adjusted threshold on the posterior probability of an edit. Building on this structure, we develop EDIT (Edit Discovery via Iterative Thresholding), an online algorithm for the partial-feedback setting in which edit labels are revealed only for selected interactions. EDIT combines an adaptive edit score, a confirmation-control threshold, and a cost-aware selection rule; it satisfies the editor budget at every round and controls the average confirmation rate up to a vanishing finite-horizon term, without distributional assumptions on the interaction stream. Across four reasoning and factual question-answering benchmarks, EDIT achieves a better trade-off between editor cost, Type-I error, and Type-II error than direct LLM-based batch selection.
  </details>

- **[The Ephemeral Cost of Switching Optimizers Across LLM Training Stages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22650)**  `arXiv:2610.22650`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xinyu Zhao, Davis Wertheimer, Ashutosh Sharma, Rameswar Panda, Paul Pu Liang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work suggests that switching optimizers between pretraining and subsequent training stages incurs a performance cost, but systematic investigation of this phenomenon is lacking. We study the mismatch between AdamW and Muon optimizers by pretraining 1.2B Llama-3 models from scratch while varying the proportion of updates taken with each optimizer, and similarly interpolating between the two optimizers during continued pretraining and supervised fine-tuning (SFT). Surprisingly, we find little evidence of a meaningful optimizer mismatch penalty: final performance is largely explained by the independent effects of the pretraining and post-training optimizers, with downstream performance generally improving as the proportion of Muon updates during SFT increases. Although matching the pretraining optimizer can reduce forgetting during SFT, this does not translate into improved final benchmark performance. We further evaluate AdamW and Muon for task-general SFT of open-weight models, across the Llama and Qwen families up to 14B parameters, where Muon remains competitive with AdamW, despite these models having been pretrained with AdamW. Together, our results suggest that the choice of optimizer for post-training can be made largely independently of the optimizer used during pretraining.
  </details>

- **[Noise-shaped Sampling and Annealing for Scalable On-the-edge Training of Binary and Ternary AI Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22621)**  `arXiv:2610.22621`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zihao Chen, Zhili Xiao, Rui-Jie Zhu, Yipeng Wang, Jason Eshraghian, Shantanu Chakrabartty_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The training loss of binary and ternary neural networks, expanded as a multilinear polynomial over the discrete weights, is structurally equivalent to the energy of a hierarchical high-order Ising model. We exploit this equivalence to apply Ising solvers to discrete-weight network training, while accounting for a fundamental difference in objectives: an Ising solver seeks the ground state of a fixed energy function, whereas training seeks solutions that generalize to unseen data. Our key contribution is $\Sigma\Delta$-Sampler, an optimizer that reconciles these objectives by embedding a stochastic sampling-and-annealing step as a noisy quantizer inside a first-order $\Sigma\Delta$ noise-shaping loop. The loop pushes noise from weight quantization, high-order frustration, and mini-batch sampling out of the low-frequency adaptation band, so that discrete weight updates follow the smooth descent trajectories embedded in the rugged loss landscape. We validate the framework on a planted quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) benchmark with a known minimizer; on CIFAR-10, where a $13$M-parameter ternary model comes within $1\%$ of full-precision accuracy; on ImageNet-1k, where a ternary ResNet-18 comes within $0.4\%$ Top-5 accuracy of a ternary baseline; on class-incremental continual learning, where prior-task accuracy is preserved; and on MatMul-free language models up to $2.7$B parameters, where it outperforms a straight-through-estimator baseline under matched continued pre-training. Compared with standard quantization-aware training, $\Sigma\Delta$-Sampler reduces the training memory footprint by over $50\%$, delivers an order-of-magnitude speed-up, and reduces weight-update memory traffic by four orders of magnitude, making it attractive for on-device training and fine-tuning of large binary and ternary models.
  </details>

- **[Predicting Privileged Information Effectiveness for On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22512)**  `arXiv:2610.22512`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kaijian Zou, Emiliano Penaloza, MohammadHossein Rezaei, Anisha Gunjal, Mohit Raghavendra, Lu Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) enables large language models (LLMs) to learn from privileged information (PI) without a separate teacher model. However, prior work largely treats PI as a fixed design choice, leaving the effects of its content and construction poorly understood. This challenge is particularly important in multi-turn agentic settings, where trajectories contain actions, observations, and reasoning that can be transformed into PI in many ways. We systematically evaluate 16 PI configurations across three multi-turn agentic benchmarks and three student models that vary in scale and reasoning mode. We find that PI effectiveness depends on its content, generation source, and the student model. Information-dense trajectories and PI produced by more capable generators generally yield larger gains, whereas thinking models benefit less consistently. To assess PI before training, we compare PI utility with two KL-based metrics: sampled-token reverse KL ($D$) and error-step KL contrast ($\Delta d^{-}_{\mathrm{err}}$). The KL-based metrics track post-training performance more consistently than PI utility, which can be distorted by information leakage. Empirically, selecting each task's PI by $\Delta d^{-}_{\mathrm{err}}$ also improves post-training performance over random selection, most when the PI candidates differ widely. Finally, effective PI remains beneficial when combined with GRPO, allowing the combined objective to outperform a strong GRPO baseline. Overall, our results provide practical guidance for constructing and evaluating PI in OPSD training.
  </details>

- **[Lean4AgenticRL: Advancing Agentic RL with Verifiable Multi-level Process Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22448)**  `arXiv:2610.22448`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ruida Wang, Gerald Friedland, Tong Zhang, Chris Kong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enhancing the agentic capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) to tackle complex software engineering (SWE) and general terminal-usage problems is a pressing challenge. Current state-of-the-art LLMs often go through large-scale agentic Reinforcement Learning (RL). However, they mostly rely on high-level outcome rewards, with minimal consideration of low-level toolcall and mid-level workflow-stage rewards, which makes the training less effective. To overcome this limitation, we propose **Lean4AgenticRL**, which, to the best of our knowledge, is the first framework that grounds multi-level process rewards in the unified Lean4 formalization schema for open-ended agentic RL. **Lean4AgenticRL** first builds a *problem-specific workflow* system that balances the agent's own capability and controllability during execution, laying the foundation for further verification. Subsequently, our workflow formalization and multi-level reward annotation methods enable agent behavior verification within a general harness system. It provides multi-level rewards to support more effective agentic RL with the workflow-stage-normalized GRPO algorithm. Using **Lean4AgenticRL**, we train **LAR-9B/27B**, a family of models with enhanced agentic capability. Extensive experiments indicate that our model outperforms the leading model of similar size. **LAR-27B** achieves **77.3%** on TerminalBench-2.1. On average, our models outperform the base model by **9.89%** and surpass the second-best model of comparable size we know by **5.07%**. These results indicate the potential of integrating formal methods in enhancing open-ended agentic RL.
  </details>

- **[Guarded Block Transfers for Polytope Distance: Linear Convergence with Explicit Constants and Gap-Certified Screening for SVM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15829)**  `arXiv:2610.15829`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mayank Gupta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hard-margin SVM training is the distance problem between two convex hulls, which the Triangle Algorithm (TA) solves with a certified duality gap. We propose \emph{guarded block transfers}: $k$ Mitchell--Demyanov--Malozemov (MDM) transfers per scan are aggregated into one exact line search, guarded by a comparison with the single best transfer at a cost independent of the number of points $n$, and replaced by an away step when the leading transfer is capacity-clipped. On the Minkowski-difference polytope, where the objective is $1$-strongly convex, we prove global linear convergence with explicit constants and no combinatorial swap-step bounds; the per-step contraction is $k$-independent and only the drop-count bound grows with $k$, so aggregation is provably safe while the saving in scans is measured, not proved. Since the TA certificate is the Frank--Wolfe gap divided by the current distance, gap-safe screening applies, and we bound the iteration after which it has removed every zero-weight point off the optimal faces by $O\!\left(\tfrac{k}{\rho}\log\tfrac{1}{\tau}\right)$ up to logarithmic factors in the geometry, $\rho$ the rate constant and $\tau$ the optimal-face margin. On five LIBSVM benchmarks, with fairer baselines than our submission, the solver matches the baselines to four or five digits wherever both converge, ties LIBSVM on sparse-support hard margins and beats our blended pairwise conditional gradients (BPCG). But our full-cache sequential minimal optimisation (SMO) code ties or beats it on kernel $L_2$ margins, LIBLINEAR's dual solver is up to $4.5\times$ faster on the separable $L_2$ margin, and our solver is one to three orders of magnitude slower than LIBLINEAR's Newton solver on dense-support $L_2$ margins and than LIBSVM on a nearly touching kernel hull. Its edge is the certificate, not speed.
  </details>

- **[MS-ECG-FM: Towards a More Universal Electrocardiogram Foundation Model for Health Monitoring using Multi-source Contrastive Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07662)**  `arXiv:2610.07662`  `cs.LG`  
  _Robert A. Lewis, I-Min Chiu, Kyle Verrier, Karthik Jayaraman Raghuram, Francoise Marvel, Salar Abbaspourazad, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electrocardiography (ECG) records the electrical activity of the heart, aiding diagnosis by detecting abnormalities in cardiac function. ECG foundation models have demonstrated promising results, but are limited by a reliance on ECG interpretation reports as their sole supervision. Because interpretation reports only capture the subset of waveform information routinely recognized by clinicians, this constrains representation learning to overlook the broader diagnostic signals present in ECG. We introduce a new ECG foundation model --- MS-ECG-FM --- that is trained through contrastive alignment to multiple distinct clinical note types, including ECG, echocardiography, radiology, and discharge reports. We evaluate MS-ECG-FM on an extended set of ECG detection benchmarks, showing that it comprehensively outperforms existing methods on the full span of conditions that ECG can detect, including in reduced-lead configurations. Different reports improve representations for different diagnostic domains, while multi-source alignment captures their complementary information and produces consistently strong representations across clinically diverse tasks.
  </details>

- **[Can Graph Learning Learn Circuits?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08536)**  `arXiv:2608.08536`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chester Tan, Moritz Lampert, Courtney Maynard, Ankit Ramakrishnan, Tina Eliassi-Rad, Ingo Scholtes_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Circuit localization is a mechanistic interpretability task whose goal is to identify a sparse subgraph of a transformer's computation graph sufficient to reproduce a particular behavior. Most established methods localize circuits independently for each model-task pair. We instead frame circuit localization as a graph machine learning problem in which the edges of a computation graph represent computational pathways, and graph neural networks (GNNs) model interactions among these pathways. We introduce Graph Circuit Learning (GCL), a supervised, amortized framework that trains a GNN across multiple model-task pairs and applies it to unseen cases. To provide sufficient data, we augment the InterpBench benchmark with additional cases derived from TracrBench programs. Of the 46 evaluated GCL configurations, the one selected by cross-validation scored a median edge AUROC of 0.946 (interquartile interval [0.841, 0.980]) on the 16 held-out cases of our evaluation split. Removing all message-passing edges reduces the median to 0.926. We also adapt PGExplainer, a GNN explainability method, to circuit localization, obtaining a median edge AUROC of 0.950 on the same cases. These preliminary results suggest that graph machine learning offers a natural and potentially powerful perspective on circuit localization, one we hope encourages closer exchange between the two communities.
  </details>

- **[PowerAtlas: Feasibility-Aware Language Models for Electricity-Computing Co-Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26710)**  `arXiv:2607.26710`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kaiwen Jiang, Siya Xu, Ziyue Zhu, Kaiwen Xue, Chao Yang, Anh Tuan Luu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electricity-computing co-scheduling (ECCS) decides where and when computing tasks run together with how generators are dispatched, because task placement changes nodal demand while power availability determines whether tasks can be served. Prior work addresses such coupled problems with optimization or learned controllers, and large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate structured decisions for scheduling and power systems. Solver runtimes vary across instances, and LLM outputs that parse correctly can still violate grid and service constraints. We formalize ECCS as a structured decision task and release ECBench, 2,000 instances with Gurobi-optimal reference schedules and executable checks for grid limits, task service, cost, and latency. We propose PowerAtlas, which trains an LLM with supervised initialization and Feasibility-Aware GRPO on deterministic scorer feedback, and generates the joint schedule in a single response without solver calls or retrieval. On 10-40 paired instances per backbone, selected PowerAtlas checkpoints lower violations by 89-98% and penalized cost by up to 26x relative to base models, with residual supply deficits. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Graph Transductive Sharpening: Leveraging Unlabeled Predictions in Node Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20248)**  `arXiv:2605.20248`  `cs.LG`  
  _Brown Zaz, Mar Gonzàlez I Català, Ferran Hernandez Caralt, Moshe Eliasof, Pietro Liò_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In the transductive setting, where the full graph is observed but node labels are only partially available, progress in semi-supervised node classification has largely focused on architectural innovation. In this paper, we revisit an orthogonal axis: the training objective. We start from a simple observation: transductive models produce predictions for every node during training, including nodes without labels. These unlabeled-node predictions may contain useful training signal, but standard supervised objectives discard them because no ground-truth labels are available. Inspired by the decomposition of cross-entropy into a label-dependent alignment term and a label-independent entropy term, we propose prediction confidence as a natural way to extract this signal in the absence of labels. This motivates Transductive Sharpening (TS): a loss-level modification that minimizes prediction entropy on unlabeled nodes while counterbalancing this effect on labeled nodes. We evaluate Transductive Sharpening across a wide range of node-classification benchmarks and observe consistent performance improvements without requiring any changes to the backbone architecture. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SpecXMaster Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23101)**  `arXiv:2603.23101`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yutang Ge, Yaning Cui, Hanzheng Li, Jun-Jie Wang, Fanjie Xu, Jinhan Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Intelligent spectroscopy serves as a pivotal element in AI-driven closed-loop scientific discovery, functioning as the critical bridge between matter structure and artificial intelligence. However, conventional expert-dependent spectral interpretation encounters substantial hurdles, including susceptibility to human bias and error, dependence on limited specialized expertise, and variability across interpreters. To address these challenges, we propose SpecXMaster, an intelligent framework leveraging Agentic Reinforcement Learning (RL) for NMR molecular spectral interpretation. SpecXMaster enables automated extraction of multiplicity information from both 1H and 13C spectra directly from raw FID (free induction decay) data. This end-to-end pipeline enables fully automated interpretation of NMR spectra into chemical structures. It demonstrates superior performance across multiple public NMR interpretation benchmarks and has been refined through iterative evaluations by professional chemical spectroscopists. We believe that SpecXMaster, as a novel methodological paradigm for spectral interpretation, will have a profound impact on the organic chemistry community.
  </details>

- **[RCPN: towards robust pseudo-labels in unsupervised CLIP adaptation via consistent prototypes and neighborhoods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.22075)**  `arXiv:2507.22075`  `cs.LG`  
  _Eman Ali, Chetan Arora, Muhammad Haris Khan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In unsupervised adaptation for vision-language models such as CLIP, pseudo-labels derived from zero-shot predictions often exhibit significant noise, particularly under domain shifts or in visually complex scenarios. Conventional pseudo-label filtering approaches, which rely on fixed confidence thresholds, tend to be unreliable in fully unsupervised settings. In this work, we propose a novel adaptive pseudo-labeling framework that enhances CLIP's adaptation performance by integrating prototype consistency and neighborhood-based consistency. The proposed method comprises two key components: PICS, which assesses pseudo-label accuracy based on in-class feature compactness and cross-class feature separation; and NALR, which exploits semantic similarities among neighboring samples to refine pseudo-labels dynamically. Additionally, we introduce an adaptive weighting mechanism that adjusts the influence of pseudo-labeled samples during training according to their estimated correctness. Extensive experiments on 11 benchmark datasets demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance in unsupervised adaptation scenarios, delivering more accurate pseudo-labels while maintaining computational efficiency.
  </details>

- **[StopWatch: Goal Grounding for Object-goal Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23841)**  `arXiv:2610.23841`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Xinhao Liu, Yunsheng Tian, Yuheng Qiu, Pieter Abbeel, Xi Chen, Guanya Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pretrained VLMs bring rich world knowledge and semantic priors to object-goal navigation, and fine-tuning a VLM into a navigation policy has become a common recipe. Despite this progress, success rates remain low, and it is unclear which capability these fine-tuned policies lack. Our diagnosis identifies goal grounding as the dominant failure. These policies explore well, yet often fail to notice that the goal is already in view or to estimate how far away it is. To address this failure, we ground the goal explicitly. Explicit grounding substantially improves success and reaches state-of-the-art results on six benchmarks across three scene collections. We also correct annotation defects in these benchmarks, so that future methods are evaluated more faithfully. Our analysis on the clean benchmarks shows that, with ground-truth grounding, our policy nearly matches the expert that generated its demonstrations, leaving the headroom in perception rather than policy learning. Our work points to goal grounding as a missing piece of object-goal navigation and offers a simple and effective solution to the task.
  </details>

- **[EgoTac: In-the-wild Tactile Prediction from Egocentric Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15060)**  `arXiv:2608.15060`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Wenkang Zhang, Chengbo Yuan, Zicheng Zhang, Zhengxue Cheng, Yang Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Touch is fundamental to dexterous manipulation, yet most egocentric human data increasingly used for robot learning lacks tactile information. Directly collecting large-scale tactile data is challenging due to sensor limitations, while human video data is abundant, contact-rich, and easily scalable. This motivates a natural question: can tactile signals be inferred purely from vision? To address this, we introduce EgoTac, a generalizable model that predicts rich tactile information directly from egocentric human videos. EgoTac is trained on a unified corpus of over 5.7M image-tactile pairs, covering both continuous force measurements and binary contacts. By learning from this diverse dataset, EgoTac captures nuanced touch dynamics across varied interactions. Experiments demonstrate strong performance: in-domain prediction achieves an average force error below 0.06N. On out-of-domain contact prediction benchmarks, EgoTac consistently outperforms the state-of-the-art contact estimator. It also captures the rise and fall patterns of real tactile data and enables zero-shot predictions on unconstrained real-world videos. Scaling analyses further reveal that both data diversity and volume improve performance steadily. Overall, EgoTac provides a scalable pathway to extract tactile priors from egocentric human videos, enabling broadly applicable tactile-aware robot learning. Related resources can be found atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RealtimeWAM: One-Step Asynchronous World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06617)**  `arXiv:2610.06617`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Chengtao Lv, Jinyang Du, Shuyi Feng, Yang Yong, Shiqiao Gu, Shunzi Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World Action Models (WAMs) incorporate visual representations from video generation backbones to guide action prediction. Recent efficient WAMs adopt Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) architectures and compute video representations once for reuse by the action expert. However, intra-expert iteration (\ie, multi-step action denoising) and inter-expert waiting (\ie, sequential execution of the video and action experts) still limit inference efficiency. To this end, we present RealtimeWAM, an extremely efficient WAM variant with one-step action generation and asynchronous inference, addressing these two bottlenecks. To reduce intra-expert iteration, we propose Teacher-Anchored Consistency Distillation (TACD) to address a local-global error gap: low local consistency error alone does not guarantee accurate final actions. TACD supplements local consistency with explicit supervision from the frozen teacher's multi-step rollout endpoint, enabling accurate one-step action generation. Additionally, we propose Cross-Expert Wavefront Pipelining (CEWP) to eliminate unnecessary expert-level waiting. It overlaps the two experts through block-wise sharing of the video KV cache, synchronizing only immediately before the corresponding action attention consumes it. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks (\eg, LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus and RoboTwin) and model variants (\eg, Fast-WAM and Faster-WAM) demonstrate the superiority of RealtimeWAM. Notably, RealtimeWAM maintains near-lossless performance (\ie, $<1\%$ drop) across these benchmarks while delivering significant end-to-end speedup (\eg, $\sim25\times$ on H100). Our code and checkpoints are available via this \href{this https URL}{link}.
  </details>

- **[Look, Reason, Draw a Region: Recursive Refinement for Spatial Context Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23302)**  `arXiv:2610.23302`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Wajeeha Tahir, Anna Tegon, Lionnus Kesting, Gamze İslamoğlu, Kristóf Karacs, Luca Benini, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial context prediction requires visual grounding and spatial reasoning to identify vacant regions relative to objects in a scene. Spatial relationships and physical constraints remain challenging for large vision-language models (VLMs). Recursive reasoning has demonstrated strong performance on discrete and abstract reasoning tasks, motivating its use for improving spatial context prediction in natural images. Weight sharing enables higher accuracy with a small trainable parameter count. We introduce Spatial-TRM, a compact recursive model with frozen vision-language features, a convex region decoder, and just 11.2M trainable parameters. This weight-tied reasoner supports six spatial relationships, three reference frames, and open-vocabulary anchor objects. On the RoboSpatial-Home benchmark, Spatial-TRM achieves 29.51% context accuracy, a gain of 9.02 percentage points (44.0% relative) over a separately trained single-step model with the same parameter count. Including the frozen encoders, the model has 877.8M total parameters, approximately $15\times$ fewer than the reported 13B size of RoboPoint. These results show that repeated refinement improves spatial context prediction from pretrained vision-language features at a fixed trainable parameter count on this benchmark.
  </details>

- **[Can the Parts Fool the Test? Counterfactual Pairing Cycles for Relational OOD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23248)**  `arXiv:2610.23248`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Yibo Zhou, Yirong Xiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Relational out-of-distribution errors are easy to describe and hard to evaluate cleanly. An image may be natural, a caption may be fluent, and all mentioned objects may be common, yet the caption may describe the wrong relation in the image. Many evaluations intended to test this behavior can be solved without checking the pairing at all, because negatives differ in caption style, template, length, object frequency, or image statistics. We propose counterfactual pairing cycles, a blocked evaluation primitive that reuses the same two images and the same two captions on both sides of the comparison, changing only which image is paired with which caption. This makes image-only, text-only, and additive component shortcuts cancel exactly in finite samples. The same contrast also gives a training loss for relational outlier exposure and a diagnostic for bilinear vision-language scores. Across synthetic controls, GQA, and COCO, cycles separate coarse image-caption matching from directional role-binding failures, while also identifying geometric size relations that remain strongly visible in global features. In a shortcut-contaminated COCO outlier-exposure stress test, a model selected by a near-perfect shortcut validation AUROC retains substantial relational signal but remains below cycle training on exact cycles; cycle training improves CycleAcc from 0.882 to 0.927. The main lesson is simple: to test whether a model understands a pairing, the benchmark should keep the parts fixed, change the pairing, and ask only about coupling. The official implementation is available online.
  </details>

- **[Shift-Reliance Alignment: Ranking Vision-Language Models by the Cues They Trust](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22610)**  `arXiv:2610.22610`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Faisal Mohammad, Mohamed Younis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) are usually chosen from leaderboards, which give one ranking for every application. On questions about objects that a scene suggests but does not contain, however, the model with the lowest error on the POPE hallucination benchmark is not the one with the lowest error on such questions. For yes/no questions about object presence, we show that the best model for a target domain can be predicted without running any candidate on that domain, given 650 to 1,200 labeled target items that calibrate a detector. We measure each model's reliance on a cue, a feature such as scene context or text in the image, by editing 100 labeled source images so that the cue suggests the wrong answer, and comparing with a matched control edit. Reliance predicts which VLMs are misled by physical sticker attacks (Spearman .93 on nine models not used to design the score) and which hallucinate on unedited COCO photographs, beyond what their tendency to answer yes explains. Shift-Reliance Alignment (SRA) scores a model by its source error on the labeled source questions plus its reliance multiplied by the shift. The shift is the proportion of target questions on which the cue misleads, and a detector estimates it on the unlabeled target images. SRA selects the model with the lowest score. In pre-registered tests on new images where most questions mislead, it lowered the error of the selected model from 30.4% under selection by source error to 18.8% on Objects365 and OpenImages but, against its prediction, not on COCO. On CueDial, a benchmark we release that varies how often a cue misleads, selection by model confidence falls more than 40 percentage points behind the best model when overlaid text denies a visible object, and SRA stays within 3 points.
  </details>

- **[Towards Reconstructing Geographically Diverse Architecture with 3D Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22514)**  `arXiv:2610.22514`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Nicky Kriplani, Yiwen Zhang, Angelina Wang, Hadar Averbuch-Elor_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Internet-scale photo collections of real-world landmarks have driven progress in 3D computer vision yet remain highly challenging for modern 3D foundation models (3DFMs) that estimate scene structure in a single feed-forward pass. In this work, we introduce ArchWorld, a benchmark of 3D architectural landmarks with high geographic coverage and rich metadata. We use this benchmark to conduct a thorough error analysis of current 3DFMs to understand where they fall short and how they can be improved. We examine socially relevant disparities in model error and find that model performance varies by geographic region; however, unlike typical fairness regimes, the trends only partially match training data representation. Our investigation reveals that the observed geographic disparities reflect not only differences in data representation and scene size but also differences in region-specific architectural features and background distractors that make scenes from different regions inherently vary in difficulty. Leveraging insights from these model failings, we introduce an intervention scheme that improves 3DFM performance while simultaneously reducing geographic disparities.
  </details>

- **[Handwritten Text Recognition Lives in the High-Pixel Variance Subspace](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35473)**  `arXiv:2609.35473`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Carlos Garrido-Munoz, Jorge Calvo-Zaragoza_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In self-supervised pretraining for Handwritten Text Recognition (HTR), pixel reconstruction methods outperform contrastive methods, unlike in natural-image classification. We argue that this difference follows from where discriminative signal lies in pixel space: for HTR, it is concentrated in high-variance directions and largely absent from low-variance ones. This predicts that objectives preserving high-variance pixel content will transfer best. We test six SSL methods from three families (pixel-grounded MIM, JEPA, and contrastive) under matched encoder, data, and evaluation protocols on six handwriting benchmarks across five languages. With full labels, pixel-groundrounded SSL achieves the lowest CER on every benchmark and both frozen probes, exposes per-position character information that other families recover only through the readout, and is the only family to benefit from pretraining on real handwriting. Pixel-grounded representations are also more label efficient. Across datasets, encoder alignment with the high-variance pixel subspace predicts CER within every method. With a pretrained LLM decoder, a frozen pixel-grounded encoder is competitive with fully fine-tuned supervised baselines; full fine-tuning achieves the lowest mean CER and ranks first or second on every benchmark. These results show that the value of pixel reconstruction depends on where discriminative signal lies in the input.
  </details>

- **[Not Every Mention Is a Claim: Rethinking Object Hallucination Detection in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23610)**  `arXiv:2610.23610`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Hsin-Ling Hsu, Neeraja Kirtane, Kuan-Hao Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Object hallucination in vision-language models (VLMs) is usually measured by checking each object a caption names against the image. Most benchmarks and detectors alike treat every object mention as a claim that the object is present. This assumption fails when a caption names an object without asserting it, as in "a bench for people to sit on", which mentions people without claiming that any are in the image. We call such mentions non-assertive. Treating them as hallucinations inflates the error rates attributed to models that use such language. To separate them from real hallucinations, we introduce NAME-Bench (Non-Assertive Mention Evaluation Benchmark), 25,497 object mentions from captions that five VLMs generate for COCO images. Each mention is labelled concrete (asserted and present), hallucinated (asserted and absent), or non-assertive, and non-assertive mentions are further divided into four subtypes: depictive, purpose, negation and hedged. All the hallucination detectors that we evaluate flag non-assertive mentions as hallucinations, at rates between 14% and 80%. Adding non-assertive mentions to detector training as negatives largely corrects this on LLaVA-1.5. On the four newer VLMs, a comparable correction takes five to ten times more data, and the detectors then catch fewer real hallucinations. These findings show that current detectors conflate mentioning an object with asserting it, and that this conflation is harder to undo on the four newer VLMs than on LLaVA-1.5. Hallucination evaluation should therefore separate what a caption asserts from what it merely mentions.
  </details>

- **[Enhancing RAG-based Medical VQA with Gated Multimodal Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23588)**  `arXiv:2610.23588`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Daniela Jimenez-Pano, Hugo Jair Escalante, Delia Irazú Hernández-Farías_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical Vision-Language Models (Med-LVLMs) have shown strong performance in Medical Visual Question Answering (MVQA), yet they may still produce factually inconsistent responses. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) can mitigate this issue by incorporating external clinical evidence; however, retrieval is often guided by a single modality, potentially overlooking the joint semantics of medical images and clinical questions. We introduce a multimodal RAG framework that learns a joint query representation through a Gated Multimodal Unit, enabling evidence retrieval to leverage information from both modalities while keeping the underlying Med-LVLM frozen. We evaluate the proposed approach on benchmark datasets. Results show that the multimodal query representation consistently outperforms a reference Med-LVLM and remains competitive with state-of-the-art methods. Our findings demonstrate the potential of multimodal query representations for more effective evidence retrieval in Medical VQA.
  </details>

- **[Execution Before Memory: Diagnosing Memory Failures in Video World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23542)**  `arXiv:2610.23542`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Weiyang Jin, Jiwen Yu, Sihan Xu, Yuwei Niu, Xihui Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A memory test for a video world model should say whether the scene was set, whether the asked change was carried out, and whether the last frame shows the result. Current tests fold these into one number, so a low score is easy to read as forgetting. When the change is not carried out, the hidden state transition cannot appear, so the test is not really a test of memory. We introduce MIST, an evaluation that separates the change from the memory it should leave behind. The model first sets the scene while the change stays hidden, and only later is asked to show it, with leakage before that point kept under control. This separation locates the bottleneck: the model sets the scene but does not carry out the instructed change. The same gap appears in general memory evaluations. On that basis we propose M3-Agent (Make the Missing Move), which recursively revises the details of the input instruction so that the model successfully carries out the requested instruction. Relative to the baseline, it more than triples the MIST reveal score. On the benchmarks, WRBench's score T rises by 26.1% and MBench-T's score U by 52.7%. The method meets the problem from the execution side: it shows what the frozen model can do once the move is stated, and it sets a stricter baseline.
  </details>

- **[SpaceCast-Bench: Evaluating Predictive Spatial Reasoning in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12402)**  `arXiv:2610.12402`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Hongxing Li, Jinyue Su, Dingming Li, Wenqi Zhang, Weiming Lu, Jun Xiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing spatial reasoning benchmarks mainly test spatial perception: reading off relations already visible in the input. Yet real-world spatial intelligence demands predictive spatial reasoning: constructing a scene from observations, anticipating how an intervention changes it, and reasoning about the unseen outcome. We introduce SpaceCast-Bench, the first benchmark to directly and diagnostically evaluate this capability. Built around an observe-transform-infer framework, its 3,862 questions from 182 real-world scenes span 16 task types at three levels: static perception, local prediction, and global prediction, progressively requiring scene understanding, spatial state updating, and relational inference over unobserved outcomes. Evaluating 21 models exposes a stark gap: the strongest model reaches only 58.0% against 87.2% human performance, while spatially specialized models remain near random chance. Controlled analyses further reveal that bridge views are critical for integrating distributed observations, and that explicit 3D evidence benefits models more reliably than generated outcome images or videos. Fine-tuning on our programmatically generated data lifts Qwen3-VL-4B from 34.0% to 65.7% with macro-average gains across six out-of-domain benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Would you still call this Dax? Novel Visual References in VLMs and Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05409)**  `arXiv:2606.05409`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Ada Defne Tür, Gaurav Kamath, Joyce Chai, Siva Reddy, Benno Krojer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs), like human learners, are frequently exposed to new visual concepts, but how they map novel visual references to language after exposure remains largely underexplored, particularly when those references contradict prior knowledge from pre-training. To study this, we present the Novel Visual References Dataset (NVRD): 19,176 images spanning 90 visual concepts across different levels of visual novelty, each with up to 20 increasingly perturbed versions of the original object to probe generalization. Unlike prior work on visual augmentations of familiar concepts, NVRD comprises entirely novel, open-ended stimuli constructed from scratch, mirroring how humans encounter genuinely new concepts. We evaluate 3 open- and 2 closed-source models alongside 2,400 human judgments for direct human-model comparison, and find that (i) models struggle to acquire novel concepts in-context when they contradict prior knowledge, and (ii) while models and humans show correlated sensitivity to visual perturbations, models significantly overgeneralize, extending learned labels to stimuli that humans reject. We contribute NVRD as a corpus and benchmark for research on visual concept learning in both humans and machines.
  </details>

- **[Referring Multi-Object Tracking with Diverse Dynamic Expressions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.05039)**  `arXiv:2406.05039`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Yani Zhang, Dongming Wu, Wencheng Han, Xingping Dong, Shengcai Liao, Bo Du_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Referring understanding is a fundamental task that bridges natural language and visual content by localizing objects described in free-form expressions. However, existing works are constrained by limited language expressiveness, lacking the capacity to model object dynamics in spatial numbers and temporal states. To address these limitations, we introduce a new and general referring understanding task, termed referring multi-object tracking (RMOT). Its core idea is to employ a language expression as a semantic cue to guide the prediction of multi-object tracking, comprehensively accounting for variations in object quantity and temporal semantics. Along with RMOT, we introduce a RMOT benchmark named Refer-KITTI-V2, featuring scalable and diverse language expressions. To efficiently generate high-quality annotations covering object dynamics with minimal manual effort, we propose a semi-automatic labeling pipeline that formulates a total of 9,758 language prompts. In addition, we propose TempRMOT, an elegant end-to-end Transformer-based framework for RMOT. At its core is a query-driven Temporal Enhancement Module that represents each object as a Transformer query, enabling long-term spatial-temporal interactions with other objects and past frames to efficiently refine these queries. TempRMOT achieves state-of-the-art performance on both Refer-KITTI and Refer-KITTI-V2, demonstrating the effectiveness of our approach. The source code and dataset is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[GroundingPI: A Grounding Foundation Model towards Physical Intelligence with Visual Primitives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39601)**  `arXiv:2609.39601`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Qize Yu, Lianrui Fan, Boyu Chen, Jiaqi Liang, Xini Ding, Yue Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Precise grounding matters. It specifies which object is the target and where that object is, even in clutter and for tiny objects, and it has to be fast enough for closed-loop control. Yet vision-language-action (VLA) and world-action models (WAMs) take perception from general-purpose vision-language and video-generation backbones, which still fail in these settings. We introduce GroundingPI, a 4B grounding foundation model that generates points and boxes as quantized coordinates in a shared vocabulary. Training combines multimodal and spatial pretraining, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning with GRPO, using supervision from public datasets and dedicated data engines. Against 44 baselines across 34 grounding benchmarks spanning 11 perceptual capabilities, GroundingPI establishes a new state of the art, averaging 73.68%, above the larger GPT-6 Astra (71.54%). As a downstream visual backbone, GroundingPI improves performance on robotic manipulation and autonomous driving. On RoboTwin 2.0, it outperforms every mainstream backbone we evaluate in all four out-of-distribution settings, by up to 24.8% relative to the strongest backbone. On RoboCasa-GR1, GroundingPI trained with 50% of the demonstrations outperforms those baselines trained with 75%. On nuScenes, used as the visual backbone, GroundingPI attains an average open-loop L2 error of 0.296 m. We systematically analyze GroundingPI's pretraining in scale and data composition. Downstream autonomous driving and robotic manipulation improve as the pretraining is scaled. Analyzing the data recipe across these 11 perceptual capabilities shows dense grounding's substantial benefits for both, and OCR's potential as a catalyst for perceptual learning. These results support grounding as a perceptual foundation, and dedicated perceptual pretraining as a promising direction for foundation models of physical intelligence.
  </details>

- **[GroundAnything: Reconciling Parallel Decoding with Precise Visual Grounding at Flash Speed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39600)**  `arXiv:2609.39600`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Qize Yu, Lianrui Fan, Bowen Ping, Xini Ding, Zetian Song, Junbo Niu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive (AR) grounding models serialize spatial predictions, introducing sequential latency and imposing a causal order on output tokens. We view grounding as visual evidence extraction: objects, locations, and spatial relations are jointly constrained by the image and query, yet their dependencies do not imply an intrinsic left-to-right generation order. This distinction makes bidirectional diffusion a natural fit, allowing spatial hypotheses to emerge in parallel and be jointly refined through iterative denoising. We introduce GroundAnything, a 4B-parameter grounding foundation model that reconciles fast parallel decoding with precise localization through blockwise denoising. Training combines grounding pretraining from public datasets and dedicated data engines, direct AR-to-diffusion conversion with joint AR and diffusion objectives, supervised fine-tuning, and GRPO-based reinforcement post-training. Across 30 grounding benchmarks, our autoregressive variant, GroundAnything-VLM, establishes a new overall state of the art among similarly sized models at 72.42%, remaining competitive with GPT-6 Astra (71.35%). With entropy-guided decoding, GroundAnything also surpasses the prior state of the art at this scale, averaging 61.75% versus 53.32% for the fast MTP-based LocateAnything model. We further explore decoding strategies, showing that an optional self-speculative mode achieves a $4.51\times$ speedup over the AR counterpart with a 0.74 percentage-point drop in COCO F1mIoU. Infrastructure experiments show that progressive inference optimizations translate parallel decoding into practical speedups. These support efficient visual grounding in latency-sensitive real-world systems.
  </details>

- **[BenthicDINO: Physics-Informed Self-Distillation for View-Invariant Side-Scan Sonar Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23215)**  `arXiv:2608.23215`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Taqi Hamoda, Hayat Rajani, Nuno Gracias_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated interpretation of side-scan sonar (SSS) imagery is traditionally hindered by view-dependence, as recorded intensities inextricably conflate intrinsic seabed reflectivity with transient acquisition geometries and acoustic artifacts. To address this, we introduce BenthicDINO, a physics-informed self-supervised learning framework that decouples acquisition dynamics from seabed representations. Utilizing a ConvNeXt-v2 backbone, physics-guided augmentations, and an HSIC penalty, BenthicDINO explicitly enforces feature invariance to physical viewing parameters like slant range and incidence angle. Evaluations demonstrate exceptional data efficiency - achieving near-peak performance in benthic habitat mapping with only 10% of available labels - and strong cross-domain generalization, outperforming fully supervised baselines on the AI4Shipwrecks benchmark using frozen representations.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Negative Prompting for Concept Suppression: Unconditional Anchoring and Localized Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.07658)**  `arXiv:2412.07658`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`
