To bridge numerical coding symbols with the LLM's native text token space, we introduce a dedicated numerical interface featuring value embeddings and a coefficient prediction head. Furthermore, a task-adaptive prompting strategy is designed to enable a single shared model to adapt to diverse subbands within a unified framework. Built on Llama-3.2-3B with LoRA adaptation, WaveLLM achieves 2.51, 2.24, and 2.27 bpsp on Kodak, CLIC Pro, and DIV2K, respectively, outperforming the PixelCNN-based codec using the same wavelet transform by 0.43, 0.36, and 0.46 bpsp, and surpassing recent LLM-based lossless compressors on all three benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[SPARE: Shared Perception and Adaptive Retrieval of Evidence for Streaming Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14671)**  `arXiv:2610.14671`  `cs.CV`  
  _Senbin Xu, Zijun Zhang, Xinhua Wu, Li He, Jubo Feng, Li Qiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming video understanding requires answering questions from the observed video, with evidence drawn from recent observations or earlier events. Historical context can improve recall but may interfere with judgments based on recent frames. Its usefulness depends on both when it is introduced and which clips are selected, while relevance alone does not guarantee sufficient evidence for an answer. We propose SPARE, a framework that combines adaptive historical retrieval with visual encoder sharing. A lightweight gate predicts whether recent observations provide sufficient evidence to answer the question. When additional evidence is predicted to be necessary, a multimodal embedding model retrieves relevant historical clips to supplement the current context. LoRA instruction tuning trains the understanding model to use recent observations and retrieved history jointly and abstain when the evidence remains insufficient. To reduce redundant visual parameters, the understanding and retrieval branches share a visual encoder while retaining separate language backbones. Four linear adapters align the shared features with the retrieval model through distillation at the visual token and final retrieval embedding levels. With visual-only inputs, SPARE achieves 77.78% on the evaluated Real-time and Backward task groups of OVO-Bench and 73.31% on StreamingBench. Compared with Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct evaluated at 2 fps, SPARE improves accuracy by 20.68 percentage points on OVO-Bench and 14.15 percentage points on StreamingBench.
  </details>

- **[Towards Physically Grounded Articulated 3D Assets: A Multi-Agent Pipeline and Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13947)**  `arXiv:2610.13947`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xudong Cai, Ruijie Zhu, Haoliang Shang, Ruining Li, Marc Pollefeys, Shangzhe Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we study physically grounded articulated 3D asset generation---moving the goal from reproducing how objects look to capturing how they move and function. Inspired by the literature on procedural and agentic generation, we present Artisan, a system that extends the latest method Articraft by introducing a multi-agent pipeline with physical simulation feedback, enabling comprehensive attributes beyond geometry, appearance, and physically plausible articulation, such as collision-free motion, stability under gravity, and accurate mass and material properties. Furthermore, we construct a unified benchmark spanning four key aspects: spatial fidelity, physical plausibility, downstream task performance, and overall quality. The benchmark encompasses 100 reference assets from ArtVIP and LightWheel, 50 stability prompts, and 50 ABO objects with reference masses, with protocols evaluating various metrics. Through extensive evaluation of more than 10 representative models, Artisan achieves competitive spatial fidelity while attaining the lowest collision rates and highest stability rates among the evaluated methods. Together, our system and benchmark establish insights for advancing 3D generation towards physically plausible, functionally usable assets.
  </details>

- **[CCDF: A Benchmark Dataset for Deepfake Detection in Surveillance Footage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07939)**  `arXiv:2610.07939`  `cs.CV`  
  _Baptiste Chopin, Thomas Swearingen, Arun Ross, Antitza Dantcheva, Christian Rathgeb_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Due to rapid advances in Generative AI, commercial video generation tools can be used to produce fabricated surveillance footage that can fool both human viewers and automated synthetic video detectors. Since these tools are so widely accessible, a malicious user can create a harmful video clip at minimal cost. The production and dissemination of such videos in high-stakes settings, such as crime reporting and elections, can misdirect emergency response efforts or distort political discourse. Existing deepfake video datasets, used by the research community to develop deepfake detection algorithms, exhibit two limitations: (1) they emphasize benign web content rather than footage of possibly malicious activity, and (2) they rely on older or open-source generators that do not represent recent advances in generative systems. We assemble CCtv DeepFakes (CCDF), a video deepfake dataset, to address both gaps. CCDF contains 1840 videos (460 real and 1380 generated) spanning 16 crime and accident categories, with generated content produced using three leading commercial systems: Grok Imagine, Google VEO 3.1, and OpenAI Sora 2. CCDF is a highly realistic, small-scale, manually annotated dataset targeting evaluation of detection models. We release three versions of the dataset: the raw generated data, a cleaned version in which video metadata are standardized between real and synthetic samples to prevent detectors from exploiting trivial cues, and an altered version simulating low-effort post-processing attacks. We evaluate CCDF with ten recent state-of-the-art detectors covering different detection approaches. Our results suggest that these approaches do not reliably distinguish CCDF's generated videos from real ones, despite their strong reported performance on existing datasets. These results further confirm the benefits of the CCDF dataset for advancing research in DeepFake detection.
  </details>

- **[ME-VLM: A Unified VLM for Embodied Cognition and Agent Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24526)**  `arXiv:2609.24526`  `cs.CV`  
  _Foundation Model, Li Auto Inc_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Physical AI requires models to ground visual and linguistic understanding in real-world environments while accounting for environmental constraints and execution feedback. We introduce MachEmbodied-VLM (ME-VLM), a unified vision-language model with two variants, 4B and 35B-A3B, that brings together embodied cognition and multimodal agent capabilities. Our work emphasizes physical perception and spatiotemporal reasoning, together with planning, interaction, and outcome assessment in both digital and physical environments. We construct training data spanning embodied and multimodal agent tasks, including execution observations and feedback to support outcome assessment and decision refinement. The training pipeline comprises embodied capability injection, separate reinforcement learning of embodied and multimodal-agent experts, and multi-teacher on-policy distillation that consolidates their complementary capabilities into a single model. Experiments show competitive performance on both embodied and agent benchmarks, as well as on autonomous-driving and embodied-navigation tasks. For edge deployment, visual token compression, W4A8 quantization, and hardware-software co-optimization enable on-device inference of the 4B variant on the M100, reducing prefill latency from 400 ms to 188 ms. Project Page:this https URLCode Repository:this https URL
  </details>

- **[IMPLICIT-Bench: Stereotyped Defaults and Prompt Following in Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24228)**  `arXiv:2609.24228`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yue Dai, Ziyang Liu, Marc Cheong, Caren Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image (T2I) models often produce stereotypical images when stereotype-relevant attributes are left unspecified. We ask whether explicit requests can overcome these defaults, especially when the requested attribute contradicts the stereotype. We introduce \mbox{IMPLICIT-Bench}, a challenge benchmark of 1,831 prompt triplets built on a bias knowledge graph (KG) of stereotypes recorded in StereoSet and CrowS-Pairs. Each triplet describes one scene with a neutral prompt, a stereotype prompt, and an anti-stereotype prompt. The neutral prompt measures the model's default, and the two explicit prompts measure whether the model can follow a request on either side of the stereotype. On this benchmark, Qwen-Image depicts the stereotype in 52\% of neutral images and the anti-stereotype in 5\%. On both Qwen-Image and Stable Diffusion~3, explicit requests that contradict this default succeed far less often than requests that agree with it. Where Qwen-Image's default is strongest, explicitly requesting the stereotype succeeds in 97\% of cases, whereas requesting the anti-stereotype succeeds in only 54\%. In these scenes, 31\% of the images for anti-stereotype requests show the stereotypical attribute instead. We further test whether prompt rewriting and embedding steering can reduce the default on neutral prompts. With the prompt's own KG unit, the best configurations improve a combined bias and alignment score by up to 9.5 points, and with a unit retrieved from the bias KG, by at most 2.9. Debiasing thus depends on knowing which stereotype a prompt evokes.
  </details>

- **[Beyond UV Mapping: Mesh Texture Compression via Surface-Aligned Texture Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23606)**  `arXiv:2609.23606`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jianqiang Wang, Junhui Hou, Siyu Ren, Weiyao Lin, Wenping Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mesh texture compression typically relies on 2D UV atlases, whose chart discontinuities and mapping overhead can limit coding efficiency. To tackle this challenge, we introduce TexF, a surface-aligned texture field that organizes texture attributes in sparse voxels derived from the mesh surface. This representation supports high-resolution textures while preserving local 3D correlations for compression and enabling direct surface queries. For bitstream compression, TexF reuses established 3D attribute codecs, with voxel locations reconstructed from the decoded mesh without separate transmission. For GPU-resident compression, we develop 3DNTC, which combines quantized hash features with a lightweight decoder for random-access reconstruction at surface positions. Differentiable rendering enables image-space refinement of both voxel attributes and compressed neural fields. Experiments on the MPEG and AOM mesh compression benchmarks demonstrate improved average rate-distortion performance over representative UV-based methods for both bitstream and GPU-resident compression. 3DNTC also supports real-time rendering.
  </details>

- **[Open ultrasound foundation model for robust segmentation and clinical measurement across heterogeneous settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19230)**  `arXiv:2609.19230`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chao Qin, Fahad Shahbaz Khan, Salman Khan, Sarim Ather, Siddiq Anwar, Rao Muhammad Anwer, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ultrasound is the most widely deployed imaging modality worldwide, yet clinical AI remains fragmented into narrow single-task models that fail when device, operator, or anatomy changes. Here we present SonoCorpus, an open resource unifying 456,963 images and 1,626,085 expert masks from 53 public datasets spanning 24 clinical applications and 17 countries, and SonoBase, an interactive segmentation foundation model pretrained on it. Across fifteen evaluation datasets introducing new organs, devices, operators, and geographies, SonoBase outperforms SAM2, MedSAM2, and the concept-promptable MedSAM3 on every dataset and matches per-dataset specialist models trained on the same data; on fully external data it exceeds the accuracy these baselines achieve on their own in-distribution benchmarks. Ejection fraction derived from its segmentations falls within inter-observer variability (6.63\% error), with fewer misclassifications at the defibrillator-candidacy threshold than either promptable baseline (13\% versus 18--42\%); fetal head-circumference (1.81~mm) and gestational-age (1.2 days) errors fall below inter-observer variability. Where a baseline fails outright, one in four test cases, SonoBase recovers a usable segmentation in 81\% of them, including on handheld probes operated by minimally trained users in two low- and middle-income countries (Sierra Leone and Tanzania). Five labeled examples can help the model adapt to a new setting, and the identical training protocol transfers well to newer models such as SAM3, locating the advantage in ultrasound-specific pretraining rather than any single architecture. To ensure reproducibility and enable the community to build on SonoBase as a platform, we release all checkpoints, optimizer states, data-split indices, deduplication hashes, and starter code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[BG-REAL: A Public Real-Data Anchored Benchmark for Background Manipulation Detection and Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26232)**  `arXiv:2607.26232`  `cs.CV`  
  _Bugra Alperen Uluirmak, Rifat Kurban_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Background manipulation is a practical but under-specified image-forensics setting: the manipulated evidence can sit outside the salient foreground object, while many evaluations emphasize object-centric copy-move, splicing, or generic synthetic edits. We introduce BG-REAL, a public real-data anchored benchmark package for background manipulation detection and localization. The current release is built from Open Images V7 instance-segmentation sources and contains 7,000 processed samples over 1,200 source groups, including 6,000 public-data anchored samples and 1,000 synthetic control samples. BG-REAL covers six edit families, matched authentic controls, source-group splits, mask and leakage QA, 599 human-assisted quality-control rows, three completed external baselines (TruFor, MVSS-Net, and HiFi-Net), and five-seed model evaluation. Beyond aggregate accuracy, we use matched-authentic-control diagnostics to measure how often baselines misclassify re-encoded authentic images as manipulated at a threshold fixed on held-out validation data; false-positive rates range from 0.57 (TruFor, the lowest) to 1.00 (several weak or mask-informed baselines), indicating that re-encoding artifacts are a shared shortcut risk across baselines rather than a problem specific to any one model. The release provides the construction pipeline, evaluation protocol, paper-ready figures, and reproduction documentation. We frame BG-REAL as a background-manipulation-focused complement to general image-manipulation-localization benchmarks, not as a fully real-only or general-purpose benchmark.
  </details>

- **[TSA: Temporal Slot Activation for Persistent Object-Centric Video Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13714)**  `arXiv:2606.13714`  `cs.CV`  
  _Duc Nguyen, Sieu Tran, Hao Vo, Khoa Vo, Duy Minh Ho Nguyen, Nghi D. Q. Bui, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unsupervised video object-centric learning aims to decompose dynamic scenes into temporally persistent entity representations. Existing recurrent video slot-attention methods propagate a fixed set of slots across frames, but typically assume unconditional slot propagation: every slot is updated and decoded at every frame, regardless of whether its corresponding object is visible. We show that this design violates a basic lifecycle requirement for persistent slots: when an object is absent or fully occluded, its slot should preserve its previous state and avoid explaining unrelated visible content. Instead, unconditional propagation creates update-induced state drift, where current-frame evidence overwrites the absent object's representation, and decoder-induced reconstruction interference, where the inactive slot remains coupled to reconstruction through decoder attention. We propose Temporal Slot Activation (TSA), a lightweight mechanism that learns a per-slot, per-frame activation score $\alpha_{k,t}\in(0,1)$ without visibility supervision. TSA uses this activation as a shared latent control variable for slot lifecycle modeling. When a slot is inactive, TSA anchors its state to the previous slot via activation-gated updating and suppresses its decoder participation through an activation-dependent additive bias on attention logits before softmax normalization. This jointly reduces state drift and reconstruction-driven interference. To improve decisions under partial occlusion and gradual reappearance, TSA further conditions activation prediction on a per-slot temporal memory produced by a Temporal Context Encoder. We evaluate TSA on MOVi-C, MOVi-E, YTVIS-HQ, and the occlusion-heavy OVIS benchmarks, using standard (FG-ARI, mBO) and tracking-based (IDF1, HOTA) metrics. TSA improves object decomposition and temporal identity preservation, with large gains on long, heavily occluded videos.
  </details>

- **[LDDR: Linear-DPP Dynamic-Resolution Frame Sampling for Video MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11477)**  `arXiv:2605.11477`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jingfeng Chen, Jiawen Qian, Wendi Deng, Yinuo Guo, Jiaqi Yu, Sicong Leng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models, when used for long video understanding, require selecting an informative frame set from massive and redundant candidates. Limited by the model's visual-token budget and reasoning ability, frame selection is therefore essential to reduce input size while preserving the most relevant information for accurate video reasoning. Existing frame-sampling methods like uniform sampling or relevance/diversity-sampling often miss important events or retain redundant frames. On the other hand, agentic frame selection methods incur very high computational costs. Further, these methods allocate equal tokens to all selected frames, regardless of their importance. We therefore propose LDDR (Linear DPP Dynamic Resolution), which is a training-free and budget-aware video frame sampling framework. It is computationally efficient and allows non-uniform token budget allocation within the candidate frame set. Specifically, LDDR performs query-aware Determinantal Point Process (DPP) frame selection in a task-conditioned feature space, achieving a 3x runtime speedup over standard DPP baselines. It further introduces a Group DPP importance score to guide frame retention and dynamic resolution allocation: assigning more tokens to informative and non-redundant frames while downscaling or pruning less useful ones. We test LDDR on four popular video benchmarks spanning short, medium, and long-range videos. LDDR achieves a 2.5-point gain in low-budget settings and a 1.6-point gain in high-budget scenarios. These improvements are consistently observed across multiple MLLM families and sizes. Further manual qualitative analyses are included to prove the method's effectiveness.
  </details>

- **[GazeCLIP: Gaze-Guided CLIP with Adaptive-Enhanced Fine-Grained Language Prompt for Deepfake Attribution and Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.29295)**  `arXiv:2603.29295`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yaning Zhang, Linlin Shen, Zitong Yu, Chunjie Ma, Zan Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current deepfake attribution or deepfake detection works tend to exhibit poor generalization to novel generative methods due to the limited exploration in visual modalities alone. They tend to assess the attribution or detection performance of models on unseen advanced generators, coarsely, and fail to consider the synergy of the two tasks. To this end, we propose a novel gaze-guided CLIP with adaptive-enhanced fine-grained language prompts for fine-grained deepfake attribution and detection (DFAD). Specifically, we conduct a novel and fine-grained benchmark to evaluate the DFAD performance of networks on novel generators like diffusion and flow models. Additionally, we introduce a gaze-aware model based on CLIP, which is devised to enhance the generalization to unseen face forgery attacks. Built upon the novel observation that there are significant distribution differences between pristine and forged gaze vectors, and the preservation of the target gaze in facial images generated by GAN and diffusion varies significantly, we design a visual perception encoder to employ the inherent gaze differences to mine global forgery embeddings across appearance and gaze domains. We propose a gaze-aware image encoder (GIE) that fuses forgery gaze prompts extracted via a gaze encoder with common forged image embeddings to capture general attribution patterns, allowing features to be transformed into a more stable and common DFAD feature space. We build a language refinement encoder (LRE) to generate dynamically enhanced language embeddings via an adaptive-enhanced word selector for precise vision-language matching. Extensive experiments on our benchmark show that our model outperforms the state-of-the-art by 6.56% ACC and 5.32% AUC in average performance under the attribution and detection settings, respectively. Codes are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[GraphPilot: Grounded Scene Graph Conditioning for Language-Based Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.11266)**  `arXiv:2511.11266`  `cs.CV`  
  _Fabian Schmidt, Markus Enzweiler, Abhinav Valada_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models have recently emerged as promising planners for autonomous driving, where success hinges on reasoning over spatial structure and dynamic interactions from multimodal input. However, existing models are typically trained without supervision that explicitly encodes relational dependencies, requiring them to infer interactions between traffic entities implicitly from raw sensor data. In this work, we introduce GraphPilot, a model-agnostic approach that conditions language-based driving models on structured relational context in the form of traffic scene graphs. We serialize scene graphs at different abstraction levels and formats and inject them through structured prompts without modifying the underlying architecture or training objective. Extensive closed-loop evaluations on LangAuto and Bench2Drive show persistent improvements across LMDrive, BEVDriver, and SimLingo. Controlled ablations and hidden-state probing further indicate that relational supervision contributes substantially to these improvements while increasing the decodability of scene relations. Open-loop SpaceDrive results on nuScenes further demonstrate transfer to real-world data. These results indicate that diverse architectures can effectively internalize and ground relational priors through scene graph-conditioned training, even without requiring scene graph input at test-time. Code, fine-tuned models, and our scene graph dataset are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[GoDe: Gaussians on Demand for Backbone-Agnostic Progressive Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.13558)**  `arXiv:2501.13558`  `cs.CV`  
  _Francesco Di Sario, Riccardo Renzulli, Marco Grangetto, Akihiro Sugimoto, Enzo Tartaglione_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Splat-based radiance fields have rapidly diversified in primitive type, kernel support, and appearance parameterization. This diversity makes compression difficult: most codecs are single-rate, requiring a separately optimized model for each target bitrate, while progressive codecs remain tied to representation-specific assumptions that do not transfer across backbones. We introduce GoDe (Gaussians on Demand), a progressive compression framework whose central contribution is backbone agnosticism. It reorganizes any trained differentiable splat representation into a single scalable bitstream without modifying the backbone or re-deriving the compression structure for each new primitive type. GoDe builds a nested level-of-detail hierarchy via masked-gradient importance ranking, refines all levels with one quantization-aware fine-tuning stage, and stores the base layer and enhancement layers as progressive chunks compressed with shared entropy dictionaries. Any decoded prefix is directly renderable, so one training pipeline provides multiple rate-distortion operating points without per-rate retraining. Across five heterogeneous backbones and three standard benchmarks, GoDe achieves competitive rate-distortion performance while enabling faster rendering and substantially faster progressive decoding than existing methods. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[KiranaBench: Can a Language Model Run a Neighbourhood Shop?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22980)**  `arXiv:2610.22980`  `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Bhuvan Arora, Ankita Karmakar, Chaitanya Jain, Dilesh Churiwal, Harshit Jain, Shivansh Manish Dubey, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly evaluated on long-horizon tasks, including autonomously running a business. Existing benchmarks for this setting are almost exclusively English-language, Western in retail context, and single-agent, with the agent observing the business directly. We study how these results transfer to a multi-agent Indic setting. We introduce KiranaBench, a simulated Indian general store in which four roles (manager, cashier, helper, and bookkeeper) are each played by a separate LLM agent. Interactions with customers and suppliers occur in English, Hindi, or Hinglish, and product demand is calibrated to India's national Household Consumption Expenditure Survey. Evaluating four open-weight LLMs, we find that all fall far short of a fixed-rule shopkeeper baseline: the best model earns only one-eighth of the baseline's profit, and two models finish below an unmanaged shop. Performance is also consistently lower for Hindi and Hinglish than for English supplier communication. KiranaBench offers evidence that current LLM agents are not yet reliable for autonomously operating small businesses in India, and provides a testbed for developing agents suited to settings that are long-horizon, collaborative, and multilingual.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Answer: Student-Conditioned Trace Construction for Reasoning Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23831)**  `arXiv:2610.23831`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Shaoang Li, Yanhang Shi, Yufei Li, Mingfu Liang, Frank Shyu, Sandeep Pandey, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A reasoning trace can accompany a correct answer without providing effective supervision for a particular student. We study how to construct traces that improve learning beyond answer-only supervision by considering both their intermediate content and their accessibility to the target learner. We introduce ReDiST, a student-conditioned trace-construction and distillation framework for evidence-rich reasoning. Starting from the student's own attempts, ReDiST revises answer-correct drafts against the supplied evidence and repairs unsuccessful drafts with additional guidance from the verified answer. A frozen student-matched reader screens and ranks original and edited candidates using answer recovery and verified-answer probability, given the question, evidence, and candidate trace. We distill the selected targets while balancing trace and final-answer supervision with an auxiliary answer loss. Construction and selection occur offline, and inference uses only the distilled student. Experiments on question-answering benchmarks show that ReDiST can improve both source-task and cross-benchmark performance over answer-only, original-trace, and the evaluated trace-selection and rewriting baselines. Across supervision recipes, performance rankings can differ between source and transfer benchmarks, highlighting the importance of evaluating trace teaching utility across distributions.
  </details>

- **[HeRE: A Novel Benchmark for Hebrew Relation Extraction via LLM-Guided Denoising of Knowledge Graph Alignments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22542)**  `arXiv:2610.22542`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ron Keinan, Amir David Nissan Cohen, Reut Tsarfaty_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While LLMs demonstrate strong capabilities in English Relation Extraction (RE) tasks, a significant gap remains in assessing their RE performance in low-resource languages, due to a lack of standardized benchmarks. Creating native, language-specific annotated RE benchmarks is expensive and requires human expertise, while relying on translation yields suboptimal, inaccurate datasets that introduce English-centric biases. To tackle this, we propose a scalable, language-agnostic methodology to create RE data by denoising naturally occurring yet noisy alignments of knowledge bases and corresponding texts. We define a proper alignment via a Natural Language Inference (NLI) task, where a relation triplet is entailed by an input text. We empirically assess five denoising strategies, and demonstrate that multilingual models outperform language-specific models in the few-shot LLM setting, while Hebrew-specific encoders remain competitive under NLI fine-tuning and are strongest in the label-free cross-training setting. We further find that prompting in the native language achieves performance on par with or slightly exceeding English prompts, confirming that the pipeline operates naturally in the target language. The best open-weights model, Gemma-4-31B-it (F1\,=\,0.915), matches the best API model, GPT-5.4 (0.921), and reaches F1\,=\,0.844 on a fully held-out, dual-annotated test set. Applying the optimized pipeline, we release HeRE: a novel, massive, 1.5-million-instance silver dataset for Hebrew RE, designed to support data-intensive experimentation and model fine-tuning. HeRE bridges a critical gap in standard Hebrew RE evaluation, while providing the NLP community with a reproducible, language-agnostic pipeline for RE dataset construction in other low-resource settings.
  </details>

- **[RubricEM: Meta-RL with Rubric-guided Policy Decomposition beyond Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10899)**  `arXiv:2605.10899`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Gaotang Li, Bhavana Dalvi Mishra, Zifeng Wang, Jun Yan, Yanfei Chen, Chun-Liang Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep research agents plan, search, evaluate evidence, and synthesize long-form reports. Training these agents with reinforcement learning (RL) is challenging because they operate over long horizons and their outputs lack exact answer verifiers. To this end, we introduce RubricEM, a unified RL framework for deep research that combines stagewise credit assignment with reflection-based meta-RL for experience reuse. Rubrics unify the framework by providing a shared interface for policy execution, judge feedback, and experience reuse. RubricEM first instills a rubric-guided scaffold through supervised fine-tuning, teaching the agent to plan with rubrics and organize its trajectory into semantic stages. Building on this structural prior, we optimize the task policy with Stage-Structured GRPO, using stagewise judge feedback for finer-grained credit assignment. In parallel, we asynchronously train a shared-backbone reflection meta-policy (with meta-RL) to distill completed trajectories into reusable guidance, contributing through both shared-parameter updates and textual memory. The resulting RubricEM-8B model achieves strong performance across four long-form research benchmarks, outperforming existing open models of larger sizes and approaching proprietary deep-research systems. Beyond final performance, we perform thorough controlled analyses to understand the key ingredients of RubricEM.
  </details>

- **[Aligning Language Models Towards Counterfactual Invariance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22942)**  `arXiv:2610.22942`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Basar Demir, Emily Shen, Ksheeraj Sai Vepuri, Sourabh Kulkarni, Harihar Subramanyam, Ankit Jain, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small variations in prompt characteristics can meaningfully alter the semantics, tone, and contextual assumptions of large language model outputs. Such characteristics can involve group variables such as demographics or other sensitive latent attributes. Sensitivity to such input variables is undesirable when demographic information is causally irrelevant to the task, but might still be relevant for generation of semantically coherent responses. This makes the task of enforcing counterfactual invariance while preserving model utility difficult. To study this problem, we first show that standard model alignment recipes (such as reinforcement learning) increase differences in response quality across demographics, while improving overall response quality, ultimately amplifying what is known as counterfactual bias. To address this, we develop a policy gradient technique involving a group-level reward function that operates on matched pairs of counterfactual prompts. For completeness, we build a dataset of controlled counterfactual prompt pairs and design an evaluation that separately measures cases where demographic information is causally relevant to the answer. Finally, we validate our findings on several fairness benchmarks. We demonstrate that our methods reduce counterfactual differences while preserving overall response quality and that the models continue to adapt appropriately when such sensitive information is relevant.
  </details>

- **[Competence-Based Evaluation of Logical Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22894)**  `arXiv:2610.22894`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jize Jiang, Chengxiang Zhai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have brought impressive performance across diverse tasks; however, shortcomings remain in logical reasoning, including inconsistencies under linguistic variation. To improve the reasoning capacity of LLMs, we must be able to accurately measure their reasoning capacity. Current benchmarks primarily report single accuracy numbers on selected reasoning tasks. Such a performance-based evaluation methodology says little about a model's generalization capacity in reasoning. To address this limitation, we argue for a different evaluation paradigm, called competence-based evaluation (CBE), which tests whether models reliably apply the same competence across a family of problems. A parameterized generator scales complexity while keeping the required competence fixed, and matched answer-preserving presentations test correctness beyond one formulation. We instantiate CBE on elementary ordering tasks, scaling input size while varying presentations of the same ordering rule. Across six GPT and Qwen models, performance is reasonably good over bounded complexity ranges, but even strong models fail the competence condition across the tested family. Chain-of-thought and GRPO extend strong performance without resolving all tested failures; supervised LoRA shows little held-out transfer. CBE provides diagnostic profiles and an evaluation protocol that can grow with future models.
  </details>

- **[SIT-Eval: Situation-Based Interactive Turn-by-Turn Evaluation of Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23612)**  `arXiv:2610.23612`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ravil Mussabayev, Saveliy Zavertyaev, Dmitriy Parpulov, Andrey Savchenko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid proliferation of conversational AI agents necessitates evaluation methods that assess performance across diverse dialogue scenarios, utilizing multiple situational criteria, as appropriate behavior depends on context and encompasses various aspects. Existing dialogue benchmarks typically use static natural conversations, which underrepresent rare situations---a critical safety gap, as low-probability events become frequent failures at production scale. However, limited sample sizes force a choice: either maintain natural dialogues for realism or turn to adaptive situation sampling for comprehensive yet statistically reliable coverage. We introduce SIT-Eval, an adjustable framework for evaluating situation-based conversational agents. We formalize an agent's desired behavior as situational rules: if situation is P, follow rules Q. At each conversation turn, an LLM-interlocutor enacts a uniformly sampled situation. A context-aware LLM-judge leverages all conditionally applicable rules as criteria to evaluate the agent's reply for that situation. The judge is validated using human-verified synthetic golden dialogues. Benchmarking 8 LLMs on 1292 conversation turns reveals failure modes hidden by aggregate metrics: models with similar overall scores vary widely in their adherence to individual rules. All models struggle most with ambiguity clarification and Socratic questioning.
  </details>

- **[MGE-PO: Entropy-Aware Credit Assignment for Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23245)**  `arXiv:2610.23245`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jihao Huang, Guangjia Chai, Zhirui Chen, Lei Wang, Yao Liu, Liu Qi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GRPO assigns the same group-relative advantage to equally rewarded responses within a question, leaving their generation uncertainty unused. We introduce MGE-PO, centered on four-quadrant (4Q) advantage shaping that combines verified correctness with rollout generation entropy. Correctness determines the sign of an additional correction, while entropy controls its magnitude: lower-entropy successes receive larger bonuses and higher-entropy failures receive larger penalties. An online entropy-based question curriculum supports this credit rule using cached rollout measurements. At the final checkpoint after eight training epochs on Qwen3-8B-Base, adding 4Q across three policy optimization settings yields absolute gains of 3.23-3.69% in six-benchmark macro avg@32 and 4.24-11.06% in macro pass@32. On Qwen2.5-7B-Base, the complete configuration raises four-benchmark macro avg@32 from 41.5% to 45.4%.
  </details>

- **[Prism Attention: Proposal-Refined Index Sharing Mechanism for Efficient LLMs Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22985)**  `arXiv:2610.22985`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhenxu Tian, Kebin Liu, Zhengwu Yang, Yi Su, Qingqing Dang, Yanlin Sha, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context decoding in large language models (LLMs) is increasingly bottlenecked by the memory and computation required to attend over an ever-growing Key-Value (KV) cache at each autoregressive step. Although token-level sparse attention reduces this burden by restricting computation to a small subset of relevant past tokens, its practical gains are often offset by repeatedly recomputing layer-wise sparse indices as the context grows. A common remedy is to share sparse indices across layers by computing them only at designated anchor layers, but such static reuse ignores the layer-wise evolution of attention patterns, causing the reused indices to progressively deviate from the layer-specific optima and incur substantial attention loss. By revisiting cross-layer sparsity from the perspective of attention dynamics, we find that inter-layer salient token shift is highly localized: although the exact top-$\kappa$ indices differ across layers, the core high-attention tokens remain stable, with changes concentrated near the expanded boundary of previously selected regions. Motivated by this, we propose Prism Attention, a proposal-refined sparse attention framework. It replaces rigid cross-layer index reuse with a coarse-to-fine strategy, preserving layer-wise adaptability while retaining the efficiency benefits of cross-layer sharing. Extensive experiments show that Prism Attention achieves state-of-the-art performance among mainstream sparse attention methods on both reasoning and long-context benchmarks, while attaining a $3.4\times$ end-to-end decoding speedup over FlashAttention at 128K context length.
  </details>

- **[Uncertainty-Aware Budget Allocation for Efficient LLM Tree Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22858)**  `arXiv:2610.22858`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jianguo Huang, Zhiyi Lyu, Fuxiang Zhang, Yanchen Deng, Deheng Ye, Bo An_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tree search enhances multi-step reasoning in large language models (LLMs), yet prevailing methods expand every step with a fixed budget, irrespective of the marginal utility of expansion. We first show, across existing search algorithms, that continuations sampled from a shared intermediate state are largely duplicates and collapse to a few distinct answers. Critically, we find that the semantic dispersion of sibling actions predicts the redundancy of their downstream continuations, permitting a subtree's budget to be fixed before expansion. An information-theoretic analysis motivates this: treating the per-step budget as the Monte Carlo sample size for estimating a step's residual answer entropy, we derive a sufficient budget that shrinks with sibling dispersion. We accordingly propose $\textbf{Uncertainty-Aware Allocation}$ (UAA), a training-free framework that decouples value from uncertainty: values determine which candidates are promising, while uncertainty, estimated from sibling dispersion at no additional model cost, determines how many merit expansion, via uncertainty-aware pruning and uncertainty-aware budgeting. Across mathematical reasoning, logical reasoning, and planning benchmarks, UAA reduces token usage by up to $90\%$ and latency by up to $9\times$ at comparable accuracy, generalizing across four tree search algorithms and both open- and closed-source LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Self-Evolving Coding Agents via Harness-Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22791)**  `arXiv:2610.22791`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhepei Wei, Wenlin Yao, Baolin Peng, Danqing Wang, Qianhui Wu, Hao Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents have shown promising potential in coding tasks, and their performance is jointly determined by the policy model and the harness that mediates their interaction with the environment. Indeed, harness and policy are mutually reinforcing and evolving only one component is suboptimal---a harness tuned for one checkpoint may not target the failure modes of an updated policy, while policy learning remains limited to the trajectories elicited by a fixed harness. In light of this, we propose Self-HPO, a recursive self-improving framework for coding agents that alternates harness and policy optimization, using the evolving policy as its own harness proposer. Specifically, in each round, the current policy first analyzes its own failures and proposes new harness candidates (H-step). The policy is then updated through reinforcement learning (RL) under the selected harness (P-step), and hard tasks identified during training form the next harness search set, keeping harness optimization targeted to the policy's evolving failure modes. Across three coding benchmarks, Self-HPO consistently outperforms harness-only and policy-only baselines, improving Qwen3.5-35B-A3B's average resolve rate from 57.8% to 64.8%, with gains generalizing to out-of-distribution settings. Further analysis shows that both optimized components retain gains even when paired with their base counterparts, while their joint gain exceeds the sum of their individual gains. Self-HPO also improves reliability (Pass@1) and expands the capability frontier (Pass@k) over the base agent. Additionally, we show that with a stronger external model (e.g., Opus 4.8) as the harness proposer, the same framework yields a further performance boost.
  </details>

- **[Explore Separately, Adopt Selectively: Execution-Guided Tool Use over Large Tool Libraries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22688)**  `arXiv:2610.22688`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Anna Mazhar, Seiji Maekawa, Nikita Bhutani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents must discover relevant tools from large libraries, e.g., thousands of tools, as new needs arise during task execution. When several candidates could satisfy the same need, execution may be required to identify the relevant one. Investigating candidates in a shared trajectory can leave alternatives unexplored or allow failed attempts to influence subsequent reasoning. To address this, we present BRIA (BRanch-Inspect-Adopt), a simple yet effective agent that temporarily isolates candidate investigations in separate branches. Each branch retrieves and executes tools to investigate its candidate; a task agent inspects the resulting execution evidence and adopts only useful results into its continuing trajectory. BRIA retrieves additional tools as new capability needs arise during task execution. We evaluate BRIA on PlanBench-XL and MCP-Atlas using a shared pool of 1897 tools, three language models, and static and adaptive retrieval baselines. Across all settings, BRIA matches or exceeds the strongest retrieval baseline. On MCP-Atlas, it achieves an average claim score of 89.77, compared with 75.40 for MCP-Zero, a gain of 14.37 percentage points. BRIA also lies on the Pareto frontier for task performance and input-token cost.
  </details>

- **[A Plausible Explanation Is Enough to Mislead Multimodal Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22631)**  `arXiv:2610.22631`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Anglin Cai, Zhiqi Wang, Xiaozhi Yang, Yurun Lin, Dongwon Lee, Yuchen Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) increasingly serve as judges in training and evaluation pipelines, often assessing answers together with explanations. Although AI judges are sensitive to textual factors, it remains unclear whether ordinary plausible explanations can sway judgments requiring visual verification. We introduce JUPE, a visual-question-answering (VQA)-style benchmark that fixes answers while varying explanations.Across 14 MLLM judges, ordinary explanations that sound plausible mislead judges, with effects comparable on average to deliberately persuasive arguments. Plain justifications and chain-of-thought rationales reduce rejection of incorrect answers by 12.7 and 13.8 percentage points, respectively. We call this failure plausibility capture. Using signal detection theory, we find a significant mean shift in decision criterion toward acceptance, without a clear change in mean discriminability. Explanations sway judgments across domains, explanation generators, and judging protocols, while tested mitigations offer limited, model-dependent protection. Such shifts can inflate performance estimates when judges evaluate answers with explanations.
  </details>

- **[IndicDetect: Evaluating Cross-Lingual LLM-Generated Text Detection for Hindi, Telugu, and Tamil](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29919)**  `arXiv:2608.29919`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Bhaskar Ganesh Devalla, Junchao Wu, Nilesh Dokuparthi, Greeshma Yaluru, Tatiana Muniz Rodriguez, Lidia S. Chao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid proliferation of LLMs has further heightened the need to develop dependable AI-generated text detection, especially beyond English. Nevertheless, current benchmarks pay little attention to Indic languages and test detectors in idealized settings that do not represent the real world. We present a generalized benchmark for AI-generated text detection in Hindi, Telugu, and Tamil, which we call IndicDetect, designed to assess the robustness of detectors under realistic distribution shifts. IndicDetect comprises highly curated human-written texts matched with LLM-generated counterparts across various domains and generators, and systematically evaluates detectors in the presence of domain shift, generator shift, and adversarial perturbation. Using a single and repeatable evaluation scheme, we evaluate a wide range of statistical and neural detectors. We find substantial robustness failures: supervised neural detectors perform well in-distribution, while training-free methods degrade considerably under unseen generators and adversarial attacks. The severity of these failures varies across languages, with Hindi exhibiting the largest overall degradation under adversarial perturbations. These results highlight that the primary weakness of existing detectors in Indic settings lies in their robustness, not in their peak accuracy. IndicDetect provides standard data splits, an evaluation protocol, and baselines to establish a robust, language-aware foundation for AI-generated text detection in Indic scripts.
  </details>

- **[BEAR-Bench: A Bilingual Enterprise and Academic Reasoning Benchmark for Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17895)**  `arXiv:2608.17895`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Liubov Chubarova, Alexandra Kuleshova, Daniil Volkov, Kirill Sultanov, Alexey Zaytsev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made significant strides in visual comprehension, their ability to reason about text-dense, professional documents remains incompletely evaluated. Existing benchmarks emphasize information extraction, require external domain knowledge, or cover professional documents only as one of many settings. They are also largely English- or Chinese-centric, leaving other languages and Russian, in particular, substantially underrepresented. To address these limitations, we introduce BEAR-Bench (Bilingual Enterprise and Academic Reasoning), a self-contained, complex English-and-Russian benchmark comprising 1000 human-annotated questions based on text-rich business and scientific documents. We evaluate 16 proprietary and open-weight MLLMs, including Gemini 3.1 Pro and Qwen3.5-397B, on BEAR-Bench and observe clear headroom even for the strongest systems. Finally, we use the resulting model outputs to compare existing hallucination detection methods, evaluating not only how often models fail on BEAR-Bench but also how reliably those failures can be identified.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Tokenization: Direct Timestep Embedding and Contrastive Alignment for Time-Series Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18986)**  `arXiv:2606.18986`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yafeng Wu, Huu Hiep Nguyen, Thin Nguyen, Hung Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have given rise to time-series question answering (TSQA), which formulates time-series analysis as natural-language question answering. However, directly feeding raw numerical series into LLMs suffers from a tokenization bottleneck: Byte Pair Encoding fragments continuous values into unstable tokens whose embeddings lack meaningful metric structure, resulting in the loss of magnitude, scale, and trend information. Prior methods use patch-based encoders that split the series into fixed windows, locking in one granularity that breaks patterns and hides exact timesteps, through a separate module that rarely transfers across datasets with different lengths or sampling rates. To address this challenge, we propose CADE (Contrastive Alignment with Direct Embedding), a novel framework for TSQA built upon two key components: direct timestep embedding and semantic alignment. The proposed framework maps each timestep directly into the LLM embedding space through a point-wise linear encoder and MLP projector, preserving exact index-level access while eliminating the need for patching and padding. To further bridge the semantic gap between time-series and language representations, we introduce a novel one-directional supervised contrastive loss that aligns time-series embeddings with frozen class-name text anchors. Experimental results on the public Time-MQA benchmark demonstrate that our framework consistently improves performance across six TSQA tasks, outperforming both open-source and proprietary LLM baselines.
  </details>

- **[Algorithmic Primitives of Theory of Mind Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17819)**  `arXiv:2604.17819`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Qiutong Tony Yi, Wang Bill Zhu, Robin Jia, Jesse Thomason_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Do Theory of Mind (ToM) benchmarks really test theory of mind, or are they actually testing parsing and algorithmic state tracking? While benchmarks vary modality and dialogue length, this paper finds that they re-encode the same few state transition algorithms. We create PDDL-Mind, a neuro-symbolic approach that recovers such algorithms and upstream inductive biases of ToM benchmarks explicitly by parsing narratives into the Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) and executes candidate actions against a predefined set of update algorithms. Doing so provides program-verified state traces to the model. We use our method's performance gain to audit how much of a benchmark's difficulty our inductive bias absorbs. Across MMToM, MuMA, and FanToM, outsourcing parsing to programs alone brings performance close to strong agentic pipelines, while outsourcing state tracking to PDDL moves GPT-4o to close-to-human performance. We demonstrate that complex ToM benchmarks primarily punishes Large Language Models' unreliable implicit implementation of state tracking and parsing algorithms rather than limitations in belief and counterfactual reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Test-Time Scaling with Diffusion Language Models via Reward-Guided Stitching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22871)**  `arXiv:2602.22871`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Roy Miles, Aysim Toker, Andreea-Maria Oncescu, Jiankang Deng, Ismail Elezi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning with large language models often benefits from generating multiple chains-of-thought, but existing aggregation strategies are typically trajectory-level (e.g., selecting the best trace or voting on the final answer), discarding useful intermediate work from partial or "nearly correct" attempts. We propose Stitching Noisy Diffusion Thoughts, a self-consistency framework that turns cheap diffusion-sampled reasoning into a reusable pool of step-level candidates. Given a problem, we (i) sample many diverse, low-cost reasoning trajectories using a masked diffusion language model, (ii) score every intermediate step with an off-the-shelf process reward model (PRM), and (iii) stitch these highest-quality steps across trajectories into a composite rationale. This rationale is then used to recompute only the final answer. This modular pipeline separates exploration (diffusion) from evaluation and solution synthesis, avoiding monolithic unified hybrids while preserving broad search. Across math reasoning benchmarks, we find that step-level recombination is most beneficial on harder problems, and ablations highlight the importance of the final solver in converting stitched but imperfect rationales into accurate answers. Using low-confidence diffusion sampling with parallel, independent rollouts, our training-free framework improves average accuracy by up to 23.8% across six math and coding tasks. At the same time, it achieves up to a 1.8x latency reduction relative to both traditional diffusion models (e.g., Dream, LLaDA) and unified architectures (e.g., TiDAR). The code is availablethis https URL
  </details>

- **[DiscoveryRSI: Recursively Learning to Discover Valuable Hypotheses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23839)**  `arXiv:2610.23839`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jianyuan Zhong, Chenwei Wu, Zhaochen Yu, Xixian Jiang, Xuewei Li, Hao Xiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific discovery requires developing worthwhile hypotheses into practical investigations, yet learning from this evolving research process remains challenging. We introduce an operational definition of valuable and testable research hypotheses: evidence-grounded claims about consequential uncertainties, coupled with feasible investigations, explicit decision criteria, and alternative explanations. Our DiscoveryRSI framework develops hypothesis trees through multi-agent evolutionary search, compiles selected investigations into executable Harbor tasks, and recursively learns through three mechanisms: hypothesis credit optimization attributes scientific assessments to documented research contributions; harness replay identifies productive strategies and recurring failures to refine research workflows; and rediscovery applies accepted improvements to new investigations, generating further experience for subsequent learning. We also introduce DFM-Bench, which provides comprehensive assessment of hypothesis quality and cumulative research progress at the levels of hypotheses, revisions, development paths and research runs. Across four life-science domains, automated evaluation shows that hypothesis quality rises from 4.86 for the base model to 5.39 after two rounds of self-improvement, with the large gains in falsifiability and data resolvability. We further validate hypothesis quality through expert review, comparing our DiscoveryRSI-27B with other research agents. Preliminary human assessments show that DiscoveryRSI-27B scores higher than GPT-6 Luna on hypothesis importance and whether claims are plausible and evidence-bounded. DiscoveryRSI-27B achieved a 6.3% rate of hypothesis correspondence with research published after the model cutoff, largely outperform GPT-6 with 2.5% and Claude Opus 5 with 2.3%, while delivering greater human-rated improvement along research paths.
  </details>

- **[Perceive, Solve, Reason: Progressive Capability Formation in Time Series-LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23521)**  `arXiv:2610.23521`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xin Qiu, Junlong Tong, Wei Zhang, Xiaoyu Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can now perceive, describe, and reason about images and audio, yet time series understanding remains underestimated: most work uses LLMs as forecasters and classifiers, reasoning studies are few and narrow, and no suite trains and evaluates the full range of capabilities. In this paper, we build the first such suite, with 337K training and 44K evaluation samples across three progressive capabilities, Perceive, Solve, and Reason, and use it to ask how time series should enter an LLM that must master all three, and in what order. We find that a dedicated encoder is essential: with one, a 4B model surpasses GPT-4o on every perception attribute we test, whereas textualizing the series costs 8--10 points in reasoning accuracy. The capabilities then build on one another: learning them in order beats mixing the same data and rivals reasoning-only training with over three times the reasoning data, but only when an encoder is used. Analyses further reveal that capabilities are transferred through distinct network depths rather than simply stacked, and that a stage converged on its own benchmark is not necessarily ready for the next. Distilled into a simple recipe, these findings yield 4B TS-LLMs that, without any target-domain data, outperform four recent 7B TS-LLMs on seven of eight out-of-domain metrics, by up to 11 points. We therefore advocate treating time series as a first-class modality for LLMs, read through dedicated encoders and taught to perceive before they reason.
  </details>

- **[On the Tip of the Tongue: First-Token Cueing as a Behavioral Probe of Recall Failures in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23400)**  `arXiv:2610.23400`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jaewon Sok, Jewon Yeom, Seonghyeon Park, Jeongjae Park, Hwiyeong Lee, Taesup Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models often answer factual questions incorrectly in closed-book settings, and such errors are usually attributed to missing knowledge. Yet models often encode answers they fail to produce. We show that many closed-book errors are first-token recall failures: the model commits to a wrong first token, but once given the right one it completes the answer and often recognizes it as correct. We study this with First-Token Cueing, a behavioral probe that prefills the response with the first token of the gold answer. Model uncertainty peaks at the first answer token, where it already predicts correctness, and among gold prefixes of any length the first token alone accounts for the largest share of recovered errors. Across 16 open models, this one token corrects between a fifth and a half of the errors on multi-token answers in English QA and improves accuracy in all seven languages of a multilingual benchmark. Most of the errors it corrects are also solved by the model's own sampled reasoning, a criterion for encoded knowledge, and models recognize correct starts they do not produce: they continue a correct start more confidently than an incorrect start of the same answer type and prior probability, yet rarely find it without the gold answer. These results locate a recoverable class of factual errors at the first decoding step, where inference-time methods may gain the most.
  </details>

- **[Verbalising Model's Computation for Chain-of-Thought Faithfulness Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23275)**  `arXiv:2610.23275`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yujing Wang, Yulan He, Wenya Wang, Hanqi Yan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current Chain-of-Thought (CoT) verification methods detect reasoning faithfulness from model outputs or static activations, but are limited in explaining how unfaithfulness develops during generation. We introduce VERITAS to verbalise the model's computation: how different reasoning anchors emerge, transfer across layers, and surface in the output. VERITAS adopts the layer-wise Jacobian projection to produce a verbalised account of the model's internal reasoning evolution at each generated CoT token. By training a classifier on the extracted features along the traces, we show that these structured features provide insightful perspectives on how and where reasoning diverges toward unfaithfulness. Experiments on four datasets from FaithCoT-Bench and BonaFide show that VERITAS achieves a significant performance gain over LLM-as-Judge, with a 28.4% gain in balanced accuracy, and an average improvement of 16.0% over activation probing. Further analysis shows VERITAS can be generalised to different datasets and model architectures. Our work shows that verbalising model computation provides a richer and more interpretable lens on CoT faithfulness, moving beyond detection toward a mechanistic understanding of how reasoning fails.
  </details>

- **[What LLMs Do in the Shadows: Reading Context Reliance from the Unembedding Dark Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23182)**  `arXiv:2610.23182`  `cs.CL`  
  _Alexandra Kuleshova, Andrey Volodichev, Daria Kotova, Alexey Zaytsev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Detecting faithfulness hallucinations in large language models (LLMs) is crucial for their safe deployment in high-stakes domains. Recent approaches address this by contrasting contextual against parametric knowledge utilization, yet measuring context reliance typically demands noticeable overhead, such as materializing attention maps, running additional forward passes, or training auxiliary probes. In this work, we show that context reliance can be estimated by projecting hidden states onto the dark space - directions that compose weakly with the unembedding matrix. Beyond its recognized function as an initial-token attention sink, we show that this subspace actively mediates context processing, and that its share of hidden-state norm - termed the Dark Matter Ratio (DMR) - provides a direct score of context utilization. Pairing DMR with a parametric knowledge score yields DarkLens, a lightweight faithfulness detector operating strictly within a single forward pass. Evaluated across three QA and summarization benchmarks on six open-weight LLMs (4B-13B parameters), DarkLens matches or outperforms a wide range of baselines without requiring extra forward passes or probe training.
  </details>

- **[Do Speech BCIs Need Larger Models? Rethinking Neural Decoding beyond Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23175)**  `arXiv:2610.23175`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zehui Feng, Weichuan Wang, Xiaohan Chen, Cuntai Guan, Ting Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent speech brain-computer interfaces (BCIs) increasingly rely on large models and complex multi-stage pipelines for neural decoding. However, in speech BCI, where training data are scarce, noisy, and expensive to collect, the benefits of model scaling remain unclear. This work revisits a fundamental question: are large models truly necessary for accurate neural decoding? We propose BEST (Brain-Encoding-Speech-Text), a two-stage framework for efficient neural-to-text decoding. Instead of relying on large generative models or phoneme-centric pipelines, BEST introduces hierarchical intermediate representations and a compact speech-oriented decoder that directly maps neural activity to text. This design preserves essential structure while significantly reducing computational cost, enabling robust generalization in low-resource settings and practical deployment under constrained resources. On the Brain-to-Text '24 and '25 benchmarks, BEST achieves word error rates (WERs) of 6.19% and 1.62%, respectively, using only 3.85% of the parameters required by comparable methods. Beyond these results, we provide three key insights: (1) decoder scaling yields non-monotonic gains; (2) cross-modal alignment is more critical than model size; and (3) overfitting degards sequence-level performance. Representation analysis further reveals a consistent neural-audio-text alignment pathway.
  </details>

- **[Chinese Decision: A Family of Language Models for Single-Pass Probabilistic Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23170)**  `arXiv:2610.23170`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qiang Yan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many language-based applications require probabilities over a finite set of decisions rather than open-ended responses. We present Chinese Decision, a family of Chinese-language models for single-pass probabilistic decision-making. Given context, an instruction, and candidate descriptions, the model extracts final-position candidate-letter logits in one forward pass and normalizes them over valid options, without autoregressive answer generation. A shared interface supports Boolean judgments, categorical choices, and ordinal scores. We report a completed 0.8B model trained with distribution cross-entropy on 13.048 million decisions and preliminary 2B checkpoints trained with an additional RLCD objective. On the full CJ-Bench of 307,900 decisions, the uncalibrated 0.8B model achieves 72.24% general-domain accuracy and 76.98% average accuracy across medicine, law, and finance using a single checkpoint, with 1.07% overall expected calibration error. The 2B model reaches 72.56% general-domain accuracy after 40.13% of one epoch but has not surpassed the completed 0.8B model on the professional-domain average. We further evaluate a validation-temperature-scaled deployment model and its Q8 quantization, finding close aggregate performance while documenting individual prediction changes. We release the training, evaluation, and deployment pipeline. The current results establish a reproducible approach to Chinese probabilistic decisions but do not isolate the effect of model scale.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Surface Cues: Robust LLM-Generated Korean Text Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22940)**  `arXiv:2610.22940`  `cs.CL`  
  _SungHo Kim, SangKeun Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) generate increasingly fluent text, the need for reliable detection of LLM-generated content has grown substantially. However, existing research remains heavily English-centric, leaving Korean largely underexplored. Moreover, current evaluations of Korean text detection often rely on superficial cues, leading to overly optimistic estimates of detection performance. To address these limitations, we introduce \textsc{KoLing}, a bias-controlled adversarial benchmark comprising 50.9k instances that suppresses superficial shortcuts while preserving semantic content and separately evaluates LLM-generated and LLM-modified text detection. We further propose KoLingProbe, an interpretable detector grounded in theoretical linguistics that models Korean-specific properties across multiple linguistic levels. Experiments show that KoLingProbe substantially outperforms strong baselines on all detection tasks while remaining robust to adversarial attacks. Our analyses further reveal that integrating complementary linguistic features across multiple levels yields both improved performance and interpretability.
  </details>

- **[Bengali Hallucination Detection with Retrieval and Programs: A System Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22892)**  `arXiv:2610.22892`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shamim Hasan, Samiya Ahmed Chowdhury_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bengali hallucination detection must distinguish unsupported factual claims from incorrect reasoning, often without a supplied reference passage. We present and characterize an inference-only system that combines response-conditioned query rewriting, hybrid retrieval, and task-specific language-model judging. Six retrieval routes search question-answer and passage collections; a router directs each example to a 27B textual judge or a 14B agent that can compare retrieved answers, reason directly, or generate and execute Python. We characterize the system using version-specific code, saved predictions, execution traces, and corpus metadata from the Olikbochon challenge. Kaggle associates a displayed private macro-F1 of 0.956 with Version 2; its saved run processes 2,516 examples in 220.99 minutes on two T4 GPUs. Of 253 math-routed examples, 88 terminate through candidate matching, while execution traces expose 49 stderr-bearing run events across 35 examples. A later run changes six predictions despite identical core worker code. A bounded audit of 1,090 benchmark-related corpus records finds exact question or context overlap for 38 processed evaluation inputs, precluding an unqualified claim of corpus-independent generalization. The contribution is a concrete retrieval-and-computation judging architecture with an auditable operational characterization, not a controlled estimate of component gains. We distinguish the reported leaderboard result from independently recomputed diagnostics and identify the evidence needed for stronger effectiveness and reproducibility claims.
  </details>

- **[NLData2Opt: Benchmarking Parameter Derivation and Prediction for Modeling Optimization Problems from Natural Language and Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22889)**  `arXiv:2610.22889`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lijia Liu, Kyohei Atarashi, Jiyi Li, Koh Takeuchi, Shunji Umetani, Hisashi Kashima_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Formulating optimization problems from raw inputs of problem descriptions and data is time-consuming and costly. While Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a promising way to automate this process, existing benchmarks overestimate their capabilities by relying on idealized inputs where all data is relevant and complete. This diverges from reality, where data-centric reasoning is required to extract, compute, and predict parameters from noisy sources. To address this, we introduce NLData2Opt, a benchmark to evaluate LLMs on the multi-stage reasoning inherent in practical optimization workflows. NLData2Opt decomposes problems into problem descriptions and data, and requires models to (1) extract relevant parameters from data containing misleading attributes, (2) compute missing parameters derived by transforming existing data attributes, and (3) predict unavailable future values from historical observations. Evaluation results show substantial performance decline relative to existing benchmarks, indicating that benchmark progress does not directly translate to formulation accuracy in practical optimization workflows. NLData2Opt is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[On the Position Bias of Large Language Models: A Comprehensive Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22807)**  `arXiv:2610.22807`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yining Li, Huayang Li, Lemao Liu, Taro Watanabe_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent studies have shown that position bias remains a critical vulnerability in large language models (LLMs), undermining both the integrity of multiple-choice evaluations and the reliability of retrieval-augmented generation. However, existing understanding of this phenomenon is largely derived from studies with limited model coverage and English-centric benchmarks, leaving the behavior of frontier models and diverse languages underexplored. In this work, we conduct a comprehensive investigation of position bias across model scales ranging from billion-level to trillion-level parameters, covering both multiple-choice and multi-document tasks in five languages. Our experiments reveal that while position bias remains noticeable in small-scale models and Non-English languages, increasing model scale and employing standard Chain-of-Thought prompting significantly mitigate this issue. Moreover, we demonstrate that emerging \emph{thinking models} with extended reasoning capabilities are nearly immune to position bias, suggesting a costly but general approach toward resolving this vulnerability.
  </details>

- **[Multimodal Filesystem Memory For Long-Horizon Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22686)**  `arXiv:2610.22686`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chloe Su, Jiahan Li, Zhenting Qi, Chaoran Cheng, Haoyu Chen, Sejoon Jun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents operating over long horizons need an effective memory that allows them to reuse their experience throughout long interactions. If the entire history of an interaction is retained, it becomes difficult to find the relevant experience, while compressing it into summaries may result in the loss of visual and spatial details that are required later on. We present \textbf{MFM}, a multimodal filesystem memory architecture that keeps complementary types of agent memory. \textbf{Episodic memory} stores the observations, actions, and outcomes, and \textbf{spatial memory} associates experiences with locations. \textbf{Semantic and procedural memory} build up general knowledge and reusable methods of acting, and \textbf{working memory} holds the current plans. MFM makes use of searchable textual records to connect these different representations to the underlying multimodal episodes. The agents are able to find previous experiences by means of contextual cues and to examine the original observations in order to recover details that are missing from their textual descriptions. Through consolidation, the agents convert experience into higher-level knowledge and procedures while still keeping references to the original episodes. In the case of long-horizon Minecraft and computer-use tasks, MFM leads to improved performance with three base models. With GPT-6-Astra, MFM completes \emph{Mine Diamond from Scratch} and reaches 16 out of 24 milestones on the \emph{Ender Dragon} task, as against 12.7 for the best baseline. On CUABench-KiCad it attains a mean reward of 0.440, as compared to 0.400 for the best baseline and 0.267 in the case of no persistent memory.
  </details>

- **[ProPerAgent: Toward Memory-Driven Proactive Assistance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22671)**  `arXiv:2610.22671`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shenglai Zeng, Xiao Yang, Tiffany Ma, Xinyuan Zhang, Nanshu Wang, Deren Lei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As assistants move from reactive answering toward proactive intervention, memory changes role: it no longer only supplies the answer to a query, it determines whether and when to speak at all. Prior work in this setting hands the system the relevant fact in context; we study the regime that makes the problem hard, where the evidence justifying an intervention must be mined from a long-horizon personal memory. We introduce ProPerMem, a benchmark for memory-driven proactive assistance (MPA), pairing egocentric video with year-long, distractor-rich user memories (~618K tokens on average), human-verified trigger windows, and a protocol that scores content and timing jointly. Every baseline we evaluate performs poorly (at most 15%), including frontier models given summarized or retrieval-augmented memory. A controlled study explains why: with oracle evidence delivered inside the trigger window GPT-5.3-Chat reaches 96%; letting it choose the timing drops it to 38%, and replacing oracle evidence with retrieval drops it to 15%. Content and timing are separately necessary, and missing either is catastrophic. Guided by this, we build ProPerAgent (Proactive Personalized Agent) with a plug-and-play harness that surfaces memory from a graph-organized store and gates it down to what is worth interrupting for, plus a lightweight RL stage that curbs early and repeated firing. Without any training, ProPerAgent lifts strict accuracy from below 10% to 37.5% on Qwen3.8-27B and 38% on GPT-5.3-Chat; RL post-training reaches 42.4%.
  </details>

- **[The Model Knows Better: Defending Medical Multi-Agent Systems Against Prompt Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22594)**  `arXiv:2610.22594`  `cs.CL`  
  _Fardin Ahsan Sakib, Ziwei Zhu, Ozlem Uzuner_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical multi-agent systems, in which LLM agents discuss a case, are increasingly studied for clinical decision support. Prompt injection, text that an attacker adds to a model's input, can manipulate LLMs into wrong decisions. How it affects these systems, and how to protect them, remains unclear. Across two systems, two model families and three benchmarks, a single injected passage succeeds in up to 61% of cases, and collaboration does not consistently protect against it. Existing defenses help only partly: they miss injections that look like ordinary clinical text. Yet when asked to check the injected text against the patient's findings, a model that followed the injection usually rejects it. We therefore propose a training-free defense that verifies the clinical judgments behind the system's decision. Following clinical practice, it separates the patient's findings from clinical judgments, such as diagnoses, recommendations and instructions, and checks each judgment against the findings alone, so the decision rests only on supported judgments. Our defense outperforms all baselines on every benchmark, lowering attack success on MedQA to 4-7% while maintaining accuracy on cases without an attack, and remains effective with a medical model and against defense-aware and adaptive attacks.
  </details>

- **[SAG2: Embedding-Free Multi-Hop Retrieval with BM25 and JEV](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22588)**  `arXiv:2610.22588`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuchao Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Strong multi-hop retrieval should be practical to deploy without expensive corpus preparation or complex database infrastructure. We present SAG2, an embedding-free retrieval architecture built around a single SQLite database, BM25, and contextual evidence decisions from JEV. SAG2 follows the query-time association principle of SAG (SQL-Retrieval Augmented Generation), while replacing its event-entity and dense indexes with a lexical index over original passages.
