Selected passages supply terms for further retrieval, and newly retrieved evidence guides subsequent decisions. This moves semantic evidence selection to query time, eliminating offline embedding computation, LLM-based event-entity extraction, and dedicated database servers. The resulting system combines lightweight preparation with strong retrieval accuracy: on 1,000-question subsets of MuSiQue, 2WikiMultiHopQA, and HotpotQA, SAG2 achieves the highest Recall@5 and Recall@10 among the compared methods, with Recall@10 of 86.62%, 97.43%, and 99.00%. It also reaches 81.10% document Recall@10 over the 511,962-document EnterpriseRAG-Bench corpus. Indexing all 11,656 MuSiQue passages takes 2.36 seconds, produces a 25.87 MiB database, and requires no model calls. SAG2 demonstrates that accurate multi-hop evidence retrieval can be supported by compact, rapidly prepared infrastructure, making it a practical foundation for knowledge retrieval applications.
  </details>

- **[Pearl-Chat: Engineering a native Luganda language model from first principles in JAX and Flax NNX](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22529)**  `arXiv:2610.22529`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wesley Kambale, Roland Sankara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive foundation language models predominantly target high-resource Indo-European languages, imposing computational and morphological penalties on under-represented African languages. Generic multilingual tokenizers routinely exceed fertility rates of 3.2 tokens per word on agglutinative Bantu languages, degrading context efficiency and inflating latency. We present Pearl-Chat, an 8,473,122-parameter ($\sim$8.5M) causal decoder-only transformer engineered for Luganda (Oluganda, ISO 639-2: lg), a low-resource Niger-Congo Bantu language spoken by over 10 million people in East Africa. Built from first mathematical principles in JAX and Flax NNX, Pearl-Chat uses explicit functional state decomposition (this http URLandthis http URL), deterministic resumable data streaming via Google Grain, and SPMD tensor parallelism usingthis http URL. We introduce a byte-level byte-pair encoding (BPE) subword tokenizer trained from scratch on curated Luganda corpora ($V = 8,192$), achieving 4.88 characters per token compression and 1.60 tokens per word fertility, a two-fold compression improvement over LLaMA-3 (128K) and Gemma (256K). The model is trained with a two-stage curriculum: Stage 1 pre-training on 59,501 sequences across parallel, biblical, encyclopedic, and journalistic corpora, and Stage 2 instruction fine-tuning on 510 dialogues with assistant-only loss masking. Across 4,000 optimization steps, validation cross-entropy loss drops monotonically from 9.4695 (perplexity: 12,958.98) to 0.1818 (perplexity: 1.20), a decrease of 9.2877 nats. On a three-tier held-out diagnostic benchmark, Pearl-Chat achieves 0.9792 ROUGE-L and 0.9405 BLEU while maintaining concordial agreement across Bantu noun classes. We provide a Python Server-Sent Events (SSE) streaming runtime and open-source all weights, tokenizers, and code.
  </details>

- **[RealCLI: Toward a Unified Evaluation of Human-Agent Collaboration for CLI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22507)**  `arXiv:2610.22507`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yunqi Liu, Peiyu Wang, Bingli Wang, Leo Luo, Xuanwu Song, Xudong Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Command-line interface (CLI) agents increasingly execute longer-horizon tasks, yet existing benchmarks either assume that user instructions remain fixed throughout execution or evaluate task outcomes without capturing runtime collaboration quality. In this paper, we present REALCLI, a paired benchmark that unifies the evaluation of task execution and human-agent collaboration quality on realistic CLI tasks. To comprehensively measure collaboration, REALCLI builds a five-dimensional framework, operationalized into 22 sub-dimensions and 51 interaction scenario templates. REALCLI provides two modes for paired comparison: REALCLI-EXEC supplies complete specifications upfront, whereas REALCLI-COLLAB introduces a scripted user that delivers progressive information, corrections, constraint revisions, and authorization decisions. Evaluations reveal profound capability gaps in frontier agents: the best configuration attains an average score of only 49.2%, and when evaluated by the joint collaboration score ($J_{\mathrm{collab}}$), the peak performance reaches merely 37.6%. These results show that task effectiveness diverges sharply from collaboration quality, with models consistently struggling in steerability, trust calibration, and safety boundary management. REALCLI provides a rigorous, reproducible foundation for measuring and advancing collaborative CLI agents.
  </details>

- **[Anuvani-LID: Robust and Calibrated Spoken Language Identification for Indian Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20263)**  `arXiv:2610.20263`  `cs.CL`  
  _Stephen Fernandes, Narayan Choudhary_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Every multilingual speech pipeline begins with a language decision, an error there propagates silently through recognition, translation and routing. In India it spans 22 scheduled languages across four language families, heard over short, noisy, narrowband and code-mixed audio. Foundational speech models increasingly present language identification (LID) as an inherent latent, generalized capability. Autoregressive decoders in speech models emit a special language ID token, and modern LLMs with audio capabilities can name the language when prompted. We test this assumption on identical clips and find that whisper and similar speech models has tokens for only 15 of the 23 languages, on one second inputs Gemini 3.5 Flash reaches 0.52 macro-F1 and GPT-audio 0.14. We present Anuvani LID, a dedicated 115M parameter LID conformer model trained on 54,643 hours of Indian speech spanning 22 Indian languages and Indian accented English. Along with designed and controlled studies on 102 condition benchmark with speaker and corpus held out tests. Across 37 conditions, it reaches 0.914 mean macro-F1, 5.2 points above Gemini 3.5 Flash and ahead of 14 other systems. It is 24 points ahead of Gemini at one second, scores 0.897 on all 23 languages where no other system exceeds 0.713, and is calibrated to an ECE of 0.008. Reliable Indian LID still requires a robust, dedicated and calibrated model.
  </details>

- **[EdgeWise: Bringing Long-Horizon Agents to the Edge via Selective Cloud Delegation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17560)**  `arXiv:2610.17560`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zeru Shi, Jindong Jiang, Ryo Hachiuma, Yonggan Fu, Byung-Kwan Lee, Peter Belcak, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Complex and long-horizon agent tasks often rely on frontier models deployed in the cloud to achieve strong performance. However, using these models for every step of a long-horizon trajectory incurs substantial inference cost, even though not all steps may require the same level of model capability. In this paper, we find that allocating the LLM to only a small subset of key steps can achieve performance comparable to full LLM execution while significantly reducing inference cost. Motivated by this observation, we propose EdgeWise, an edge-cloud collaboration framework that deploys an SLM on the local device as the primary solver and selectively delegates critical steps to a cloud LLM when stronger model capacity is needed. To enable autonomous delegation without additional models at inference time, we introduce a step-level RL method that jointly optimizes task solving and delegation, with fine-grained credit assignment for individual delegation decisions. We also introduce an external judge-based delegation strategy and use it to systematically investigate key design choices in edge-cloud collaboration, including when to delegate, how delegation decisions should be made, what context should be shared, and how frequently the cloud LLM should be invoked. Experiments show that EdgeWise matches or outperforms pure-LLM execution while reducing inference cost up to 62% on SWE-Rebench and 34% on Terminal-Bench 2.1. We further deploy EdgeWise on a local workstation, demonstrating comparable cost savings in a practical edge-cloud setting.
  </details>

- **[ConceptGuard: Benchmarking Context-Sensitive Unlearning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20338)**  `arXiv:2608.20338`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sahil Kale, Ian Harris_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) often require selective removal of harmful or sensitive knowledge, called unlearning, yet existing benchmarks evaluate this capability incompletely. They rely on disjoint forget and retain sets of independent facts, and measure success by simple factual recall. This misses a key requirement of unlearning from a safety perspective, namely eliminating harmful behaviours while preserving benign and beneficial knowledge. We argue that effective unlearning must operate at the level of concepts, removing unsafe applications while maintaining correct and useful usage. To evaluate this, we operationalize dual-use concepts, i.e., concepts that can be used in both harmful and benign contexts, and construct ConceptGuard, a benchmark of 2,583 harmful--benign pairs over 68 concepts, where forget and retain sets are explicitly paired in concept usage. Evaluation is intent-sensitive and centres on contextual separation, which measures how well a model suppresses harmful behaviour while preserving benign usage of the same concept. Across four unlearning methods and four models, we find that current techniques reduce harmful memorization but show strong forgetting--utility trade-offs and limited contextual separation, well below a retain-only reference. Separation trends also depend more on the concept and model than on the method. Our results point to the need for unlearning approaches that better align with real-world safety requirements. Our dataset is publicly available.
  </details>

- **[FrenchNews-7: Benchmarking Cross-Publisher French News Editorial Desk Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18097)**  `arXiv:2608.18097`  `cs.CL`  
  _Amr Sobhy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present FrenchNews-7, a cross-publisher France-based French-language news editorial desk classification benchmark combining a large multi-outlet corpus, a URL-derived seven-class taxonomy, and a fine-tuned CamemBERT classifier. Labels are assigned via a hybrid pipeline combining publisher URL slugs with LLM annotation for structurally ambiguous cases, audited through an inter-rater study (2 humans + 2 LLMs; pairwise $\kappa \geq 0.766$, human--human $\kappa = 0.806$). We evaluate lexical, multilingual, and French-specific trained classifiers under both in-distribution and held-out-publisher settings, with additional comparison against zero-shot LLM baselines (GPT-OSS-120B, Mistral Small 3.2, Llama-3.3-70B) on the held-out pool. The strongest model, CamemBERT-base on full article text, outperforms headline-only input, generalizes to unseen outlets, and exceeds all three zero-shot LLM baselines on overall recall (0.799), with the gap concentrated in the ambiguous editorial-boundary categories Economie and Societe. Cross-publisher evaluation reveals uneven boundary stability: Sport, Culture & Loisirs, and International transfer cleanly, while Economie (recall = 0.517) is statistically indistinguishable from blinded human agreement on the same articles (0.55, $n = 40$), and Societe (precision = 0.577) absorbs boundary ambiguity, both suggesting editorial conventions rather than recoverable classifier headroom. The fine-tuned CamemBERT-base model, labeled manifest, reference collection scripts, and a reliability-tier guidance table are available atthis https URL(model) andthis https URL(dataset).
  </details>

- **[Paved with True Intents: Intent-Aware Training Improves LLM Safety Classification Across Training Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27210)**  `arXiv:2606.27210`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jeremias Ferrao, Niclas Müller-Hof, Iustin Sîrbu, Traian Rebedea, Yftah Ziser_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We argue that safety classifiers should model user intent as an explicit signal between the prompt and the final label. To study this, we introduce AIMS, a human-annotated dataset of 1,724 difficult safety prompts, each paired with an intent description and harm label. We use AIMS to evaluate intent-aware training across supervised fine-tuning, preference learning, reasoning distillation, and reinforcement learning. Despite its size, AIMS enables competitive safety classifiers across training regimes: DPO from model-generated intent errors improves over SFT, and intent-conditioned distillation outperforms reasoning-only distillation in most teacher-student pairs. Most notably, directly rewarding intent faithfulness with GRPO yields the strongest average performance across five external safety benchmarks, while our intent-aware models form the inference latency-F1 Pareto frontier. These results show that faithful intent modeling is a compact, high-quality supervision signal for more robust safety classifiers.
  </details>

- **[PragReST: Self-Reinforcing Counterfactual Reasoning for Pragmatic Language Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18624)**  `arXiv:2606.18624`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jihyung Park, Minchao Huang, Leqi Liu, Elias Stengel-Eskin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural language understanding often depends on meanings that are implied rather than explicitly stated, requiring pragmatic reasoning. Despite strong performance on math and logical reasoning, large language models (LLMs) still struggle with making pragmatic inferences, often choosing literal interpretations. To improve LLM pragmatic reasoning, we introduce PragReST, a self-supervised framework that constructs pragmatic QA data, generates counterfactual reasoning traces, and trains models to internalize them through supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, without human-labeled training data or distillation from a stronger teacher. Across four pragmatic benchmarks (PragMega, Ludwig, MetoQA, and AltPrag), PragReST improves over backbone models, task-specific pragmatic tuning baselines, and non-counterfactual variants of the same pipeline. On accuracy-based benchmarks, PragReST improves over the instruct backbone by 5.37 and 5.50% (absolute) for Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-14B, respectively. Our error analysis and ablations underscore the importance of counterfactual reasoning: PragReST primarily reduces errors caused by failures to contrast observed utterances with plausible alternatives, and removing counterfactual reasoning substantially reduces performance. Moreover, our training preserves out-of-domain performance on general-knowledge and mathematical reasoning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[LLMs Think with DAGs: Probing the Latent Graph Structure of Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17593)**  `arXiv:2601.17593`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tianjun Zhong, Linyang He, Ziyang Li, Nima Mesgarani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models often externalize reasoning as linear chains, but many reasoning problems can be more naturally modeled as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs), where intermediate conclusions branch, merge, and are reused. Whether such graph structure is reflected in model internals remains unclear. We introduce Reasoning DAG Probing to answer this question. We formalize each premise, intermediate conclusion, and final answer as a DAG node, and train lightweight probes to predict node depth, pairwise distance, and adjacency from LLM hidden states. Across logical, mathematical, and code reasoning benchmarks, we find that DAG structure is meaningfully encoded in LLM representations: it peaks in intermediate layers, with later layers preferentially recover deeper nodes and longer-range reasoning dependencies, and becomes stronger with model scale. DAG structure also progressively emerges during autoregressive generation in ways that anticipate answer correctness, while steering activations toward the ground-truth DAG structure can convert some originally incorrect generations into correct ones. These results suggest that LLM reasoning is not purely sequential, but reflects measurable latent graph structure.
  </details>

- **[Automatic Tensor-level Execution of Abstract Interpreters for Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20055)**  `arXiv:2507.20055`  `cs.CL`  
  _Avaljot Singh, Yasmin Chandini Sarita, Aditya Mishra, Gagandeep Singh, Charith Mendis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Developing new abstract interpretation-based DNN certifiers is difficult because their mathematical design is expressed at the neuron level, while implementations must operate at the tensor level. Existing libraries such as auto_LiRPA, IVAN, and ERAN support fixed sets of certifiers; extending them requires expertise in formal methods and tensor programming. Domain-specific languages such as ConstraintFlow let researchers specify certifiers at the neuron level, but cannot run them on real DNNs.We present Neuton, a framework that automatically translates neuron-level certifier specifications into tensor-level executables, enabling new DNN certifiers without manual tensor programming. Neuton makes three contributions. First, a stack-based intermediate representation (IR) and shape analysis infer implicit tensor operations, including broadcasts, dimension expansions, and reductions, required for correct execution. Second, domain-specific rewrite rules optimize common tensor operation sequences into more efficient equivalents. Third, gBCSR is a sparse tensor format using variable-sized, layer-aligned blocks over a non-zero default value, tailored to DNN certifier sparsity.We prove that translation preserves neuron-level semantics. We evaluate Neuton on 8 certifiers and 32 DNN benchmarks. Neuton compiles four existing certifiers and produces the first executable implementations of four others: two previously specified but never run, and two new designs. These expose precision-runtime trade-offs unavailable in existing libraries. For example, SkipPoly matches DeepPoly's precision on all 32 DNNs while running as fast or faster on half. Neuton's runtime on existing certifiers is competitive with hand-optimized libraries. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Optimizing Singular Spectrum for Large Language Model Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.15092)**  `arXiv:2502.15092`  `cs.CL`  
  _Dengjie Li, Tiancheng Shen, Yao Zhou, Bernard Ghanem, Yibo Yang, Ming-Hsuan Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities, yet prohibitive parameter complexity often hinders their deployment. Existing singular value decomposition (SVD) based compression methods simply deem singular values as importance scores of decomposed components. However, this importance ordered by singular values does not necessarily correlate with the performance of a downstream task. In this work, we introduce SoCo (Singular spectrum optimization for large language model Compression), a novel compression framework that learns to rescale the decomposed components of SVD in a data-driven manner. Concretely, we employ a learnable diagonal matrix to assign importance scores for singular spectrum and develop a three-stage training process that progressively refines these scores from initial coarse compression to fine-grained sparsification-thereby striking an effective balance between aggressive model compression and performance preservation. Thanks to the learnable singular spectrum, SoCo adaptively prunes components according to the sparsified importance scores, rather than relying on the fixed order of singular values. More importantly, the remaining components with amplified importance scores can compensate for the loss of the pruned ones. Experimental evaluations across multiple LLMs and benchmarks demonstrate that SoCo surpasses the state-of-the-art methods in model compression.
  </details>

- **[NEXUS: Learning Safety Constraints and Action Models for Safe and Robust Embodied Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09387)**  `arXiv:2605.09387`  `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Tiehan Cui, Peipei Liu, Yanxu Mao, Gaosheng Wang, Mingzhe Xing, Datao You_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) enable embodied agents to plan from naturallanguage instructions, yet their actions may be physically infeasible or hazardous. Existing neuro-symbolic learners primarily refine execution models, while formal safety methods acquire or enforce safety constraints. This separation limits the reuse of both execution corrections and safety knowledge across tasks. We present NEXUS, a modular neuro-symbolic framework that jointly maintains a PDDL domain model and an LTL safety specification. Execution failures are used to repair action schemas, whereas detected hazards are converted into reusable LTL constraints through hierarchical semantic and translation sampling with equivalence consensus. Accumulated constraints are enforced through LTL progression during symbolic search; comparing constrained and unconstrained search outcomes distinguishes physical unreachability from safety-induced refusal. Experiments on SafeAgentBench evaluation set show that NEXUS achieves the best overall trade-off between safetask completion and unsafe-task refusal, while maintaining low violation rates under jailbreak attacks. Ablations confirm the complementary benefits of PDDLdomain refinement and LTL-constraint accumulation.
  </details>

- **[Detecting Multi-Agent Collusion Through Multi-Agent Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01151)**  `arXiv:2604.01151`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Aaron Rose, Carissa Cullen, Sahar Abdelnabi, Philip Torr, Brandon Gary Kaplowitz, Christian Schroeder de Witt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Non-verbalised or secret collusion may impose a ceiling on transcript-level monitoring in multi-agent deployments. Internal model activations are known to provide an additional monitoring surface for single-agent phenomena such as deception, but detecting collusion within an unknown subgroup is ultimately a joint inference task over multiple agents. This paper derives collusion detection statistics based on the composite hypothesis testing literature and introduces HELIOS, a multi-agent detection framework for collusion. Using per-agent activation probes as sensors, we then evaluate these principled statistics alongside heuristics on NARCBench, a novel benchmark for evaluating multi-agent collusion detection under environment distribution shift. Across four open-weight models, both our group-level statistics and heuristics transfer zero-shot to NARCBenchs holdout environments. Notably, the best method on every model reaches 0.99 to 1.00 AUROC when transferred from committee deliberations to a steganographic collusion task that unprimed transcript monitors fail to separate. We find empirically that no single statistic or heuristic dominates across tasks, consistent with our analysis that there is no known universally most powerful test for this setting. We then evaluate detector transfer to third-party environments with emergent, rather than instructed, collusion. In one such environment, only 2 of 229 collusive episodes verbalise the coordination in the transcript. We find evidence of partial transfer from NARCBench to these environments, indicating that probes fitted to instructed collusion may not fully capture emergent collusion signals. Overall, we establish multi-agent interpretability as a complementary monitoring technique, while highlighting the need to evaluate detector performance on both emergent and instructed collusion risks.
  </details>

- **[Characterising and Analysing Retrieval-Augmented Generation Techniques](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23226)**  `arXiv:2610.23226`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Emmanuel Igbekele, Olawande Daramola_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The advent of Large Language Models (LLMs) across many application domains has increased the need for Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to reduce hallucinations and improve LLM performance. Thus far, several RAG techniques with impressive features and capabilities have been proposed in the literature, making it a herculean task to select the most appropriate RAG techniques for specific problem scenarios. Thus, we propose a conceptual framework comprising main dimensions and sub-dimensions for characterising (defining "what it is") and analysing (defining "how well it does") existing RAG techniques. The conceptual framework was developed through a qualitative analysis of the attributes of RAG techniques reported in the literature. It can serve as a pre-empirical filtering mechanism for researchers and practitioners in selecting the highest-fit RAG candidates that meet specific user and task requirements before empirical benchmarking of RAG techniques, which can be computationally and financially expensive.
  </details>

- **[Null Controls Are Necessary but Not Sufficient: Evaluating Acceptance Guards for Scientific Discovery and Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22445)**  `arXiv:2610.22445`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Tianyu Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated discovery systems propose scientific explanations and test which to accept. Does passing a test establish the intended claim? We develop four executable operations for changing physical descriptions: identifying redundancies (L1), coarse-graining dynamics (L2), transferring structure (L3), and inferring missing quantities from conservation imbalances (L4). We evaluate these operations and three interpretability settings through null acceptance (NAR), positive acceptance (PAR), and consequence agreement (CAR).A reduction mapping every state to zero predicts its own future perfectly. It passes closure on 96% of 129 public chaotic systems despite retaining no dynamical variation. An independent observable-preservation test rejects it throughout. On controlled benchmarks, the combined criteria accept 24/24 genuine reductions and reject 72/72 nulls. The other operations distinguish symmetries from redundancies, validate transfers, and reject spurious conservation imbalances. In language and ESM-2 protein models, linear CKA remains unchanged when a proposed correspondence is randomly rotated, whereas localized interventions distinguish genuine mappings from tested nulls. In sparse autoencoders, enrichment rejects all 16 tested nulls, yet independent ablation supports 45% of evaluable enriched features, compared with 0.5% of matched controls.These results reveal two acceptance gaps: descriptive tests can accept scientifically empty candidates, and accepted candidates can lack independently verified consequences. Reliable automated discovery requires evaluating both what a test accepts and the scientific consequences it establishes. Code and data:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Prompt Optimization of Large Language Models With Chess](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00416)**  `arXiv:2610.00416`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Timothée Lesort, Alejandra López de Aberasturi Gómez, Tristan Karch, Tom Veniat, Philippe Modard, Karl Tuyls, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating large language models becomes increasingly challenging as their capabilities advance: benchmarks can saturate, public test sets risk contamination, and assessing harder tasks can require expensive grading or execution infrastructure. These challenges are amplified in automatic prompt optimization (APO), where evaluation is repeated throughout the search for better prompts. Studying APO therefore requires a benchmark that is cheap and deterministic to score, hard enough to leave room for improvement, and renewable as models evolve. We introduce a chess benchmark built from 1,118 Lichess puzzles to study APO for frozen LLMs: we optimize their prompts without updating their model weights. Chess combines inexpensive exact-match scoring, engine-based evaluation of alternative moves, and a renewable supply of problems with adjustable difficulty. Unlike evaluations that report only success on isolated test items, the benchmark also connects puzzle-solving gains to short game-play rollouts within the same domain. We use it to evaluate six APO algorithms on eight target models, measuring not only baseline strength but also how much each model responds to optimization and whether optimized prompts transfer across models and to game play. Chess is thus a well-suited benchmark for APO: it is (i) challenging, as even the strongest evaluated model, Gemini 3.5 Flash (used as the meta-model), solves only about 55\% of puzzles; (ii) discriminative, revealing gains, unchanged performance, and regressions across methods and models; (iii) renewable, with fresh puzzles to reduce contamination risk and adjustable difficulty to maintain headroom as models improve; and (iv) affordable, as the complete study runs for around \$800. We release the puzzles, optimization and evaluation code, and dataset-renewal scripts (this https URL).
  </details>

- **[Towards Unified World Models for Visual Navigation via Memory-Augmented Planning and Foresight](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.08713)**  `arXiv:2510.08713`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Yifei Dong, Fengyi Wu, Guangyu Chen, Lingdong Kong, Qiyu Hu, Yuxuan Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enabling embodied agents to imagine future states is essential for robust and generalizable visual navigation. Yet, state-of-the-art systems typically rely on modular designs that decouple navigation planning from visual world modeling, which often induces state-action misalignment and weak adaptability in novel or dynamic scenarios. We propose UniWM, a unified, memory-augmented world model that integrates egocentric visual foresight and planning within a single multimodal autoregressive backbone. UniWM explicitly grounds action selection in visually imagined outcomes, tightly aligning prediction with control. Meanwhile, a hierarchical memory mechanism fuses short-term perceptual cues with longer-term trajectory context, supporting stable and coherent reasoning over extended horizons. Extensive experiments on four challenging benchmarks (Go Stanford, ReCon, SCAND, HuRoN) and the 1X Humanoid Dataset show that UniWM improves navigation success rates by up to 30%, substantially reduces trajectory errors against strong baselines, generalizes zero-shot to the unseen TartanDrive dataset, and scales naturally to high-dimensional humanoid navigation. These results position UniWM as a principled step toward unified, imagination-driven embodied navigation. The code and models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ECHOES: Seeing Beyond the Current Image via Recursive History Compression and Privileged On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22408)**  `arXiv:2610.22408`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Tengda Guo, Jiawang Hu, Yifei Tao, Yin Wang, Haotian Hu, Fanyi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon GUI agents rely on visual history to maintain task states, but retaining historical screenshots increases visual-token usage and inference costs, while textual summaries can discard critical state information, undermining long-horizon reliability. We propose ECHOES, which leverages training-time privileged information to guide textual history compression and strengthen long-horizon reasoning. Specifically, ECHOES adopts a Compression-Think-Action (CTA) format to recursively update a textual history state and reason. The same model, given privileged multi-image inputs, serves as a teacher that provides dense token-level on-policy distillation on student-generated CTA rollouts. Our Perception-Memory Fidelity (PMF) Gap-guided OPD-RL framework dynamically balances privileged distillation and task-reward optimization, with periodic teacher updates supporting continued improvement. We further introduce ECHOESBMK, a fine-grained diagnostic benchmark derived from real GUI agent failure trajectories to jointly evaluate visual perception and cross-modal state retention. Experiments on four GUI benchmarks and ECHOESBMK demonstrate substantial gains in task performance and memory fidelity. Without retaining historical screenshots at deployment, ECHOES achieves competitive performance with a favorable performance-inference-cost trade-off and exhibits positive zero-shot transfer to unseen visual planning tasks.
  </details>

- **[MultiWorldBench: Do Independently Controlled Views Describe One Shared World?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11723)**  `arXiv:2610.11723`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhangbo Xu, Ruoxi Zhang, Rui Hu, Yisong Wang, Xitong Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multiplayer world models must ensure that independently controlled views remain consistent with a single shared and persistent world. We introduce MultiWorldBench, a diagnostic Minecraft benchmark comprising 495 case configurations across seven task suites and ten capabilities, including independent control, cross-view motion, shared-state synchronization, persistence, structural reasoning, concurrent interaction, and delayed revisit. Using Engine GT as a reference, we evaluate two multiplayer world models, Solaris (MP) and Gamma-World, together with a single-player generation variant, Solaris (SP). Among the generated systems, Gamma-World achieves the highest ten-capability average at 21.39, followed by Solaris (MP) at 20.88 and Solaris (SP) at 10.06, while Engine GT reaches 91.69. Gamma-World performs better on several control, shared-state, and revisit capabilities, whereas Solaris (MP) leads in cross-view motion and race-condition consistency. Nevertheless, all generated systems score at most 8.00 on state persistence and 1.33 on structural consistency, and none succeeds in spatial reasoning or building-identity preservation. Human pairwise evaluation yields the same overall system ranking and shows strong alignment with the automatic evaluation, with a mean dimension-level Spearman correlation of 0.95. These results show that current multiplayer world models still exhibit a substantial gap relative to Engine GT in maintaining a coherent shared world.
  </details>

- **[Audit-First VAPO: Risk-Certified Selective Updates under Imperfect Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33662)**  `arXiv:2609.33662`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Miaobo Hu, Shuhao Hu, Xiaobo Guo, Xin Wang, Bokun Wang, Rui Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Imperfect verifiers can assign a harmful update direction even when clipping and regularization bound its magnitude. We introduce Audit-First VAPO, which separates discrete directional admission from continuous magnitude control. An observation-only accept-appeal-abstain policy uses a finite secondary-verification budget; its action trace is frozen before clean labels are joined. Simultaneous finite-sample bounds then certify selected harmful risk, coverage, and verifier-call rate over a predeclared policy family. Conditional Hoeffding-Azuma bounds account for the dependence induced by shared budgets, and rollout or verifier changes initiate a new certification stage. After admission, a bounded trust-clip-KL actuator controls magnitude. We evaluate two models on two reasoning benchmarks against static RLVR, matched-random selection, confidence thresholding, noise correction, and verifier augmentation. On Qwen3.5-0.8B and GSM8K at target risk $\rho=0.08$, RC-VAPO achieves 74.1% accuracy, selected harmful risk 0.0697, coverage 0.4125, and relative verifier cost $1.16\times$. At matched coverage and update magnitude, its selected-risk difference from matched random is -0.0260 with paired 95% interval $[-0.0364,-0.0157]$. Across asymmetric, confidence-dependent, and correlated-verifier noise, the certificate is satisfied on 57 of 60 independent runs. These comparisons isolate informative directional selection from proposal suppression, update shrinkage, and additional verifier computation.
  </details>

- **[AI-Driven Reproducibility and Analytical Robustness in Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23845)**  `arXiv:2610.23845`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Shicheng Liu, Krista Opsahl-Ong, Andrew Drozdov, Julian Killingback, Arnav Singhvi, Ronak Pradeep, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reproducible and analytically robust research findings are essential to scientific progress, but evaluating these properties typically requires costly manual effort. We investigate whether LLM agents can help automate reproduction and robustness analysis, using a medical case study grounded in the Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER) database, a widely used U.S. cancer registry underlying tens of thousands of published studies. First, we introduce SEER-Repro, a benchmark of 20 published SEER-based papers together with the data and resources required to reproduce their findings. Second, we create an AI agent that successfully reproduces 77.4% of 2,190 atomic claims, while providing executable baselines for further analysis. Third, we develop an agentic analysis pipeline to test the robustness of the medical findings by identifying analytical choices underlying each finding, proposing defensible alternatives, and rerunning the analysis to determine whether those choices affect the conclusion. Across published papers in SEER-Repro, our pipeline identifies potentially consequential analytical choices in 9.5% of fully reproduced findings and flags them for expert review. These results show how LLM agents can support scientific review by reproducing findings, systematically testing their robustness, and directing expert attention toward potentially consequential analytical choices.
  </details>

- **[ScoutRoute: Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Routing Using Internal Language Model Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23443)**  `arXiv:2610.23443`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Rajdeep Pandhare, Delzaan Bharucha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efficient expert routing is a fundamental challenge in Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) systems, particularly for heterogeneous collections of independently trained Large Language Mod els (LLMs). Existing routing approaches typically rely on jointly trained gating networks, prompt-based predictors, or computationally expensive multi-model inference, limiting their flexibility and efficiency. This paper presents ScoutRoute, a lightweight post-hoc routing framework that predicts expert competence using internal language model signals extracted from a single forward pass through an inexpensive scout model. Hidden-state representations are used to estimate cross-model competence through lightweight logistic probes, while logit-derived confidence measures are incorporated within a domain-aware routing strategy for efficient expert selec tion without modifying the underlying language models. ScoutRoute isevaluatedon fiveopen-weightlanguagemodels across fourbenchmark datasets covering mathematical reasoning, code generation, commonsense reasoning, and multidis ciplinary knowledge. Experimental results show that ScoutRoute consistently outperforms individual routing signals while requiring only a single scout-model inference. The proposed competence probes also achieve superior calibration compared with conventional confidence measures, demonstrating that internal language model representations provide transferable information for accurate and efficient expert routing across heterogeneous language models
  </details>

- **[Sep-Latent: Separator Tokens as Natural Compression Anchors for Implicit Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22924)**  `arXiv:2610.22924`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yinlong Xu, Jianfeng Huang, Zhengdong Zhou, Mingjie Lin, Fuyu Lv, Dan Ou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable success in complex reasoning through explicit Chain-of-Thought (CoT), but the associated computational overhead and token verbosity remain significant barriers to efficiency. While implicit reasoning in continuous latent spaces offers a promising alternative, existing methods like Coconut and CODI often rely on fixed-length latent sequences and suffer from representational collapse. In this paper, we propose Sep-Latent, an adaptive implicit reasoning framework that utilizes separator tokens as structural semantic anchors. By introducing an Attention-Decay mechanism, we force the model to aggregate and compress reasoning logic into these anchors during training. We then align explicit and implicit pathways through multi-level distillation across all layers to ensure logical consistency. Crucially, Sep-Latent enables dynamic reasoning depth by adaptively terminating the latent thinking phase based on query complexity. Experimental results on the GSM8k-Aug benchmark show that Sep-Latent achieves an accuracy of 56.8\% with an average of only 5.4 tokens, significantly outperforming prior fixed-length implicit models in efficiency while narrowing the performance gap with explicit CoT. Further analysis via PCA and symbolic variant testing confirms that our method ensures semantic grounding and exhibits superior robustness to perturbations.
  </details>

- **[GlobeBench: Evaluating Text-Based World Model Capabilities for Scalable Environment Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22708)**  `arXiv:2610.22708`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Nicholas Saban, Charvi Bannur, Mariya Vasileva, Darshan Deshpande_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-based world models have recently shown potential to simulate complex RL environment state transitions for a diverse set of use cases. However, no standardized benchmark exists to compare, study and improve these models. Through this paper, we attempt to solve this problem by building GlobeBench, a novel benchmark with 1,632 tasks across five separate domains and nineteen simulation skills. The tasks check whether models can predict the environment's response to a specific action given the full agent trajectory by understanding earlier state changes, following environment rules, and predicting the effects of actions on both related and unrelated objects. We compare the predicted environment response with an execution-derived reference, using a hybrid architecture with both deterministic gates and a semantic judge. Among 20 general-purpose LLMs and specialized text world models, GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest task success rate at 49.57%. We find that model accuracy decreases as actions affect a larger portion of the environment. Specifically, when the agent's changes must propagate to dependent objects, task success drops to an average of 5.1% across all 20 models. Although model strengths differ across tasks, 631 tasks remain unsolved by any model, suggesting a significant gap in general LLM capabilities as a world model.
  </details>

- **[Not All Graphs Are Maps: A Task-Agnostic Exploration Framework for Mobile GUI Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22672)**  `arXiv:2610.22672`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Adamo Orsini, Seyed Mehdi Ayyoubzadeh, Sadman Rakib Pinon, Hannuo Zhang, Koosha Gholipour Baradari, Yuanhao Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern mobile GUI agents can perceive visible screens and execute local actions, but they still lack persistent app-specific navigation knowledge: where functions live, which pages contain them, and which screens are equivalent across repeated visits. We introduce TAG-Nav, a Task-Agnostic Graph Navigation framework that wraps existing GUI-agent backbones with reusable destination memory. Offline exploration is distilled into compact records of app functions, target pages, and waypoint routes before any evaluated instruction is seen. When a future task's destination is covered, TAG-Nav retrieves the destination and provides route guidance without task-specific demonstrations. This separates mobile GUI destination navigation from full task completion: many tasks share the prefix of reaching the right page, while final actions may enter content, send data, make purchases, delete information, or change sensitive settings. To evaluate route finding without downstream execution confounds, we introduce DestBench, a navigation-only protocol that asks whether an agent reaches the correct pre-action destination screen. DestBench enables a controlled graph-memory study comparing no-memory agents with random, automated BFS, and clean graph memory. Graph-derived memory improves strict navigation success rate of UI-TARS-1.5-7B from 52.4% without graph memory to 58.3% with automated BFS memory, and improves Qwen-VL-Max success rate from 59.5% to 71.4% on the same conditions. Together, TAG-Nav and DestBench show that destination coverage and route cleanliness strongly shape graph-memory value, making graph quality a measured variable rather than an assumed benefit of exploration.
  </details>

- **[Repairing LLM-Generated Specifications with Deterministic Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22568)**  `arXiv:2610.22568`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Giovanni Vaccarino, Emanuele La Malfa, Dehai Min, Hanrong Zhang, Pier Luca Lanzi, Lu Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as formalizers: given a reasoning problem expressed in natural language, they produce a formal specification that a symbolic solver can then solve, with guarantees relative to that specification. Many specifications are runnable yet semantically wrong, encoding incorrect constraints, domains, objectives, predicates, or goals, and solver-error feedback dries up once execution succeeds. Purely LLM-based diagnosis, lacking a formal ground truth, often fails to spot these errors, and its ungrounded critiques cause regressions in 11.8% of initially correct specifications in our experiments. We propose DRAGO, a repair framework that replaces this ungrounded critique with deterministic, formalism-specific diagnosis, and retains a rewrite only when the diagnosed error weight strictly decreases under a reference-free acceptance predicate. Across five benchmarks spanning logical reasoning (SMT-LIB2), constraint programming (MiniZinc), and planning (PDDL), DRAGO cuts regressions by nearly an order of magnitude, to 1.3%, while improving answer accuracy by 12.9 percentage points over zero-shot formalization and 8.7 percentage points over LLM-diagnostic repair, averaged across benchmarks. We also uncover two rendering defects in Planetarium that cause 50% (3,086 instances) of its floor-tile split to be inconsistent with the reference formalizations, and release a corrected split.
  </details>

- **[Are Large Reasoning Models Good Everyday Reasoners? A Comparison with Non-Reasoning Models on Logical Reasoning Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22538)**  `arXiv:2610.22538`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Kentaro Ozeki, Risako Ando, Takanobu Morishita, Hirohiko Abe, Koji Mineshima, Mitsuhiro Okada_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large reasoning models (LRMs) that leverage test-time computation achieve strong performance on mathematical and scientific benchmarks and are increasingly deployed in real-world applications. However, their ability to perform \textit{everyday logical reasoning}, which often departs from textbook formal logic, remains underexplored. In this work, we systematically evaluate how well reasoning models align with everyday reasoning patterns by introducing a benchmark based on linguistic and pragmatic reasoning phenomena. We also compare reasoning models with non-reasoning models under controlled prompting conditions. We find that, in many cases, (1) reasoning models show degraded performance in everyday reasoning when such reasoning deviates from formal logical rules, (2) some non-reasoning models with chain-of-thought prompting outperform comparable reasoning models in everyday reasoning, and (3) prompting with additional instructions provides limited control over reasoning behavior. These findings suggest that current reasoning models do not reliably capture everyday reasoning and point to limitations in post-training, motivating improved training objectives and evaluation across diverse forms of reasoning.
  </details>

- **[STAIF: A Stage-wise Optimization for Complex Instruction Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22649)**  `arXiv:2607.22649`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jian Hong, Chen Cheng, Quan Liu, Yuhao Chen, Enhong Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Following complex instructions with multiple explicit constraints remains a fundamental challenge for large language models (LLMs). Existing alignment methods, such as DPO, optimize holistic reward signals that often underemphasize strict satisfaction of individual constraints, particularly under out-of-distribution or multi-constraint settings. In this paper, we propose STAIF, a stage-wise optimization framework that decouples the alignment of subjective (soft) constraints from the optimization of objectively verifiable (hard) constraints. Stage 1 applies preference optimization with multiple negative samples to sharpen sensitivity to soft constraints, while Stage 2 applies Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) to enforce strict compliance with hard constraints. To support this method, we construct STAINSTRUCT, a high-quality bilingual (English, Chinese) dataset of approximately 31,000 complex multi-constraint instructions. Extensive analyses validate the design of STAIF and show state-of-the-art performance on representative benchmarks against strong baselines, as well as genuine generalization.
  </details>

- **[DrugHarness: An Evolvable Agent Harness for Computational Drug Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23791)**  `arXiv:2610.23791`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuxing Chen, Zhisheng Zhang, Cheng Tang, Wei Li, Minghui Zhang, Ziyan Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computational drug discovery requires agents to compose scientific operations and adapt to changing evidence and constraints. Here we introduce DrugHarness, an evolvable agent harness coupling composable scientific execution with conditional skill induction. Its environment integrates over 80 scientific operations, biomedical resources and task-specific code, preserving associations among candidates, results and artifacts. From execution trajectories and outcome feedback, DrugHarness induces action hypotheses with observable applicability conditions, recommended actions and supporting evidence. An independent review agent evaluates candidate skills derived from these hypotheses through source verification and, when needed, targeted execution trials. Admitted skills guide subsequent tasks and are revised through feedback. Evaluation on MolBench and SMDD-Bench demonstrates capabilities in molecular screening, optimization and constrained design. Without evolution, DrugHarness exceeds reported MolClaw-CC results by 10.8 percentage points in affinity-comparison accuracy and 55% in mean top-three active hits on MolBench. Retrospective evaluation on skill-development tasks shows that evolution increases Qwen's affinity-comparison accuracy from 62.2% to 81.1% and optimization success from 79.5% to 87.2%. In a prospective HPK1 study, DrugHarness achieves a more favorable median docking score than Biomni among system-specific candidates. A synthesized compound achieves a cellular IC50 of 14.19 nM, a 2.64-fold potency improvement over the best input reference, with activity competitive with two selected compounds designed by experienced medicinal chemists. These results support conditional skill evolution for scientific agents and demonstrate a workflow connecting computational drug design with experimental validation.
  </details>

- **[OwlSight: A Panoramic Monitor for Chain-of-Thought Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23670)**  `arXiv:2610.23670`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lewen Yan, Yanxu Zhu, Jie Zhang, Jing Shao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-Thought (CoT) monitoring offers actionable evidence for oversight AI systems, with the potential to enable early detection and intervention. However, existing approaches are focused on specific tasks, whereas the regulation of AI, particularly reasoning models, requires monitoring a broader range of tasks. We introduce Factor for CoT monitoring, a strategy that supports customizing monitoring requirements in the prompt, which enables a single monitor to cover different tasks by relying on different factors. Furthermore, we introduce two types of factors, fine-grained and coarse-grained (e.g., monitoring whether a specific email address has been leaked versus monitoring privacy leakage), to accommodate diverse user monitoring needs. Based on this, we develop a CoT monitor named OwlSight that relies solely on factor to monitor behaviors related to multiple dimensions, including capability, safety, and trustworthiness. Experiments across 15,971 reasoning trajectories show that OwlSight surpasses general-purpose LLMs by 1.32-30.57 and 11.71-27.81 points at fine and coarse granularity, respectively, and achieves state-of-the-art results on OpenAI's CoT monitorability benchmark. It also generalizes to unseen models' CoTs and remains robust to Factor rephrasings, CoT compression, and obfuscation, offering a promising paradigm for CoT monitoring that can contribute to the oversight of frontier AI.
  </details>

- **[From Trajectories to Differentiated Skills: Cognitive Differentiation for Self-Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23599)**  `arXiv:2610.23599`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yewei Zhao, Derong Xu, Wei Chen, Yin Xu, Peilun Zhou, Xiaohan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents tackle complex tasks through reasoning and interaction with external environments. Agent skills provide persistent, transferable capabilities that guide task planning, inform contextual decisions, support tool use, and enable executable procedures. Yet existing skill-evolution methods largely organize knowledge around skill artifacts, overlooking the functional heterogeneity within individual skills and its implications for knowledge maintenance and use. Inspired by cognitive science, we propose Cognitive Differentiation in Skill Evolution (CDSE), which evolves a unified skill through four functionally distinct yet coordinated components: Meta, Experience, Tool, and Script. CDSE extracts evidence-grounded semantic gradients from successful and failed trajectories, consolidates them by functional role, and applies component-specific updates and validation with cross-component coordination and adaptive invocation. An analysis of 500 top-ranked skill packages shows that 96.8% contain knowledge spanning at least two functional roles. Across six benchmarks, three agent models, and two execution harnesses, CDSE achieves the highest scores in all 36 settings, improving over the Base agent by 7.6 percentage points and the strongest competing method in each setting by 2.6 points on average. Ablation studies further support the importance of functional differentiation and adaptive invocation.
  </details>

- **[RL-Guided Temporal Localization for Dual-Channel Retrieval in Long-Horizon Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23598)**  `arXiv:2610.23598`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dabin Sheng, Zhe Wu, Jinming Zhao, Junliang Xing, Yuanchun Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-aware retrieval is crucial for long-horizon LLM agents to effectively leverage ever-growing long-term memory. Existing methods focus on lexical or semantic similarity, leading to target memories being crowded out by temporally mismatched yet semantically similar candidates. Even time-aware approaches inject temporal cues only during memory organization or answer generation, overlooking temporal constraints at the retrieval stage, where candidate selection occurs - the root cause of the displacement. Inspired by the temporal contiguity effect in human episodic memory, we propose TIDER (Temporal Interval-driven Dual-channel Evidence Retrieval), a time-aware agentic retrieval framework. Central to TIDER is a reinforcement learning (RL)-trained temporal interval locator that infers the temporal windows in which the evidence lies. RL optimizes window localization as a discrete, non-differentiable retrieval decision, using composite rewards to convert sparse answer supervision into fine-grained retrieval feedback. These temporal windows first define the temporal retrieval channel; the retrieved candidates are then merged with results from the semantic channel and reranked by proximity to the windows. This temporal grounding enables TIDER to retrieve temporally aligned evidence that would otherwise be drowned out by the volume of semantically similar yet temporally mismatched memories. With a lightweight 1.7B locator, TIDER achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on both long-horizon memory benchmarks - 61.52% on LifeBench (+8.82%) and 78.96% on LoCoMo (+3.66%) - which indicates that temporal cues are crucial for pinpointing target evidence amid a vast pool of semantically similar historical events in long-term daily-life memory.
  </details>

- **[Darwin-Claw: Environment-Adaptive Experience Consolidation for Recursively Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23574)**  `arXiv:2610.23574`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mingze Yin, Xiaohan Wang, Dian Li, Longfei Luo, Jiahong Yan, Yiheng Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Large Language Model (LLM) agents have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across complex tasks, they predominantly operate in isolated sessions, lacking the ability to continually learn from historical interactions or adapt to user-specific environments. Existing self-evolution paradigms often indiscriminately inject raw interaction trajectories into memory or skill modules, leading to obscured behavioral patterns and compromised update controllability. Furthermore, as foundational LLM reasoning improves, the bottleneck for agentic evolution shifts from accumulating generic knowledge to effectively managing situational experience. To address this, we propose Darwin-Claw, an environment-adaptive self-evolving framework that systematically distills episodic experiences to drive the comprehensive evolution of operational skills and durable memory. Theoretically grounded in an iterative agent-actor and evolver-critic co-adaptation, Darwin-Claw introduces a Hierarchical Experience Consolidation System that extracts fine-grained error attributions and generalizes them into dynamically updated prototype vectors. This structured situational experience serves as interaction patches for context-sensitive perception and explicit optimization signals for skill refinement. Practically, Darwin-Claw is a readily deployable, automated framework compatible with mainstream agent systems without requiring manual curation. Extensive experiments on WildClawBench demonstrate that Darwin-Claw yields substantial performance improvements, establishing a controllable and stable pathway for the continual, user-centric adaptation of autonomous agents in real-world ecosystems.
  </details>

- **[SkillBrowser: Selective Skill Exposure for Scalable Skill-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23554)**  `arXiv:2610.23554`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jin Ye, Yifan Jiang, Lan-zhe Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents increasingly rely on skills to extend their capabilities in complex tasks. However, scalable skill use requires Selective Skill Exposure (SSE): providing relevant procedural content when needed while avoiding unnecessary exposure that may disrupt reasoning. Existing paradigms fall short in complementary ways. Progressive disclosure exposes metadata for a growing skill library, introducing context pollution; retrieval limits catalog exposure, but static retrieval from the initial task query may be misaligned with capability needs that emerge during reasoning and may still inject unnecessary skills. We propose SkillBrowser, a Meta-Skill-managed framework that enables an agent to identify current capability needs, formulate targeted queries, and selectively load skills on demand. Across six SRA-Bench subsets and five LLMs, SkillBrowser improves aggregate accuracy over static retrieval and LLM-based selection, including multi-step tool use, and mitigates accuracy degradation on already-solvable instances. Benefits remain model- and task-dependent, with under-invocation of useful skills and additional latency as key limitations. These results demonstrate the viability of Meta-Skill-managed SSE for scalable skill repositories.
  </details>

- **[Trapped in Your Story: Evaluating Narrative-Induced Sycophancy in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23525)**  `arXiv:2610.23525`  `cs.AI`  
  _Runtai Yang, Dandan Zhu, Kaiwei Zhang, Guangtao Zhai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are known to exhibit sycophancy by adjusting their responses toward users' expressed views. However, real users often justify their opinions through reasons, personal experiences, causal explanations, and broader narratives, raising the question of whether such narratives can further steer model judgments. We construct a controlled benchmark of 500 debatable questions across ten domains, where the user stance is held fixed while its justification is systematically varied without introducing new task-relevant evidence. Each question is instantiated under 11 matched conditions: one neutral baseline and five mirrored narrative levels for each of two opposing positions, yielding 5,500 evaluation instances. We evaluate seven LLMs and observe substantial differences in narrative-induced agreement. The effect is not monotonic with narrative elaboration: personal experience can reduce agreement, whereas causal framing and worldview-based synthesis increase it. Controlled GPT-5.1 ablations further show that causal framing produces the strongest positive effect, while claim-preserving bullet presentation also increases agreement over paragraph form. These findings suggest that sycophancy is shaped not only by user stance, but also by how supporting explanations are structured and presented.
  </details>

- **[GatewayBench: Unified Capability and Security Evaluation Platform for MCP Gateways](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23449)**  `arXiv:2610.23449`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yitian Zheng, Zhaorun Chen, Haoyu Li, Bo Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents have increasingly interacted with external environments by invoking tools, with Model Context Protocol (MCP) emerging as a standard interface for connecting agents to heterogeneous tools and services. As organizations deploy agents at scale, MCP gateways have become a critical middleware layer for aggregating, governing, and shaping the tool surface exposed to agents. Despite the growing importance of MCP gateways in agentic systems, no prior work has systematically studied how gateway design affects agent capability, efficiency, and security. To address this gap, we introduce GatewayBench, the first unified evaluation framework and benchmark for MCP gateways. We characterize gateway architectures into two major paradigms: flattened gateways and hierarchical gateways. GatewayBench comprises 524 tasks across six domains, together with a pool of 11,118 real-world tools from 251 authentic MCP servers. To emulate large-scale deployments, we systematically inject task-irrelevant tools sampled from this pool into each agent environment, enabling controlled evaluation as the accessible tool surface scales. Based on GatewayBench, we conduct extensive experiments across seven widely deployed MCP gateways and diverse agent configurations, covering both benign and adversarial tasks. Our results reveal substantial differences across gateway designs in task completion, tool-use efficiency, and resilience to attacks, and further demonstrate that these effects interact strongly with the underlying model and agent harness. These findings highlight the MCP gateway as a consequential yet underexplored component of the agentic AI stack. By enabling controlled evaluation of gateway architectures under realistic tool ecosystems, GatewayBench provides both practical guidance for MCP gateway design and a rigorous testbed for developing more capable, efficient, and secure agentic systems.
  </details>

- **[OliO: ODE-based Linear Transition Operator for Self-Supervised Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23345)**  `arXiv:2610.23345`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kwangryeol Park, Seulki Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised representation learning has advanced time series forecasting by capturing robust features from unlabeled data. However, existing contrastive and masking-based methods often struggle to explicitly model the underlying temporal flow, either by treating time lag as noise or by disrupting the inherent temporal dynamics essential for forecasting. To address these limitations, we propose OliO (**O**DE-based **Li**near Transition **O**perator), a plug-and-play self-supervised learning method that learns bidirectional temporal flows as a continuous and structurally consistent dynamical system. OliO introduces a transition operator derived from linear Ordinary Differential Equations (ODEs) to align latent representations across arbitrary time shifts. By imposing a strictly upper triangular constraint on the transition matrix, we mathematically ensure numerical stability and enforce a robust polynomial boundary that effectively captures long-term dependencies while preventing the exponential divergence typically found in unconstrained ODE-based models. This structural constraint induces a temporally coherent representation space that preserves the underlying flow essential for accurate forecasting. Extensive experiments across various backbone architectures and benchmarks demonstrate that OliO achieves significant performance gains, providing Mean Squared Error (MSE) reductions of up to 5.75% over the most competitive grid-searched SOTA baselines. The code is available at *this https URL
  </details>

- **[From Memory Hits to Answerable Evidence: Query-Conditioned Memory Compilation for Long-Term Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23313)**  `arXiv:2610.23313`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shangze Li, Yanyu Yao, Derong Xu, Zhi Zheng, Tong Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term agent memory requires recovering answer-relevant evidence dispersed across turns and sessions. Existing systems increasingly use structured memories and adaptive retrieval, yet often pass retrieved or expanded units directly to the reader, coupling memory addressing with evidence presentation and leaving the final context redundant or incomplete. To address this gap, we propose MemCompile, a query-conditioned evidence compilation framework for long-term conversational memory. Given a query, the Address Plane locates relevant support through compact source-linked descriptors and capacity-aware Cells, while a normalization boundary resolves heterogeneous retrieval hits to stable SourcePacket identities. Its Evidence Plane then allocates a bounded evidence budget across complementary packets, admits limited additional support, and renders the retained claims associated with each selected packet into an answer-ready context. MemCompile achieves 80.52% Judge Accuracy on LoCoMo and extends the empirical quality-cost Pareto frontier to a higher-quality regime. Its strong performance on LongMemEval-S further suggests that the benefits of query-conditioned compilation generalize across long-term conversational-memory benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[CoTA++: Understanding the Repeat Curse in dMLLMs from an Information Flow Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23290)**  `arXiv:2610.23290`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qiyan Zhao, Xiaofeng Zhang, Xu-Yao Zhang, Shijie Han, Zhepeng Li, Shunzhi Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion-based multimodal large language models (dMLLMs) decode by iterative denoising with bidirectional attention, which precludes the standard autoregressive key--value cache. Practical deployments therefore rely on approximate caching that reuses intermediate states across denoising steps. Such caching, however, often makes a response repeat one token at adjacent positions in a run that breaks the sentence, a failure we term the \emph{Repeat Curse}. Tracing the context-token information flow reveals three disruptions. Within a step, caching disperses the attention that anchors a position on nearby context tokens. Across steps, similarity-based refresh is not staleness-aware, so some positions are decoded against stale key--value states. Across layers, the deep-layer entropy of the context fails to converge where repetition arises. We accordingly propose CoTA++, a training-free, plug-and-play framework that counteracts each disruption where it originates: Context-Token Attention Re-anchoring re-forms the attention routing against the current context, Decode-Aware Refresh reserves part of the cache's refresh budget for the positions about to be committed, and Context-Token Entropy-Guided Voting discounts the confidence of a position by the deep-layer entropy of its context, which reorders the unmasking. The three components act at non-overlapping stages of a decoding step and compose freely with existing dMLLMs and caches. Across three dMLLMs and two caching backends, CoTA++ reduces the sample repetition rate of long responses from up to 99.6\% to at most 17.0\%, recovers most of the accuracy lost to caching on nine benchmarks, and keeps the speedup of caching at under 15\% overhead, making approximate caching reliable for dMLLMs.
  </details>

- **[Not All Test-Time Compute Is Evidence: Model-Specific Bias in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23213)**  `arXiv:2610.23213`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ming Shen, Tianjun Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time scaling methods implicitly assume that additional computation from the same model provides independent evidence. We show that this assumption fails within a single reasoning model: errors are correlated across repeated samples, across reasoning depth, and in the model's own self-evaluation, so more compute from the same model is not more evidence. We introduce DIA (Disagreement-triggered Independent Arbitration), which treats disagreement between an intermediate-stage and the final-stage prediction as a per-question signal of reasoning instability and resolves it by asking a small panel of heterogeneous models to solve the question independently, without seeing either candidate, and aggregating their answers directly. Across seven benchmarks and two base models, DIA outperforms full chain-of-thought in 12 of 14 settings, including a 7.00% gain on MuSiQue, while activating on only $\sim 17\%$ of examples and using up to 47.2% fewer tokens than a multi-agent baseline. Ablations show that both independent solving and solver heterogeneity are necessary for these gains.
  </details>

- **[FuzzInsight: Navigating the Efficiency-Effectiveness Trade-off in Coverage-Guided DNN Fuzzing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23195)**  `arXiv:2610.23195`  `cs.AI`  
  _Vivek Vekariya, Fathiyeh Faghih, Alexander Pretschner_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collecting and labeling diverse test datasets for evaluating Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) is costly and time-consuming. This has motivated coverage-guided fuzzing techniques that generate defect-revealing corner cases from limited test data. However, their effectiveness depends heavily on configuration choices, particularly coverage criteria and seed-selection strategies. We present a configuration-oriented analysis and decision guidelines for scalable DNN fuzzing targeting two bottlenecks: inefficient seed utilization and the cost of coverage guidance. Our analysis shows that existing seed-selection strategies repeatedly prioritize a small subset of inputs, leaving many collected seeds unexplored. To address this issue, we propose Equalized Sampling, a constraint-based strategy that increases mean seed queue utilization to 77%, improving on prior approaches by 13 percentage points.We further evaluate 84 fuzzing configurations on four benchmark datasets: MNIST, SVHN, GTSRB, and CIFAR-100. Results show that the non-structural criteria LSCD, LSC, and MDSC achieve coverage outcomes comparable to structural and alternative non-structural criteria while requiring substantially less computation. These criteria also promote broad behavioral exploration of the DNN under test, reducing the need to monitor multiple coverage metrics. Among them, LSCD provides a modest but consistent runtime advantage.Finally, we show that retraining with generated corner cases improves generalization robustness by up to 77 percentage points on average without degrading performance on the original test distribution. Together, these findings provide a practical blueprint for selecting fuzzing configurations that balance efficiency, behavioral exploration, and model hardening.
  </details>

- **[OfficeTown: Generative Organizations for Evolving Agent Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23194)**  `arXiv:2610.23194`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hongchen Wei, Daohong Ying, Zhi Hong, Aidan Wu, Bei Liu, Ruichun Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents increasingly use files and software tools to produce professional deliverables. Such work draws on materials, decisions, and feedback accumulated across assignments. We introduce OfficeTown, a generative simulation framework for professional organizations in which tasks and their context arise from continuing organizational work. From a natural-language description of an organization's purpose and activities, OfficeTown initializes an organization whose members coordinate work, delegate bounded assignments to assistant agents, and review their deliverables. Artifacts, decisions, and unresolved matters persist and shape subsequent work, while responsibilities and permissions determine what each member can see and do. We curate work requests, their context, and review feedback into executable resources for evaluation and learning. We construct OfficeTown Bench with 406 tasks and 4,836 evaluation criteria from 23 simulated organizations across eight professional domains, incorporating domain-expert feedback. Frontier-model evaluations reveal substantial remaining headroom. Supervised fine-tuning and on-policy distillation on OfficeTown data substantially improve Qwen3.5-9B on external professional-work benchmarks, including GDPval. Supplementary evaluations show that the principal--assistant harness achieves higher scores than the single- and multi-agent baselines tested. These results highlight the value of organizational simulation in generating work-grounded data and environments for the continued development of generalist agents.
  </details>

- **[LongForge: Measuring and Improving Long-Context Instruction Data via Evidence-Assertion Reasoning Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23167)**  `arXiv:2610.23167`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ziyang Chen, Junlong Jia, Xing Wu, Tinghao Yu, Songlin Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing quality control for long-context instruction-tuning data is a one-shot judgment inside the generation pipeline that leaves only a scalar, revealing neither which document parts an answer uses nor how many reasoning steps it writes down; the data can thus be neither audited nor controlled. We formalize data quality as a property of the alignment structure among document, question, and answer, and build LongForge, which pairs an analyzer that measures long-context data with a synthesizer that generates it. The analyzer reverse-reconstructs each sample into an evidence-assertion reasoning graph and programmatically computes four dimensions on it: breadth, depth, correctness, and completeness. Every reading traces back to specific evidence spans and reasoning steps, making data auditable. Measuring seven mainstream datasets reveals two previously unquantified defects: breadth barely grows with document length, and depth is generally low, the two being nearly orthogonal. The synthesizer then embeds the analyzer into generation, gating on correctness and completeness and deferring breadth and depth to a training-time mixture over measured values, which makes the training distribution controllable. On two backbones, its two mixtures, LongForge-1 and LongForge-N, outperform the strongest baseline by 2.2-4.7 points on average over six long-context benchmarks, and in a controlled comparison that holds the gate and the candidate pool fixed, raising breadth and depth alone is worth a further 1.16 points, making the structural distribution of training data a lever on downstream quality rather than only a description of it.
  </details>

- **[LexPilot: Benchmarking the Proactive Interaction Ability of Legal LLMs with Laypeople in Chinese Civil Case Consultation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23141)**  `arXiv:2610.23141`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wenbin Zhang, ShangZhan Li, Bingxiang He, Ye He, Yuxin Zhou, Muzhen Cai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have greatly assisted legal professionals. However, our empirical studies reveal that, when faced with underspecified consultations from non-experts, current LLMs often produce irresponsible responses with limited utility despite lacking sufficient case details for definitive conclusions. To better assist laypeople, legal LLMs must identify focal points of dispute and proactively elicit crucial context from users. Unfortunately, existing data resources often focus on interaction and response quality, offering limited insight into how proactive interaction affects LLMs' downstream claim-level judgment prediction in actual civil cases. To address this gap, we introduce \textbf{LexPilot}, a benchmark linking proactive legal interaction to claim-level verdict prediction using authentic Chinese civil judgments. Constructed from 432 real-world judgments across 36 common causes of action, LexPilot comprises expert-reviewed pairs of complete and underspecified consultation histories with corresponding court verdicts. Experiments reveal that current LLMs struggle with proactive legal interactions in underspecified consultations, with downstream judgment prediction substantially underperforming that in the fully specified consultation setup (-23.84% on average). Further analysis identifies critical bottlenecks hindering these capabilities. Our work highlights the urgent need for more user-centered legal AI to better serve the public.
  </details>

- **[WebCoder: Scaling General Frontend Development with Artifact-Grounded Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23104)**  `arXiv:2610.23104`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zijun Dou, Zhen Yang, Xijun Liu, Dongzhuoran Zhou, Aohan Zeng, Jie Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world frontend development requires agents to compose reproduction, generation, modification, and repair over an evolving application state. Learning a general policy for this process requires translating heterogeneous execution feedback into task-aligned rewards and effective credit assignment across long sequences of interdependent actions.
